\begin{document}

\title{\asgard: A Radius-Ordered GRB Afterglow Toolkit for Relativistic Dynamics, Particle Transport, and Observer-Time Projection}

\author[0000-0000-0000-0000]{Jia Ren}
\affiliation{Affiliation to be supplied}
\email{email to be supplied}

\begin{abstract}
Multiwavelength \grb\ afterglow fits can be degenerate when density structure, reverse shocks, inverse Compton cooling, hadronic losses, or secondary pairs contribute to the observed flux.  We present \asgard, a Python/Fortran toolkit that evolves the local blast-wave, particle, and radiation state on the shock-radius grid before projecting the emission to observer time.  The current release includes top-hat, Gaussian, and power-law jets; uniform, \(k=2\) wind, and tabulated media; implicit, characteristic, discontinuous-Galerkin, and selected \(\chi\)-resolved electron solvers; forward-shock synchrotron and synchrotron self-Compton emission; a bounded one-dimensional hadronic branch; reverse-shock diagnostics; and component-resolved observer products.  The central design choice is to keep \(\Gamma(R)\), \(B'(R)\), particle spectra, photon fields, optical depths, and component luminosities inspectable until they are assembled into \(F_\nu(t_{\rm obs})\).  This radius-local bookkeeping makes dynamical breaks, angular-projection effects, cooling transitions, component crossings, and photon attenuation traceable to their source terms.  The implementation is intentionally bounded: formal hadronic feedback is one-dimensional, \(\chi\)-resolved hadronic transport and IC-mediated electromagnetic cascades are not included, user-defined media do not enter the Fortran transport kernels, wind profiles with \(k\ne2\) are rejected, and jet spreading is outside the present calculation.
\end{abstract}

\keywords{Gamma-ray bursts (629) --- Computational methods (1965) --- Astronomy software (1855) --- Radiative processes (2055) --- Non-thermal radiation sources (1119)}

\section{Introduction} \label{sec:intro}

Gamma-ray burst afterglows are commonly interpreted as synchrotron radiation from nonthermal particles accelerated by a relativistic blast wave \citep{MeszarosRees1997,Sari1998,Granot2002}.  The inference problem is now broader than the simplest spherical, adiabatic afterglow.  Structured jets, off-axis viewing, reverse shocks, polarization, inverse Compton emission, density structure, and possible hadronic components are all used in current modeling \citep{SariPiran1999,Kobayashi2000,ZhangKobayashi2005,ChevalierLi2000,Murase2007,GillGranot2018Polarization,vanEerten2018BlastWaves,Salafia2022JetStructure}.  GRB 170817A required structured-jet and off-axis calculations and has continued to constrain late-time jet dynamics \citep{Troja2017GW170817Xray,Hallinan2017GW170817Radio,LambKobayashi2017Structured,Mooley2018WideAngle,Mooley2018Superluminal,Lazzati2018CollimatedJet,Margutti2018GW170817,Alexander2018GW170817,Ghirlanda2019StructuredJet,Troja2019GW170817,Hajela2022GW170817,Katira2025GW170817,Ryan2024}.  GRB 190114C, GRB 190829A, and GRB 221009A made high-energy afterglow components and narrow structured-jet interpretations part of routine model comparison \citep{MAGIC2019TeV,MAGIC2019IC,HESS2019VHE,HESS2021GRB190829A,LHAASO2023TeVAfterglow,LHAASO2023Beyond10TeV,Laskar2023GRB221009A,OConnor2023GRB221009A}.

This paper has a narrower purpose than a review of those data.  It documents the model implemented in the present \asgard\ release and asks how a coupled afterglow calculation can remain physically inspectable.  For calculations in which intermediate states matter, \asgard\ provides a radius-ordered record of the dynamics, electrons, photon fields, optional hadrons, optional reverse shocks, and observer-time projection.  Claims outside that state sequence are treated as unsupported.

Several published codes define the context.  \afterglowpy\ provides efficient synchrotron flux calculations for structured jets and off-axis geometries \citep{Ryan2020}.  \jetsimpy\ emphasizes hydrodynamically motivated thin-shell evolution and lateral structure \citep{Wang2024Jetsimpy}.  \vegas\ gives a recent code-paper reference for jets, media, dynamics, radiation, observer geometry, examples, performance, and reverse-shock jump conditions \citep{Wang2025VegasAfterglow}.  \pyblast\ is a modular multiphysics blast-wave framework for transient counterparts \citep{Nedora2025PyBlastAfterglow}.  \asgard\ is complementary to these tools: it is organized around inspectable local state and component accounting, not around replacing a faster light-curve model.

The manuscript follows the calculation order used by modern afterglow code papers, while keeping the radius-local state variables explicit.  Section~\ref{sec:contract} defines the public input model and the state written by each module.  Section~\ref{sec:dynamics} gives the jet, medium, energy-injection, and dynamics layer.  Sections~\ref{sec:electron}--\ref{sec:reverse-shock} give the radiation and feedback layers: electron transport, photon fields, hadronic transport, and reverse-shock branches.  Section~\ref{sec:projection} gives the geometry and equal-arrival-time projection.  Section~\ref{sec:fitting} records the fitting and runtime interface.  Section~\ref{sec:verification} uses figure-backed state diagnostics and benchmark evidence.  Section~\ref{sec:limits} states unsupported combinations.

\section{Model Definition and State Variables} \label{sec:contract}

\asgard\ treats an afterglow calculation as a radial history of a shocked shell.  The public input specifies the jet, external density, observer, microphysics, frequency and time grids, and the optional hadronic or reverse-shock switches.  The code first turns these inputs into one runtime model and then advances the shock from small to large radius.  Observer products are not evolved as independent variables.  They are assembled only after the local emitting and absorbing material has been computed.

The radius ordering is physical, not only computational.  At a given shock radius \(R\), the dynamics determine the external density \(n(R)\), Lorentz factor \(\Gamma(R)\), swept-up mass \(M_{\rm sw}(R)\), internal energy \(U(R)\), and comoving magnetic field \(B'(R)\).  These quantities define the shell in which electrons are injected and cooled.  The same shell also supplies the synchrotron photon field, self-absorption optical depth, inverse-Compton targets, and, when requested, the \(p\gamma\), Bethe-Heitler, \(pp\), and \(\gamma\gamma\) targets.  Only after these local quantities are known does the observer module apply Doppler factors, equal-arrival-time geometry, distance factors, and attenuation.

This order is required because the observer light curve is many-to-one with respect to the local physics.  A break in \(F_\nu(t_{\rm obs})\) may be produced by blast-wave deceleration, angular structure, a cooling transition, a component crossing, a reverse shock, or local photon absorption.  If the calculation stores only the final flux, those possibilities cannot be separated.  The radius-ordered state instead keeps the quantities that generated the flux visible before they are mixed by projection \citep{Granot2002,vanEerten2010,vanEerten2012,vanEerten2018BlastWaves,Salafia2022JetStructure}.

The resulting model contract is simple.  Public inputs define a runtime configuration.  The dynamics module writes the shell state.  The electron and photon modules write the lepton distribution, synchrotron/SSC source terms, and photon-survival factors.  Optional hadronic and reverse-shock modules append their own state only when their switches are active.  The observer module then projects the assembled local emissivity and attenuation to \(t_{\rm obs}\).  If a supported electron-photon or hadronic feedback loop is active, the coupled transport is iterated before projection; the projection step itself does not repair, smooth, or reinterpret the local state.

For one active radial cell, the state record can be summarized as
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{S}(R)=
\left\{
\begin{array}{l}
\Gamma,M_{\rm sw},U,B',
N_e,n_\gamma,N_p,N_\pi,N_\mu,
\\
Q_e^{\rm sec},\tau_{\gamma\gamma},L_\nu^{\rm comp}
\end{array}
\right\}.
\end{equation}
This notation is a bookkeeping map, not a claim that every run contains every field.  A one-dimensional leptonic calculation writes the forward-shock dynamics, electron spectrum, photon field, synchrotron and SSC components, and absorption factors.  A formal hadronic calculation additionally writes the proton, pion, muon, neutrino, photon-loss, and secondary-pair fields.  A reverse-shock calculation writes a separate region-3 state before the same observer-time projection is applied.  Unsupported combinations are therefore absent from the state record rather than inferred from partial fields.

\begin{figure*}[!tp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig1_radius_state.pdf}
  \caption{Radius-ordered state contract for an \asgard\ calculation.  (a) Public inputs are normalized into a fixed sequence: runtime configuration, dynamics, electron and photon state, bounded optional branches, and observer projection.  (b) The blast-wave Lorentz factor and comoving magnetic field are stored on the shock-radius grid before observer products are formed.  (c) The equal-arrival-time mapping uses \(t_{\rm obs}=(1+z)[t_{\rm on}(R)+R(1-\mu)/c]\), so different angular cells map the same local radius sequence to different observer-time sequences.}
  \label{fig:radius-state}
\end{figure*}

The supported observer products are derived from this state record: multi-frequency
fluxes, fixed-time spectra, band-integrated fluxes, exposure-averaged fluxes,
component-resolved fluxes, and diagnostic state tracks.  The component record
contains the total flux and the active physical channels: forward-shock
synchrotron and SSC emission, reverse-shock synchrotron and SSC emission,
cross-zone inverse Compton emission, hadronic synchrotron emission, pair
synchrotron emission, and neutrino outputs.  The diagnostic state exposes the
radius, Lorentz factor, magnetic field, electron spectra, \(\chi\)-resolved
electron fields, hadronic species, photon-survival factors, reverse-shock state,
and secondary reverse-shock event diagnostics when those calculations are active.

\begin{figure*}[!tp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig2_forward_api.pdf}
  \caption{From dynamics to observer products in one forward-shock calculation.  (a) The local \(\Gamma(R)\) and \(B'(R)\) state supplies the source terms for the observer calculation.  (b) Multi-frequency light curves, (c) fixed-time spectra, and (d) the SSC flux fraction are assembled from the same radius-ordered state.  The figure is an accounting diagnostic, not an independent fit to a particular burst.}
  \label{fig:forward-api}
\end{figure*}

The public model definition is intentionally bounded.  \asgard\ is not presented
as a replacement for faster light-curve-only tools.  Its purpose in this paper is
to make a coupled afterglow calculation inspectable by preserving the state that
precedes observer projection.  The supported runtime families are listed in
Section~\ref{sec:limits}; physical combinations outside those families are not
interpreted as modeled behavior.

\section{Dynamics, External Media, and Jet Initial Conditions} \label{sec:dynamics}

This section defines the quantities that set the blast-wave motion.  The shock
needs three inputs before any electrons or photons are computed: the external
density \(n(R)\), the initial angular energy profile, and the initial angular
Lorentz-factor profile.  The dynamics then turn these inputs into
\(\Gamma(R)\), swept-up matter, and internal energy.

\subsection{External media}

The external medium is the gas swept up by the forward shock.  In the dynamics it
enters only through the proton number density \(n(R)\).  \asgard\ supplies this
density in three ways: a uniform medium, a Chevalier--Li \(k=2\) stellar wind, or
a tabulated radial profile.  Once \(n(R)\) is known, the same value is used by
the particle-injection, photon-field, and observer modules at that radius.

\subsubsection{Analytic media}

The two analytic media give \(n(R)\) directly.  A uniform medium has a constant
density.  A wind medium falls as \(R^{-2}\).  The code writes these choices as
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
n(R) &= n_{\rm ISM},\\
n_{\rm w}(R) &= \frac{A_\ast A_0}{m_pR^2},\\
n_0(R) &=
\begin{cases}
n_{\rm ISM}, & n_{\rm w}(R)\le n_{\rm ISM}/\eta_{\rm w},\\
n_{\rm w}(R), & n_{\rm w}(R)> n_{\rm ISM}/\eta_{\rm w},
\end{cases}\\
R_0 &= \left(\frac{A_\ast A_0}{m_p n_{\rm cap}}\right)^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(n_{\rm ISM}\) is the uniform density, \(A_0\) is the Chevalier--Li wind
mass-loading scale, \(m_p\) is the proton mass, and \(\eta_{\rm w}\) sets the
wind-to-ISM switch \citep{ChevalierLi2000}.  The optional cap is also simple.  A
user gives a cap density \(n_{\rm cap}\); the dynamics use the matching radius
\(R_0\).  Inside \(R_0\), the wind density is held at \(n_{\rm w}(R_0)\), while a
uniform medium remains at \(n_{\rm ISM}\).  This cap is a hard inner state.  It is
not a smoothed termination shock.  A wind profile with \(k\ne2\) is not part of
the implemented model.

\subsubsection{Tabulated media and jumps}

A tabulated medium lets the density come from user-supplied points.  The table
contains positive radii \(R_i\) and positive densities \(n_i\), with the radii in
strictly increasing order and \(N_\rho\le N_{\rho,\max}\).  Between two adjacent
points, the density is a power law in \(R\):
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
n(R) &=
\exp\left[
\operatorname{interp}\left(\ln R;\,\ln R_i,\,\ln n_i\right)
\right],\\
n(R) &=
n_i
\left(\frac{R}{R_i}\right)^{q_i},\qquad
q_i\equiv\frac{\ln(n_{i+1}/n_i)}{\ln(R_{i+1}/R_i)} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The first two table points set the density below the table.  The last two points
set the density above the table.  The code therefore extends the table as a
power law; it does not hold the end values fixed.  When a tabulated medium is
used, the Gaussian jump model below is not applied.

Density jumps are local changes on top of an analytic baseline density.  They
are used only when the caller asks for the jump-bearing density.  For several
jumps, the density is
\begin{equation}
n(R)=n_0(R)
\left[
1+\sum_j
(f_j-1)
\exp\left(
-\frac{(R-R_j)^2}{2(\Delta R_j)^2}
\right)
\right].
\end{equation}
Here \(R_j\) is the jump radius, \(f_j\) is the density factor, and
\(\Delta R_j\equiv w_jR_j\) is the width.  The older single-jump interface is the
same expression with one Gaussian.  The jump radii, factors, and widths must be
positive and must have matching lengths.  If \(f_j<1\), the term represents a
density dip rather than a density bump.  Such dips are allowed only while the
total bracket stays positive.  These Gaussian structures are a useful way to
study density changes in afterglows, but this paper does not claim that each
Gaussian corresponds to a unique astrophysical environment
\citep{DaiLu2002,NakarGranot2007,UhmZhang2014}.

The medium setup also defines the allowed input family.  It supports
\(N_J\le N_{J,\max}\) Gaussian structures and
\(N_\rho\le N_{\rho,\max}\) tabulated points.  It rejects nonpositive jump
radii, factors, or widths; nonpositive table radii or densities; tables with
fewer than two points; and tables whose radii are not ordered.  These checks are
input boundaries, not extra physical assumptions.

\subsection{Jet angular profiles}

The jet profile tells each polar element how much energy and initial Lorentz
factor it has.  It does not evolve the angular structure.  A top-hat jet is
uniform inside \(\theta_j\) and empty outside it:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
E_{\rm iso}(\theta) &=
\begin{cases}
E_c, & \theta<\theta_j,\\
0, & \theta\ge\theta_j,
\end{cases}\\
\Gamma_0(\theta) &=
\begin{cases}
\Gamma_c, & \theta<\theta_j,\\
1, & \theta\ge\theta_j .
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
In a Gaussian jet, both the isotropic-equivalent energy and \(\Gamma_0-1\)
decrease smoothly away from the axis:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
E_{\rm iso}(\theta) &=
E_c\exp\left[-\frac{\theta^2}{2\theta_c^2}\right],\\
\Gamma_0(\theta) &=
1+(\Gamma_c-1)\exp\left[-\frac{\theta^2}{2\theta_c^2}\right],
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The Gaussian profile is used only inside the declared outer support angle
\(\theta_{\max}\).  A power-law jet has a flat core and power-law wings:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
E_{\rm iso}(\theta) &=
E_c
\begin{cases}
1, & \theta\le \theta_c,\\
(\theta/\theta_c)^{-k_E}, & \theta>\theta_c,
\end{cases}\\
\Gamma_0(\theta) &=
\begin{cases}
\Gamma_c, & \theta\le \theta_c,\\
1+(\Gamma_c-1)(\theta/\theta_c)^{-k_\Gamma}, & \theta>\theta_c .
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(E_c\) and \(\Gamma_c\) are the on-axis values, \(\theta_c\) is the core
angle, and \(k_E\) and \(k_\Gamma\) set the wing slopes.  The public profile uses
\(k_\Gamma=k_E\) unless the Lorentz-factor slope is given separately.  These
profiles are initial conditions for independent angular elements.  The present
backend does not evolve lateral spreading.

\subsection{Forward-shock dynamical variables}

The forward shock is described by four evolving quantities.  The state vector is
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{y}=(\Gamma,\mathcal{M},U,R),
\end{equation}
and it is advanced in the source-frame on-axis arrival-time coordinate
\(t_{\rm on}\).
Here \(\Gamma\) is the blast-wave Lorentz factor, \(U\) is the internal energy,
and \(R\) is the shock radius.  The second entry \(\mathcal{M}\) depends on the
dynamics branch: branches 1 and 2 store a swept number column, while branch 3
stores the swept mass.

The coordinate \(t_{\rm on}\) is not the laboratory time.  It is the arrival-time
coordinate for a photon emitted on the line of sight.  Along the radial sequence,
\begin{equation}
\frac{dt_{\rm on}}{dR}=\frac{1-\beta}{\beta c},
\qquad
\frac{dR}{dt_{\rm on}}=\frac{\beta c}{1-\beta},
\qquad
\beta=\sqrt{1-\Gamma^{-2}}.
\end{equation}
The second derivative is therefore not the shock speed in the lab frame.  It is
the speed at which the code moves along the radius grid when the independent
variable is \(t_{\rm on}\).  Off-axis observer times are assembled later from
\(t_{\rm obs}=(1+z)[t_{\rm on}(R)+R(1-\mu)/c]\).

The magnetic field is set by the local post-shock energy density.  For a strong
relativistic forward shock, the comoving energy density used here is
\begin{equation}
e'\simeq4(\Gamma^2-1)n(R)m_pc^2,
\end{equation}
where \(n(R)\) is the upstream proton density.  The comoving magnetic field is
then
\begin{equation}
B'=\left(8\pi\epsilon_B e'\right)^{1/2}
=\left[32\pi\epsilon_B n(R)m_pc^2(\Gamma^2-1)\right]^{1/2}.
\end{equation}
This equation says that a fraction \(\epsilon_B\) of the shocked internal energy
is placed in magnetic field.  It also fixes the numerical cgs coefficient used by
the code; that coefficient is not a fit parameter.

The dynamics branch also records a simple synchrotron cooling scale.  Using the
comoving elapsed time \(t'_{\rm dyn}\equiv\Gamma t_{\rm on}\), the cooling
Lorentz factor is
\begin{equation}
\gamma_c=
\frac{6\pi m_ec}{\sigma_T B'^2t'_{\rm dyn}}.
\end{equation}
The lowest Lorentz factor of the injected nonthermal electrons is
\begin{equation}
\gamma_m=
1+\frac{\epsilon_e}{f_e}
\frac{m_p}{m_e}
\frac{p-2}{p-1}
(\Gamma-1).
\end{equation}
Here \(\epsilon_e\) is the electron energy fraction, \(f_e\) is the fraction of
electrons that are accelerated, and \(p\) is the injected electron index.  If the
electrons are in the slow-cooling ordering \(\gamma_c>\gamma_m\) and \(p\ge2\),
the dynamics branch uses
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_{\rm eff}=\epsilon_e\left(\frac{\gamma_m}{\gamma_c}\right)^{p-2};
\end{equation}
otherwise it uses \(\epsilon_{\rm eff}=\epsilon_e\).  This quantity only controls
the radiative-efficiency estimate in the dynamics.  It does not replace the
later electron-transport calculation, which evolves \(N_e\) explicitly.

The first radial grid needs an estimate of where the blast wave starts to slow
down.  The code first defines the energy-to-mass scale
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{D}_0=
\frac{E_{\rm iso}}{4\pi(\Gamma_0-1)m_pc^2},
\end{equation}
where \(E_{\rm iso}\) and \(\Gamma_0\) are the initial isotropic-equivalent
energy and Lorentz factor of the angular element.  The two analytic estimates
for the deceleration radius are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
R_{\rm dec,ISM}
&=\left(\frac{n_{\rm ISM}\Gamma_0}{\mathcal{D}_0}\right)^{-1/3},\\
R_{\rm dec,wind}
&=\frac{3\mathcal{D}_0}{2(A_\ast A_0/m_p)\Gamma_0}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(A_\ast A_0/m_p\) is the wind number-density normalization.  For wind
media the setup uses \(R_{\rm dec}=\min(R_{\rm dec,ISM},R_{\rm dec,wind})\);
otherwise it uses \(R_{\rm dec}=R_{\rm dec,ISM}\).  The corresponding on-axis
arrival scale is
\[
t_{\rm dec}=\frac{R_{\rm dec}(1-\beta_0)}{\beta_0c},
\qquad
\beta_0=(1-\Gamma_0^{-2})^{1/2}.
\]
For \(\Gamma_0\gg1\), this reduces to
\(t_{\rm dec}\simeq R_{\rm dec}/(2\Gamma_0^2c)\).  The solver samples the
coasting-to-decelerating transition; it does not impose a broken power law.

\subsection{Dynamical branches}

The dynamics backend has three branches.  Branch 3 is the default.  Branch 1 is
the semi-analytic radiative-efficiency form often used for afterglow dynamics
\citep{BlandfordMcKee1976,HuangDaiLu1999}.  Branches 2 and 3 use a variable
effective adiabatic index.  Below we call the three choices the
radiative-efficiency branch, the variable-index legacy branch, and the
mass-energy baseline.

\subsubsection{Shared equation of state and injection source}

Branches 2 and 3 need an adiabatic index that changes with shock speed.  A hot
relativistic gas and a cooler Newtonian gas do not use the same
\(\hat{\gamma}\).  \asgard\ therefore evaluates a fixed equation-of-state fit:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
u&=\Gamma\beta,\qquad
\Theta=\frac{u}{3}\frac{u+a_\Theta u^2}{1+u+a_\Theta u^2},\\
\zeta&=\frac{\Theta}{a_\zeta+\Theta},\qquad
\hat{\gamma}=
\frac{1}{3}\sum_{r=0}^{N_{\hat\gamma}}a_r\zeta^r .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(u\) is the dimensionless four-velocity, \(\Theta\) is the temperature-like
fit variable, and \(\zeta\) maps that variable to the polynomial domain.  The
coefficients \((a_\Theta,a_\zeta,\{a_r\})\) and the order \(N_{\hat\gamma}\) are
fixed by the relativistic Maxwell--Juttner equation-of-state fit
\citep{Service1986,Peer2012}.  They are not fitted to the afterglow examples.

The optional energy-injection source adds luminosity before the observer
projection.  In source-frame time it is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{L}(t_{\rm on})=
\begin{cases}
L_0(t_{\rm on}/t_1)^{q}, & t_1\le t_{\rm on}\le t_2,\\
0, & \text{otherwise},
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
where \(t_1\) and \(t_2\) are source-frame injection bounds, \(L_0\) is the
injected luminosity normalization, and \(q\) is the temporal index.  The public
energy-injection constructor stores these parameters in observer-frame form.  The
dynamics divide the time bounds by \(1+z\) before using them in the source-frame
equation.  This source is a prescribed luminosity term.  It is not a separate
spin-down calculation.

Branches 1 and 3 include \(\mathcal{L}(t_{\rm on})\) in the Lorentz-factor
equation.  Branch 2 does not.  Energy injection is therefore part of the chosen
dynamics branch.  It is not added after the light curve is computed.

\subsubsection{Swept-medium state variables}

The branches use two different swept-medium variables.  Branches 1 and 2 evolve
a swept number column \(\mu\).  Branch 3 evolves the physical swept mass \(M\).
They obey
\begin{equation}
q_\mu\equiv\frac{d\mu}{dt_{\rm on}}
=n(R)R^2\frac{dR}{dt_{\rm on}},
\qquad
\frac{dM}{dR}=4\pi n(R)R^2m_p .
\end{equation}
The corresponding initial ejecta normalizations are
\begin{equation}
\mu_0=\frac{E_{\rm iso}}{4\pi(\Gamma_0-1)m_pc^2},
\qquad
M_0=\frac{E_{\rm iso}}{(\Gamma_0-1)c^2}.
\end{equation}
For branches 1 and 2, the total swept mass reported to the user is
\(M_{\rm sw}=4\pi m_p\mu\).  In branch 3, \(M\) is already the swept mass in
grams, and
\[
\frac{dM}{dt_{\rm on}}=\frac{dM}{dR}\frac{dR}{dt_{\rm on}}.
\]

\subsubsection{Branch 1: radiative-efficiency dynamics}

Branch 1 slows down because it sweeps up external matter.  It can also gain
energy from the prescribed luminosity source.  Its equations are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d\Gamma}{dt_{\rm on}}=
\frac{-(\Gamma^2-1)q_\mu
+\mathcal{L}(t_{\rm on})/(4\pi m_pc^2)}
{\mu_0+\epsilon_{\rm eff}\mu
+2(1-\epsilon_{\rm eff})\Gamma\mu},
\\
\frac{dU}{dt_{\rm on}}=(\Gamma-1)q_\mu .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The negative term in the numerator is the deceleration from swept-up matter.  The
positive term is the optional injected luminosity.  The internal energy \(U\)
uses the same swept column \(\mu\).

\subsubsection{Branch 2: variable-index legacy dynamics}

Branch 2 uses the same swept column \(\mu\), but it changes the inertia with
\(\hat{\gamma}\).  Define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{N}_2&=
\hat{\gamma}(\Gamma^2-1)-(\hat{\gamma}-1)\Gamma\beta^2,
\\
\mathcal{D}_2&=\mu_0+\epsilon_{\rm eff}\mu\\
&\quad
+(1-\epsilon_{\rm eff})\mu
\left[
2\hat{\gamma}\Gamma-(\hat{\gamma}-1)(1+\Gamma^{-2})
\right],
\\
\frac{d\Gamma}{dt_{\rm on}}&=-
\frac{\mathcal{N}_2}{\mathcal{D}_2}
q_\mu,\\
\frac{dU}{dt_{\rm on}}&=(\Gamma-1)q_\mu .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Branch 2 is kept as a selectable legacy option.  It is useful for comparing
dynamics choices, but it is not the default branch for the publication figures.

\subsubsection{Branch 3: mass-energy baseline}

The default branch uses the physical swept mass \(M\).  This makes the mass
budget explicit.  Define
\begin{equation}
X=\Gamma^2-1,
\qquad
\frac{dM}{dt_{\rm on}}=\frac{dM}{dR}\frac{dR}{dt_{\rm on}},
\end{equation}
where \(X\) is a short name for the relativistic kinetic factor.  The Lorentz
factor equation is written with a numerator and denominator:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{N}_3&=
-\frac{dM}{dR}c^2\Gamma X
(\hat{\gamma}\Gamma-\hat{\gamma}+1)
\\
&\quad
-(\hat{\gamma}X+1)\Gamma(1-\hat{\gamma})
\frac{3U}{R}
+\Gamma^2\mathcal{L}(t_{\rm on})
\left(\frac{dR}{dt_{\rm on}}\right)^{-1},
\\
\mathcal{D}_3&=
\Gamma^2(M_0+M)c^2
+(\hat{\gamma}^2X+3\hat{\gamma}-2)U .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\Gamma}{dt_{\rm on}}=
\frac{\mathcal{N}_3}{\mathcal{D}_3}\frac{dR}{dt_{\rm on}}.
\end{equation}
The three terms in \(\mathcal{N}_3\) have direct meanings.  The first term is the
inertia added by newly swept-up mass.  The second term is the work done by the
expanding shocked gas.  The third term is the optional injected luminosity.

The internal energy changes by shock heating, adiabatic expansion, and optional
energy injection.  The radial expansion and heating terms are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{E}_R=
\frac{3}{R}
&-\frac{1}{\Gamma}
\left(\frac{dR}{dt_{\rm on}}\right)^{-1}
\frac{d\Gamma}{dt_{\rm on}},\\
\mathcal{H}_R
&=(1-\epsilon_{\rm eff})(\Gamma-1)
\frac{dM}{dR}c^2 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The energy equation is
\begin{equation}
\frac{dU}{dt_{\rm on}}=
\left[\mathcal{H}_R-(\hat{\gamma}-1)\mathcal{E}_R U\right]
\frac{dR}{dt_{\rm on}}
+\mathcal{L}(t_{\rm on}).
\end{equation}
This branch is the dynamics baseline for the default forward-shock calculations.

\subsection{Forward-dynamics integration}

\subsubsection{Runtime container and state vector}

The forward-dynamics kernel reads the public model once at entry.  It then works
with physical scalars:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{B}\mapsto
\begin{gathered}
\left(
\Gamma_0,\epsilon_e,\epsilon_B,p,z,n_{\rm ISM},A_\ast,E_{\rm iso},
\log_{10}t_{{\rm on},\rm end},f_e
\right),\\
\left(
t_1,t_2,L_0,q,R_{\rm tr},f_{\rm jump},w_{\rm jump},R_0
\right).
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
This container is an interface object, not a separate physical model.  The
symbol \(\Gamma_0\) is the initial Lorentz factor.  The kernel then fixes the
density profile and integrates
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{y}=(\Gamma,Y_2,U,R)
\end{equation}
from the initial state
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{y}_0=(\Gamma_0-\Delta\Gamma_{\rm init},
Y_{2,{\rm init}},U_{\rm init},R_{\rm init}),
\end{equation}
where \(\Delta\Gamma_{\rm init}\), \(Y_{2,{\rm init}}\), \(U_{\rm init}\), and
\(R_{\rm init}\) are fixed integration-start parameters.  The entry \(Y_2\)
means \(\mu\) for branches 1 and 2, and \(M\) for branch 3.

\subsubsection{Output grid and stored state}

The output grid is built in the same on-axis time coordinate.  The first positive
time is
\begin{equation}
t_{{\rm on},\min}=\min(t_{\rm floor},\eta_{\rm dec}t_{\rm dec}) .
\end{equation}
Here \(t_{\rm floor}\) and \(\eta_{\rm dec}\) are fixed integration-grid
parameters.  The output grid is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\ell_i&=\log_{10}t_{{\rm on},\min}
+\frac{\log_{10}t_{{\rm on},\rm end}
-\log_{10}t_{{\rm on},\min}}{N_R-1}(i-1),\\
t_{{\rm on},i}&=10^{\ell_i}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and the time-ordered update is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
H_i&=t_{{\rm on},i}-t_{{\rm on},i-1},\qquad t_{{\rm on},0}=0,\\
\mathbf{y}_i&=\mathbf{y}_{i-1}
+\int_{t_{{\rm on},i-1}}^{t_{{\rm on},i}}
F(t_{\rm on},\mathbf{y})\,dt_{\rm on} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(F\equiv d\mathbf{y}/dt_{\rm on}\) is the selected branch equation.  After
each accepted step, the state is stored as
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{y}_i=(Y_{\Gamma,i},Y_{2,i},Y_{U,i},Y_{R,i}).
\end{equation}
The public arrays are then
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
t_{{\rm obs},i}&=(1+z)t_{{\rm on},i},\\
\Gamma_i&=Y_{\Gamma,i},\qquad R_i=Y_{R,i},\\
M_{{\rm sw},i}&=
\begin{cases}
4\pi m_pY_{2,i},
& \text{branches 1 and 2},\\
Y_{2,i},
& \text{branch 3}.
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Runge--Kutta advance}

For \(t_{{\rm on},i-1}>0\), the integrator advances each output interval in
logarithmic time.  It changes variables from \(t_{\rm on}\) to
\begin{equation}
s=\ln(t_{\rm on}/t_{{\rm on},i-1}),
\qquad
\frac{d\mathbf{y}}{ds}
=t_{\rm on}\frac{d\mathbf{y}}{dt_{\rm on}}
=t_{\rm on}F(t_{\rm on},\mathbf{y}),
\end{equation}
and applies the classical fourth-order Runge--Kutta tableau over
\begin{equation}
0\le s\le \ln(t_{{\rm on},i}/t_{{\rm on},i-1}),
\qquad
|\Delta s|\le\Delta s_{\max}.
\end{equation}
Here \(\Delta s_{\max}\) is the fixed logarithmic-step cap used by the dynamics
kernel.  It is a numerical integration parameter, not a blast-wave scale.  For
the first interval, where \(t_{{\rm on},i-1}=0\), the same RK4 tableau is applied
directly in linear \(t_{\rm on}\).

\subsubsection{Adaptive refinement and state domain}

The adaptive check compares two integrations of the same interval.  Let
\(\mathbf{y}^{\rm f}\) be the current \(m\)-substep trial and
\(\mathbf{y}^{\rm c}\) the previous \(m/2\)-substep comparison solution.  The
relative error over positive state entries is
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_{\rm RK}^\ast=
\max_{a\in\mathcal{I}_y}
\frac{2|y_a^{\rm f}-y_a^{\rm c}|}
{y_a^{\rm f}+y_a^{\rm c}},
\qquad
\mathcal{I}_y\equiv\{a\,|\,y_a^{\rm f}+y_a^{\rm c}>0\}.
\end{equation}
The subdivision is refined until \(\epsilon_{\rm RK}^\ast<\epsilon_{\rm RK}\).
Each trial must also keep \(\Gamma>1\).  The tolerance \(\epsilon_{\rm RK}\) is
the fixed RK accuracy threshold.  These checks define the relativistic
blast-wave state domain used by the integrator.  They do not smooth or repair the
solution after the fact.

\subsection{Python binding boundary}

The Python dynamics namespace is only a binding layer.  It exposes the compiled
forward and reverse dynamics kernels to higher-level orchestration.  It does not
add a blast-wave equation, a shock jump condition, or an integration step.  The
physical equations are the compiled forward dynamics described above and the
reverse-shock equations in Section~\ref{sec:reverse-shock}.

\section{Electron Transport and Synchrotron Emission} \label{sec:electron}

The electron layer follows the particles that radiate the afterglow.  At each
radius, the shock injects nonthermal electrons.  These electrons then cool by
synchrotron, inverse Compton, and adiabatic losses.  The one-dimensional solvers
return \(N_e(\gamma,R)\), synchrotron luminosity, seed-photon density, and the
break frequencies \(\nu_m\), \(\nu_c\), and \(\nu_a\).  The two-dimensional path
adds downstream \(\chi\)-resolved electron and photon fields.  The solver choice
changes the discretization, not the physical injection law
\citep{LeVeque2002,JiangShu1996,HesthavenWarburton2008}.

\subsection{Shared electron state, sources, and coordinates}

\subsubsection{Namespace boundary}

The Python electron namespace is only a binding layer.  It selects one compiled
electron module and exposes it to the public API.  It does not add an injection
law, a cooling term, an emissivity formula, or a transport equation.  Unsupported
historical names are absent rather than redirected to another solver.

\subsubsection{Continuum state and solver storage}

All forward electron solvers start from the same continuity equation.  The
independent variable is radius:
\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial N_e}{\partial R}
+\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}
\left(g_RN_e\right)
=Q_e+Q_e^{\rm sec},
\end{equation}
where \(N_e=dN_e/d\gamma\), \(g_R=d\gamma/dR\), and \(Q_e\) is the primary shock
source.  The term \(g_R\) contains radiative and adiabatic energy drift.  The
secondary source \(Q_e^{\rm sec}\) is zero in a separated leptonic calculation.
It is supplied only when the hadronic-feedback branch feeds Bethe-Heitler,
\(pp\), or \(\gamma\gamma\) secondaries back into the electron equation.

The default implicit solver uses the log-gamma density
\begin{equation}
N_x\equiv\frac{dN_e}{dx}=\gamma\ln 10\,N_e,
\qquad x=\log_{10}\gamma .
\end{equation}
This is the physical density per \(x\)-bin.  Some kernels store the same
particles in a log-four-velocity coordinate.  The Jacobian is applied when the
initial condition and sources are projected onto that storage grid.  The implicit
shell update has the form
\begin{equation}
\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{A}_i\right)\mathbf{N}_{i+1}
=\mathbf{N}_{i}+\Delta R_i\,\mathbf{Q}_{i},
\end{equation}
where the upper-bidiagonal matrix \(\mathbf{A}_i\) is assembled from face drift
coefficients.  A high-to-low-energy sweep solves the matrix.  The source term is
integrated over cell edges, so the update evolves cell averages, not point
samples.

\subsubsection{Finite-shell source and injection scales}

Electron injection uses the same density \(n(R)\) as the dynamics.  No separate
electron-density law is introduced.  The shell volume is
\begin{equation}
V(R)=\frac{4\pi R^3}{3}.
\end{equation}
The number of swept electrons before the first active radius is
\begin{equation}
N_{\rm init}=\int_0^{R_{\rm ini}}4\pi R^2 n(R)\,dR .
\end{equation}
Thus \(N_{\rm init}=n_{\rm ISM}V(R_{\rm ini})\) for the uniform branch.  For
analytic wind and capped-wind branches the same integral is evaluated over the
corresponding piecewise intervals of \(n(R)\).  A finite shell beginning at
\(R_s\) contributes the radial source
\begin{equation}
q_R(R_s,\Delta R)
\equiv
n(R_s)\frac{V(R_s+\Delta R)-V(R_s)}{\Delta R},
\end{equation}
This is the number of upstream particles swept per unit radius, evaluated over a
finite shell.

The maximum acceleration energy is set by balancing acceleration with
synchrotron loss.  The cutoff Lorentz factor is
\begin{equation}
\gamma_{\max}=\frac{3m_ec^2}{\left(8e^3B'\right)^{1/2}} .
\end{equation}
The source is not cut below \(\gamma_{\max}\).  Above \(\gamma_{\max}\), it is
exponentially suppressed:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma)=
\begin{cases}
1, & \gamma\le\gamma_{\max},\\
\exp(1-\gamma/\gamma_{\max}), & \gamma>\gamma_{\max}.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
The forward-shock source is a power law with this high-energy cutoff.  The
normalization over the active support is
\begin{equation}
a_p=f_e\frac{1-p}{\gamma_{\max}^{1-p}-\gamma_m^{1-p}} .
\end{equation}
The local source amplitude is \(A_R\equiv q_R(R_s,\Delta R)a_p\).  In a
log-gamma cell, the source shape is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
S_x(x)
&=\ln10\,10^{(1-p)x}
\mathcal{C}_{\max}(10^x)H(10^x-\gamma_m),\\
Q_{x,j}
&=A_R\,\mathcal I_{x,j},\\
\mathcal I_{x,j}
&=\frac{1}{\Delta x_j}
\int_{x_{j-1/2}}^{x_{j+1/2}}
S_x(x)\,dx .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(H\) is the Heaviside function.  Multiplication by \(\Delta R\) gives the
number of electrons injected by that finite shell.

The injection minimum depends on the electron index \(p\).  For \(p>2\),
\begin{equation}
\gamma_m=1+\frac{p-2}{p-1}
\frac{\epsilon_e}{f_e}
\frac{m_p}{m_e}(\Gamma-1),
\end{equation}
For \(1<p<2\),
\begin{equation}
\gamma_m=
\left[
\frac{2-p}{p-1}
\frac{\epsilon_e}{f_e}\frac{m_p}{m_e}(\Gamma-1)
\gamma_{\max}^{p-2}
\right]^{1/(p-1)}+1.
\end{equation}
For \(p=2\), the normalization is logarithmic:
\begin{equation}
\gamma_m\ln\left(\frac{\gamma_{\max}}{\gamma_m}\right)
=\frac{\epsilon_e}{f_e}\frac{m_p}{m_e}(\Gamma-1).
\end{equation}
The implemented injection model therefore uses the correct branch for \(p\); it
does not apply the \(p>2\) expression outside its domain.

\subsubsection{Initial spectrum on the shared grid}

The initial electron spectrum is placed on a logarithmic energy grid.  The grid
points are
\begin{equation}
\Delta_g\equiv \frac{\ln(\gamma_H/\gamma_L)}{N_\gamma-1},
\qquad
\gamma_i=\gamma_L\exp[(i-1)\Delta_g],
\end{equation}
where \(\gamma_L\) and \(\gamma_H\) are fixed grid bounds, and \(\gamma_H\) is
chosen from the initial acceleration cutoff.  All solvers start from the same
physical spectrum; only the storage coordinate can change.

For a power-law segment, conversion to the log-gamma density is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma}
&=A\gamma^{-s}\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma),\\
\frac{dN_e}{dx}=A\ln10\,\gamma^{1-s}\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma),
x&=\log_{10}\gamma .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The initial spectrum depends on the order of \(\gamma_c\) and \(\gamma_m\).  Let
\(N_0\equiv N_{\rm init}\).  In the fast-cooling case, \(\gamma_c<\gamma_m\), the
spectrum is
\begin{equation}
\begin{gathered}
A_{\rm f}\equiv N_0\gamma_c,\\
\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma}=
\begin{cases}
A_{\rm f}\gamma^{-2}\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma),
& \gamma_c\le\gamma<\gamma_m,\\
A_{\rm f}\gamma_m^{p-1}\gamma^{-p-1}
\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma),
& \gamma\ge\gamma_m,\\
0, & \text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
In the slow-cooling case, \(\gamma_m\le\gamma_c\), the spectrum is
\begin{equation}
\begin{gathered}
A_{\rm s}\equiv N_0(p-1)\gamma_m^{p-1},\\
\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma}=
\begin{cases}
A_{\rm s}\gamma^{-p}
\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma),
& \gamma_m\le\gamma<\gamma_c,\\
A_{\rm s}\gamma_c\gamma^{-p-1}
\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma),
& \gamma\ge\gamma_c,\\
0, & \text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
Point-value solvers sample these spectra at \(\gamma_i\).  Conservative solvers
store the cell average instead:
\begin{equation}
N_{x,j}^{\rm init}=\frac{1}{\Delta x_j}
\int_{x_{j-1/2}}^{x_{j+1/2}}
\frac{dN_e}{dx}\,dx ,
\end{equation}
The integral is split at \(\gamma_m\), \(\gamma_c\), and \(\gamma_{\max}\), so
each continuous segment is integrated separately.  The quadrature rule is
\begin{equation}
\int_a^b F(x)\,dx\simeq
\frac{b-a}{2}\sum_{q=1}^{N_{\rm G}} w_q^{\rm G}
F\!\left[\frac{a+b}{2}+\frac{b-a}{2}\xi_q\right],
\end{equation}
where \((\xi_q,w_q^{\rm G})\) are fixed Gauss--Legendre nodes and weights.  If a
solver stores a four-velocity coordinate \(y\), the same spectrum is transformed
only by the coordinate Jacobian:
\begin{equation}
N_{y,j}^{\rm init}=\frac{1}{\Delta y_j}
\int_{y_{j-1/2}}^{y_{j+1/2}}
\frac{dN_e}{dx}[x(y)]\,\frac{dx}{dy}\,dy .
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Coordinate projection of shell sources}

The forward-shock source is projected into whatever coordinate a solver stores.
Only the coordinate measure changes.  In a general storage coordinate \(y\), the
cell source is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
S_y(y)&=S_x[x(y)]\,\frac{dx}{dy},\\
Q_{y,j}
&=A_R\,\mathcal I_{y,j},\\
\mathcal I_{y,j}
&=\frac{1}{\Delta y_j}
\int_{y_{j-1/2}}^{y_{j+1/2}}
S_y(y)\,dy .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Thus each solver receives the same injected physical distribution, even when it
stores the distribution in a different coordinate.

\subsubsection{Reverse-shock kinetic source}

Reverse-shock electrons use a kinetic-energy power law.  This is different from
the forward-shock source, which is written as a power law in \(\gamma\).  The
active part of the reverse-shock source is selected by
\begin{equation}
I_m(\gamma)=
\begin{cases}
1, & \gamma>\gamma_m,\\
0, & \gamma\le\gamma_m .
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
In the log-gamma coordinate, the unnormalized density is
\begin{equation}
\Psi_x(\gamma)=\gamma\ln10\,(\gamma-1)^{-p}
\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma)I_m(\gamma).
\end{equation}
The cell averages \(\bar\Psi_{x,j}\) are then normalized to the total source
count \(Q\):
\begin{equation}
Q_{x,j}^{\rm kin}=Q\,
\frac{\bar\Psi_{x,j}}
{\sum_b \bar\Psi_{x,b}\Delta x_b}.
\end{equation}
In a four-velocity coordinate, the same physical source becomes
\begin{equation}
\Psi_y(y)=\gamma(y)\ln10\,\frac{dx}{dy}
[\gamma(y)-1]^{-p}
\mathcal{C}_{\max}[\gamma(y)]I_m[\gamma(y)],
\end{equation}
and is normalized in the same way.  The source must have a non-empty active
support.  If no active cell exists, the source is outside its domain; the code
does not add an artificial floor.

\subsubsection{Thermal electron component}

The optional thermal component adds a Maxwell--Juttner electron population.  Its
total electron number is \(N_{\rm th}\).  The shock-front four-speed \(u\) sets
the dimensionless temperature:
\begin{equation}
\Theta(u)=
\frac{u^2(1+a_{\rm MJ}u)}{3(1+u+a_{\rm MJ}u^2)} .
\end{equation}
Here \(a_{\rm MJ}\) is the fixed coefficient in the temperature fit.  The
continuous Maxwell--Juttner shape is
\begin{equation}
\phi_\gamma(\gamma;\Theta)=
\frac{\gamma^2\beta_\gamma e^{-\gamma/\Theta}}
{\Theta K_2(1/\Theta)},
\qquad
\beta_\gamma=(1-\gamma^{-2})^{1/2}.
\end{equation}
The calculation uses a finite electron grid, so the shape is normalized on that
grid:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{N}_{\rm th}=
\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N_\gamma-1}
[\phi_\gamma(\gamma_j)+\phi_\gamma(\gamma_{j+1})]
(\gamma_{j+1}-\gamma_j).
\end{equation}
The log-gamma density added to the grid is then
\begin{equation}
\phi_x(\gamma_j)=
\frac{\phi_\gamma(\gamma_j)}{\mathcal{N}_{\rm th}}\,
\gamma_j\ln10 .
\end{equation}
For a source update, the added log-coordinate source is \(N_{\rm th}\phi_x\).  For
an initial centered population, the added energy density is
\(N_{\rm th}\phi_x/(\gamma\ln10)\).  This branch requires \(\Theta>0\), grid
points with \(\gamma_e>1\), and a non-zero thermal normalization.

\subsubsection{Shared energy-coordinate maps}

Several electron solvers store the same spectrum in different energy
coordinates.  The reference coordinate is
\(x_g=\log_{10}\gamma_e\).  In the log-gamma branch,
\begin{equation}
y=x_g,\qquad \gamma_e(y)=10^y,\qquad \frac{dx_g}{dy}=1.
\end{equation}
In the four-velocity branch, the stored coordinate is built from
\begin{equation}
\begin{gathered}
s\equiv\gamma_s^2-1,\\
\mathcal{Y}(\gamma)\equiv
\log_{10}\left[1+\frac{\gamma^2-1}{s}\right].
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
The stored coordinate is
\begin{equation}
y=\mathcal{Y}(\gamma_e).
\end{equation}
The inverse map and Jacobian are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_e(y)=\left[1+s(10^y-1)\right]^{1/2},
x_g(y)=\log_{10}\gamma_e(y),
\\
\frac{dx_g}{dy}=\frac{s10^y}{2\gamma_e^2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\gamma_s\) sets where the coordinate changes from logarithmic
four-velocity behavior to logarithmic energy behavior.  It is a coordinate
parameter, not a radiative cutoff.  A grid with \(N_\gamma\) cells is uniform in
\(y\):
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
y_{i+1/2}=y_L+\frac{i}{N_\gamma}(y_H-y_L),
\\
y_L=\mathcal{Y}(\gamma_{\min}),\qquad
y_H=\mathcal{Y}(\gamma_{\max}).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The diagnostic arrays returned from this grid are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
x_{g,i+1/2}&=x_g(y_{i+1/2}),\\
\gamma_i&=\gamma_e\!\left[(y_{i-1/2}+y_{i+1/2})/2\right].
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The conditions \(\gamma_s>1\) and \(\gamma_{\max}>\gamma_{\min}\) make \(s>0\)
and give a nonzero coordinate interval.  The Jacobian \(dx_g/dy\) converts
between the storage coordinate and \(dN_e/d\gamma_e\).  The same factor is used
in injection integrals and cooling face displacements.

\subsection{Shared conservative shell-transport primitives}

\subsubsection{Solver selector}

The shared transport layer chooses the one-dimensional shell solver before the
update starts:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{S}\in\{\texttt{fullhide},\texttt{dg}\},
\end{equation}
The default choice is \texttt{fullhide}.  This selector changes only the
numerical representation.  The physical source, cooling, and adiabatic terms are
assembled before the choice is made.

\subsubsection{Finite-volume shell update}

The finite-volume update evolves cell averages.  In a storage coordinate \(y\),
the cell average is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
N_{y,j}(R)=\frac{1}{\Delta y_j}
\int_{y_{j-1/2}}^{y_{j+1/2}}\frac{dN_e}{dy}\,dy ,
\\
\Delta y_j=y_{j+1/2}-y_{j-1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The particles move through the energy grid because they cool.  Let \(D_j\) be the
radiative part of \(d\ln\gamma_e/dR\) at a node, and let \(D_{\rm ad}\) be the
adiabatic part.  The face speed in the storage coordinate is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{V}_{j+1/2}=
\frac{(D_j+D_{j+1})/2+D_{\rm ad}}
{\ln10\,(dx_g/dy)_{j+1/2}} .
\end{equation}
The numerator is the physical energy drift.  The denominator converts that drift
from \(\ln\gamma_e\) to the stored coordinate \(y\).

The face flux uses the sign of this speed:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{F}_{j+1/2}^{n+1}
=\mathcal{V}_{j+1/2}^{+}N_{j+1}^{n+1}
+\mathcal{V}_{j+1/2}^{-}N_j^{n+1},
\end{equation}
where \(\mathcal{V}^{+}=\max(\mathcal{V},0)\) and
\(\mathcal{V}^{-}=\min(\mathcal{V},0)\).  The conservative update is
\begin{equation}
N_j^{n+1}=N_j^n+\Delta R\,Q_j
+\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta y_j}
\left(\mathcal{F}_{j+1/2}^{n+1}
-\mathcal{F}_{j-1/2}^{n+1}\right).
\end{equation}
The low-energy face is closed.  Cooled particles therefore remain in the first
cell instead of leaving the finite shell population.  The resulting tridiagonal
system is solved by the Thomas algorithm.  The final positive projection
\(\Pi_+(X)=\max(X,0)\) acts only on solved cell averages.  It does not smooth
neighboring bins.

\subsubsection{Diagnostic reconstruction}

Diagnostics need \(dN_e/d\gamma_e\), even when the solver stored cell averages in
\(y\).  For this conversion, the layer reconstructs a positive exponential
profile inside each cell:
\begin{equation}
\frac{dN_e}{dy}=A_j\exp[\sigma_j(y-y_j)],
\qquad
y_j=\frac{y_{j-1/2}+y_{j+1/2}}{2}.
\end{equation}
For an interior cell with positive neighboring averages, define
\begin{equation}
\ell_j^-=\frac{\ln N_{y,j}-\ln N_{y,j-1}}{y_j-y_{j-1}},
\qquad
\ell_j^+=\frac{\ln N_{y,j+1}-\ln N_{y,j}}{y_{j+1}-y_j}.
\end{equation}
The monotone log-slope is
\begin{equation}
\sigma_j=\operatorname{minmod}(\ell_j^-,\ell_j^+),
\end{equation}
and otherwise \(\sigma_j=0\).  The cell average fixes the amplitude:
\begin{equation}
A_j=
\begin{cases}
N_{y,j}, & z_j=0,\\
N_{y,j}\,z_j/\sinh z_j, & z_j\ne0,
\end{cases}
\qquad
z_j=\frac{1}{2}\sigma_j\Delta y_j .
\end{equation}
The centered diagnostic is then
\begin{equation}
\left.\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma_e}\right|_j
=
\frac{A_j}{d\gamma_e/dy}
=
\frac{A_j}{\gamma_j\ln10\,(dx_g/dy)_j}.
\end{equation}
This conversion is diagnostic only.  It is used when one-dimensional,
\(\chi\)-resolved, or DG drivers need a shell-centered
\(dN_e/d\gamma_e\) record.

\subsubsection{Cooling-break and substep diagnostics}

The cooling-break diagnostic finds the energy where cooling and expansion have
the same local scale.  With face midpoint energies
\begin{equation}
\gamma_{j+1/2}=(\gamma_j\gamma_{j+1})^{1/2},
\end{equation}
the diagnostic \(\gamma_c\) solves
\begin{equation}
\bar{\Lambda}(\gamma_c)=\frac{1}{R\ln 10},
\end{equation}
where \(\bar{\Lambda}\) is the face-averaged loss coefficient.  The
injection-support locator uses the same log-edge grid and selects cells
overlapping
\begin{equation}
\log_{10}\gamma_m\le x\le \log_{10}(\chi_{\rm sup}\gamma_{\max}).
\end{equation}
Here \(\chi_{\rm sup}\) sets the finite numerical support scanned by the source
routine.  It is not a second physical cutoff; the physical cutoff remains
\(\gamma_{\max}\).

For adaptive one-dimensional electron substeps, the controller compares one full
step with two half steps over the active cell support \(\mathcal{A}\):
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{A}\equiv
\left\{j\,\middle|\,N^{\rm h}_j>0\right\},
\qquad
\epsilon_{\rm step}=
\max_{j\in\mathcal{A}}
\frac{|N^{\rm h}_j-N^{\rm f}_j|}
{|N^{\rm h}_j|},
\end{equation}
where \(N^{\rm f}\) is the full-step solution and \(N^{\rm h}\) is the
two-half-step solution.  A substep is accepted when
\(\epsilon_{\rm step}\le\epsilon_{\rm tol}\).  The tolerance
\(\epsilon_{\rm tol}\) is a numerical accuracy setting, not a microphysical
parameter.

\subsubsection{triangular cooling sweep}

A cooling sweep moves electrons from high energy to low energy while keeping the
cell content in conservative form.  Let \(N_j\) be the electron number in energy
cell \(j\).  A face coefficient \(\lambda_{j+1/2}\) gives the number flux across
the face between cells \(j\) and \(j+1\), and \(b_0\) gives the diagonal weight of
the implicit step.  We write the sweep coefficients as
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
p_j&=b_0-\lambda_{j-1/2}\quad (j\ge2),\qquad p_1=p_2,\\
c_j&=\frac{2\lambda_{j+1/2}}{p_j+p_{j+1}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(p_j\) is the local diagonal coefficient and \(c_j\) couples cell \(j\) to
the next higher-energy cell.  Starting at the highest populated bin, the solver
then evaluates
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
N_N^{n+1}&=r_N,\\
N_j^{n+1}&=\Pi_+\!\left(r_j-c_jN_{j+1}^{n+1}\right).
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:triangular-cooling-sweep}
\end{equation}
Here \(r_j\) is the known right-hand side after source injection and diagonal
division.  \(N_j^{n+1}\) is the updated cell content.  \(\Pi_+\) projects the
computed cell content onto the non-negative domain used by this conservative
electron solver.

\subsubsection{Interpolation primitives for remaps}

Remaps need an integral of the old electron spectrum over the interval reached
by each cooling characteristic.  For point data, the local second-order
interpolant is
\begin{equation}
P_2(x)=\sum_{a=0}^{2}y_a\ell_a(x),
\qquad
\ell_a(x)=\prod_{b\ne a}\frac{x-x_b}{x_a-x_b}.
\end{equation}
Here \(x_a\) are the three local grid points, \(y_a\) are the stored values, and
\(\ell_a\) are the usual Lagrange basis polynomials.  For cell averages, the
code instead reconstructs the content in each cell with a parabolic profile.  In
the local coordinate \(\xi=(x-x_{j-1/2})/\Delta x_j\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
Q_j(\xi)&=Q_{L,j}
+(Q_{R,j}-Q_{L,j}+Q_{6,j})\xi
-Q_{6,j}\xi^2,\\
Q_{6,j}&=6Q_j-3(Q_{L,j}+Q_{R,j}).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(Q_j\) is the cell average, \(Q_{L,j}\) and \(Q_{R,j}\) are the left and
right interface values, and \(Q_{6,j}\) fixes the cell average exactly.  The
interface values come from the three-point interpolation above and are limited to
neighboring extrema.  A local extremum uses \(Q_{L,j}=Q_{R,j}=Q_j\).  If the
parabola would put negative content inside a positive cell, the interface
deviations are scaled by
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\theta_j&=\frac{Q_j}{Q_j-Q_{\min,j}},\\
Q_{L,R}&\leftarrow Q_j+\theta_j(Q_{L,R}-Q_j),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(Q_{\min,j}\) is the minimum of the parabolic profile in the cell.  This
step keeps the cell average unchanged.  The integral over any part of the cell is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
{\cal M}_j(\xi_l,\xi_r)
&=\Delta x_j\left[
Q_LD_1+\frac{Q_R-Q_L+Q_6}{2}D_2\right.\\
&\left.\hspace{3.0em}
-\frac{Q_6}{3}D_3
\right],
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(D_m\equiv\xi_r^m-\xi_l^m\).  A prefix sum gives
\(M(x)=\int_{x_{1/2}}^xQ(x')\,dx'\).  The remap uses \(M(x)\) to move exactly the
old cell content that lies between two traced faces.

\subsubsection{Positive analytic-source reconstruction}

Analytic source terms need a positive reconstruction because injection can span
many decades in electron energy.  Power-law injection and exponential cutoffs are
nearly linear in \(\ln Q\), not in \(Q\).  If three neighboring source-cell
averages are positive, the logarithmic slope is
\begin{equation}
\sigma_j=\operatorname{minmod}\left[
\frac{\ln Q_j-\ln Q_{j-1}}{x_j-x_{j-1}},
\frac{\ln Q_{j+1}-\ln Q_j}{x_{j+1}-x_j}
\right],
\end{equation}
and otherwise \(\sigma_j=0\).  With this slope, the reconstructed source in cell
\(j\) is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
Q_j(x)&=A_j\exp[\sigma_j(x-x_j)],\\
A_j&=Q_jG(z_j),\qquad z_j=\frac{\sigma_j\Delta x_j}{2},\\
G(z)&=
\begin{cases}
1, & z=0,\\
z/\sinh z, & z\ne0.
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(A_j\) is chosen so that the cell average remains \(Q_j\).  The integral of
this positive source profile is
\begin{equation}
{\cal S}_j(x_l,x_r)=
\begin{cases}
A_j\Delta x_{lr}, & \sigma_j=0,\\
A_j[E_j(x_r)-E_j(x_l)]/\sigma_j, & \sigma_j\ne0,
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
where \(\Delta x_{lr}\equiv x_r-x_l\) and \(E_j(x)\equiv\exp[\sigma_j(x-x_j)]\).
For diagnostics, the log-coordinate content is converted to a centered physical
spectrum by
\begin{equation}
\left.\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma_e}\right|_j
=\frac{A_j}{\gamma_j\ln10},
\end{equation}
where \(x=\log_{10}\gamma_e\), \(\gamma_j=10^{x_j}\), and \(A_j\) is the
reconstruction amplitude of the transported \(dN_e/dx\) cell.

\subsection{Characteristic remap primitive}

\subsubsection{Affine characteristic traceback}

The characteristic solver follows the energy from which an electron came before
it cooled.  It uses \(u=1/\gamma_e=10^{-x}\), because synchrotron cooling is
simple in this variable.  For a global affine cooling law
\begin{equation}
\frac{du}{dR}=a+bu,
\end{equation}
a point that arrives at \(u\) after radial lag \(\ell\) came from
\begin{equation}
u_{\rm back}=
\begin{cases}
u-a\ell, & b=0,\\
(u+a/b)e^{-b\ell}-a/b, & b\ne0.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Here \(a\) is the energy-independent part of the drift and \(b\) is the part
proportional to \(u\).  For a piecewise cooling curve, the code writes
\begin{equation}
w(u)=uD(u),\qquad
w(u)=a_r+s_ru\quad {\rm in\ cell}\ r,
\end{equation}
where \(D(u)\) is the supplied loss rate and \(a_r,s_r\) are the linear
coefficients in cell \(r\).  The traced equation in that cell is
\begin{equation}
\frac{du}{dR}=a_r+(s_r+b_{\rm ad})u .
\end{equation}
Here \(b_{\rm ad}\) is the adiabatic cooling coefficient.  The same inverse is
used inside one \(u\) cell.  If the traced point reaches a face, the remaining
radial lag continues in the next cell.  With traced arrival faces
\(x_{j\pm1/2}^{\rm back}(\ell)\), the conservative remap is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal R_j[M;\ell]\equiv
\frac{
M[x_{j+1/2}^{\rm back}(\ell)]
-M[x_{j-1/2}^{\rm back}(\ell)]
}{\Delta x_j}.
\end{equation}
This expression means that the new content of cell \(j\) equals the old content
between the two traced faces, divided by the cell width.

\subsubsection{Characteristic source quadrature}

A source-coupled remap adds newly injected electrons while old electrons cool
along their characteristics.  The update is
\begin{equation}
N_j^{n+1}
=\mathcal R_j[M_N;\Delta R]
+\Delta R\,s_Q
\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\rm G}^{\rm C}}w_m\mathcal R_j[M_Q;s_m\Delta R].
\end{equation}
Here \(M_N\) is the PPM prefix of the old population, \(M_Q\) is the
positive-source prefix, and \(s_Q\) is the source normalization for the current
radial step.  The quadrature uses \(N_{\rm G}^{\rm C}\) Gauss points and the map
\begin{equation}
s_m=t_m^{q_{\rm C}},\qquad
w_m=q_{\rm C}W_mt_m^{q_{\rm C}-1},
\end{equation}
where \(q_{\rm C}\) is the declared source-time clustering index and
\((t_m,W_m)\) are the Gauss nodes and weights on \([0,1]\).  The source-free
remap is the same formula with \(s_Q=0\) and one traced boundary set.

\subsubsection{Semi-Lagrangian source split}

The uniform-grid semi-Lagrangian solver used by \texttt{slc1\_1d} separates
injection and cooling in a symmetric step.  For a prefix \(M\), define
\begin{equation}
\mathcal I_j(M;x_-,x_+)\equiv\frac{M(x_+)-M(x_-)}{\Delta x}.
\end{equation}
Here \(x_-\) and \(x_+\) are the two departure faces of cell \(j\).  First the
solver adds half of the source and traces the faces back:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
N_j^{1/2}&=\Pi_+\!\left(N_j^n+\frac{\Delta R}{2}Q_j\right),\\
x_{j\pm1/2}^{\rm dep}
&=\Pi_x\!\left(x_{j\pm1/2}+\Delta R\,f_{j\pm1/2}\right),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(f_{j\pm1/2}\) is the face drift and \(\Pi_x\) keeps the departure point
inside the computational \(x\) domain.  The final cell content is
\begin{equation}
N_j^{n+1}
=\Pi_+\!\left[
\mathcal I_j(M_{1/2};x_{j-1/2}^{\rm dep},x_{j+1/2}^{\rm dep})
+\frac{\Delta R}{2}Q_j
\right].
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Implicit flux-split primitive}

The implicit flux-split solver writes cooling as a flux through cell faces.  With
face velocity \(v_{j+1/2}\), the conservative update is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
N_j^{n+1}&=N_j^n+\Delta RQ_j+F_{j+1/2}-F_{j-1/2},\\
F_{j+1/2}&=\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta x_j}
\left(v_{j+1/2}^{+}N_{j+1}^{n+1}
+v_{j+1/2}^{-}N_j^{n+1}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(F_{j+1/2}\) is the flux through the right face of cell \(j\).  The signs
\(v^+\) and \(v^-\) select the upwind cell for the implicit step, and
\(\Delta x_j\) is the local cell width.  A pre-integrated sequence uses the same
matrix after replacing \(\Delta R\,v_{j+1/2}\) by the face displacement
\({\cal F}_{j+1/2}\) and \(\Delta RQ_j\) by the source content \({\cal Q}_j\).

\subsubsection{Legacy fullhide triangular path}

The log-\(\gamma\) \texttt{fullhide} branch uses the same high-to-low triangular
solve for a fixed grid.  Its face speed and sweep coefficient are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
v_{j+1/2}&=D_{j+1/2}+\frac{1}{R\ln10},\\
\lambda_{j+1/2}&=-\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta x}v_{j+1/2},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(D_{j+1/2}\) is the radiative cooling coefficient at the cell face and
the \(1/(R\ln10)\) term is the adiabatic drift in \(x=\log_{10}\gamma_e\).  The
linear system is written as
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
p_j&=1-\lambda_{j-1/2},\\
c_j&=\frac{2\lambda_{j+1/2}}{p_j+p_{j+1}},\\
r_j&=\frac{N_j^n+\Delta RQ_j}{p_j},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and is solved by the high-to-low sweep in Equation~\eqref{eq:triangular-cooling-sweep}.
The space--radius \texttt{fullhide} branch applies the same solve over a sequence
of source and coupling columns.  For substep \(m\),
\begin{equation}
(1+\lambda_{j-1/2}^{m})N_j^{m}
-\lambda_{j+1/2}^{m}N_{j+1}^{m}
=N_j^{m-1}+S_j^{m},
\end{equation}
where \(S_j^m\) is the source added during that substep.  The lowest- and
highest-energy rows use their single adjacent face.  After all
\(m=1,\ldots,N_{\rm step}\) columns are solved, the last column is the updated
electron spectrum.

\subsubsection{Centralized transport diagnostics}

The transport routines also return diagnostic ranges used by later radiation
calls.  A spectral peak sampled on three positive neighboring bins is estimated
by a log-parabola through \((\ln\nu_{l,c,r},\ln P_{l,c,r})\):
\begin{equation}
\ln\nu_{\rm pk}
=x_c+\frac{(y_l-y_r)(x_c-x_l)}
{2(y_l-2y_c+y_r)},
\end{equation}
with the result restricted to the bracketing interval.  The active energy and
\(\chi\) ranges used by the two-dimensional driver are support definitions:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
i_{\rm act}&=\max\left[i_{\rm lo},\,i_{\rm src},\,
\min(N_\gamma,i_{\rm pop}+1)\right],\\
Q_i&>\epsilon_\gamma Q_{\rm pk},\qquad
N_i>\epsilon_\gamma\max(N_{\rm pk},Q_{\rm pk}),\\
a_{\rm act}&=\min(N_\chi,a_{\rm pop}+1),\\
N_{\chi,a}&>\epsilon_\chi N_{\chi,\rm pk}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(i_{\rm lo}\) is the lower transported energy index.  The quantities
\(Q_{\rm pk}\), \(N_{\rm pk}\), and \(N_{\chi,\rm pk}\) are peak source,
energy-population, and \(\chi\)-population amplitudes.  The thresholds
\(\epsilon_\gamma\) and \(\epsilon_\chi\) limit the transport scan to populated
cells.  They do not define a physical cutoff in the electron spectrum.  The
radial substep estimate uses the largest active energy drift,
\begin{equation}
\xi_{\max}=\max_{a\le a_{\rm act},\,i<i_{\rm act}}
\left|D_{ia}+A_{{\rm ad},a}\right|,
\end{equation}
where \(D_{ia}\) is the radiative drift and \(A_{{\rm ad},a}\) is the adiabatic
drift.  If the active-population scan finds zero drift, the routine evaluates all
\(\chi\) cells so that the reported substep size is based on the stored grid.

\subsection{Implicit finite-volume \texttt{fullhide\_1d} solver}

The \texttt{fullhide\_1d} solver follows the forward-shock electron population on
a finite-volume energy grid.  Each cell stores the number of electrons per cell
width, so the scheme can move electrons between cells without losing the total
number.  The nonthermal branch uses the four-velocity coordinate \(y\) and stores
\begin{equation}
N_{y,j}(R)=\frac{1}{\Delta y_j}
\int_{y_{j-1/2}}^{y_{j+1/2}}\frac{dN_e}{dy}\,dy .
\label{eq:fullhide-cell-average}
\end{equation}
Here \(N_{y,j}\) is the cell average, \(y_{j\pm1/2}\) are the cell faces, and
\(\Delta y_j\) is the cell width.  The solver evolves these averages as the shock
moves from one radius to the next.

\subsubsection{Local shell state}

Each radial shell supplies the local shock conditions used by the electron
solver.  For shell \(i\), the state is evaluated at the left shell radius:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
R_{s,i}&=R_{i-1},\\
\bar\Gamma_i&=\frac{\Gamma_i+\Gamma_{i-1}}{2},\\
n_i&=n(R_{s,i}),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(R_{s,i}\) is the source radius, \(\bar\Gamma_i\) is the shell-averaged
Lorentz factor, and \(n_i\) is the upstream density.  These quantities set the
magnetic field and the upper injection energy:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
B_i'&=\left[32\pi\epsilon_B n_i m_pc^2
\bar\Gamma_i(\bar\Gamma_i-1)\right]^{1/2},\\
\gamma_{\max,i}&=\frac{3m_ec^2}{(8B_i'e^3)^{1/2}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(B_i'\) is the comoving magnetic field and \(\gamma_{\max,i}\) is the
acceleration-limited electron Lorentz factor.  The same shell state gives the
synchrotron cooling Lorentz factor and shock speed,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{c,i}&=\frac{6\pi m_ec(1+z)}
{\sigma_TB_i'^2\bar\Gamma_i t_{{\rm obs},i}},\\
\beta_i&=(1-\bar\Gamma_i^{-2})^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(t_{{\rm obs},i}\) is the observer time attached to the shell and
\(\beta_i c\) is the shell speed used in radial cooling rates.

\subsubsection{Fixed radial substeps}

The finite-volume solve may split one radial shell into smaller radial substeps.
The trial substep is set by the faster of synchrotron cooling and adiabatic
cooling.  At the shell midpoint,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
R_{m,i}&=\frac{R_i+R_{i-1}}{2},\\
a_i&=\frac{\sigma_TB_i'^2}
{6\pi m_ec^2\beta_i\bar\Gamma_i},\\
\Delta R_0&=\frac{\eta_{\rm fh}}
{a_i\gamma_{\max,i}+\eta_{\rm ad}/R_{m,i}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(a_i\gamma_{\max,i}\) estimates the fastest synchrotron drift and
\(\eta_{\rm ad}/R_{m,i}\) estimates the adiabatic drift.  The shell width then
sets the number of fixed substeps,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta R_i&=R_i-R_{i-1},\\
L_i&=\max\!\left[
N_{\rm fh,min},
\min\!\left(N_{\rm fh,max},
\left\lfloor\frac{\Delta R_i}{\Delta R_0}\right\rfloor\right)
\right],\\
\Delta R&=\frac{\Delta R_i}{L_i}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(L_i\) is bounded by the public minimum and maximum substep counts.  Each
substep has radial width \(\Delta R\).

\subsubsection{Implicit finite-volume update}

One implicit substep treats cooling as flux through the faces of each energy
cell.  On face \(j+1/2\), the coordinate speed is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\bar D_{j+1/2}&=\frac{D_j+D_{j+1}}{2},\\
v_{j+1/2}&=
\frac{\bar D_{j+1/2}+R^{-1}}
{\ln 10\,(dx_g/dy)_{j+1/2}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(D_j\) is the radiative loss coefficient in cell \(j\).  Positive \(v\)
means motion toward lower electron energy.  With a closed low-energy boundary,
the conservative update is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
N_j^{n+1}&=N_j^n+\Delta R\,Q_j
+G_{j+1/2}-G_{j-1/2},\\
G_{j+1/2}&=\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta y_j}
\left(v_{j+1/2}^{+}N_{j+1}^{n+1}
+v_{j+1/2}^{-}N_j^{n+1}\right),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(v^+=\max(v,0)\) and \(v^-=\min(v,0)\).  When the low boundary is closed,
the update does not introduce a ghost-cell population below the first active
face.  The high-energy loss flux is solved at the new radius instead of being
applied explicitly.  This is why the step remains stable in stiff cooling
regimes.

\subsubsection{Finite-shell source normalization}

Electron injection is normalized by the number of upstream particles swept by a
finite radial shell.  It is therefore a source per unit radius, not a time rate.
For a substep that begins at radius \(R_s\), define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
V(R)&=\frac{4\pi R^3}{3},\\
q_R(R_s,\Delta R)&\equiv
n(R_s)\frac{V(R_s+\Delta R)-V(R_s)}{\Delta R},\\
a_p(R)&\equiv
f_e\,\frac{1-p}{\gamma_{\max}^{1-p}(R)-\gamma_m^{1-p}(R)} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(V(R)\) is the spherical volume, \(q_R\) is the number of upstream electrons
swept per unit radius over the finite shell, and \(a_p\) normalizes the injected
power law.  In the thin-shell limit, \(q_R\) approaches \(4\pi R_s^2n(R_s)\).

\subsubsection{Uniform-medium integrated solve}

In a strictly uniform medium without the thermal-electron branch, all fixed
substeps can be combined into one implicit solve.  The substep radii are
\begin{equation}
R_r=R_{i-1}+r\Delta R,\qquad r=1,\ldots,L_i .
\end{equation}
The total nonthermal source normalization over the shell is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
=a_{p,i}\sum_{r=1}^{L_i}\Delta R\,q_R(R_r,\Delta R),
\qquad a_{p,i}\equiv a_p(R_i).
\end{equation}
The source shape on the \(y\) grid is
\begin{equation}
\phi_y(R,y)\equiv
\ln 10\,\gamma_e^{1-p}
\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma_e)
\frac{dx_g}{dy}.
\end{equation}
Here \(\mathcal{C}_{\max}\) is the high-energy cutoff factor.  The source added
to cell \(j\) is
\begin{equation}
\bar Q_{y,j}^{\rm int}
=\frac{\mathcal Q_i}{\Delta y_j}
\int_{y_{j-1/2}}^{y_{j+1/2}}\phi_y(R_i,y)\,dy ,
\end{equation}
and the integrated face displacement is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{F}_{j+1/2}=
\frac{\Delta R_i\bar D_{j+1/2}
+\sum_{r=1}^{L_i}\Delta R/R_r}
{\ln 10\,(dx_g/dy)_{j+1/2}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The merged solve uses the same tridiagonal matrix as the one-substep update.  It
replaces
\(\Delta R\,v_{j+1/2}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{j+1/2}\) and
\(\Delta R\,Q_j\rightarrow\bar Q_{y,j}^{\rm int}\).

\subsubsection{Substep-by-substep branch}

Non-uniform density and thermal electrons require explicit radial substeps.  At
each substep endpoint \(R_r\), the solver re-evaluates \(n(R_r)\), \(B'\),
\(\gamma_{\max}\), and \(\gamma_m\).  The local nonthermal source is
\begin{equation}
Q_{y,j}^{(r)}
=\frac{q_R(R_r,\Delta R)a_p(R_r)}{\Delta y_j}
\int_{y_{j-1/2}}^{y_{j+1/2}}\phi_y(R_r,y)\,dy ,
\end{equation}
and a Maxwell--Juttner source is added when \texttt{thermal\_electrons} is
enabled.  The radiative cooling coefficient is assembled once from the shell
photon field and then scaled with the local density:
\begin{equation}
D_j(R_r)=D_j(R_{i-1})\frac{n(R_r)}{n(R_{i-1})},
\end{equation}
which is exact for local synchrotron cooling when \(\bar\Gamma_i\) and the shell
photon shape are fixed.  For the full cooling array, this is the supported
density-response approximation.

\subsubsection{Adaptive fullhide substeps}

Adaptive substeps control the radial integration error in the electron spectrum.
For one trial \(\Delta R\), the driver compares one full implicit step with two
half implicit steps.  Let \(N_j^{\rm f}\) and \(N_j^{\rm h}\) be these two
solutions.  The relative difference is measured on non-empty cells:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{I}\equiv
\left\{j\,\middle|\,N_j^{\rm h}>0\right\},
\qquad
\epsilon_{\rm adapt}=
\max_{j\in\mathcal{I}}
\frac{|N_j^{\rm h}-N_j^{\rm f}|}
{N_j^{\rm h}} .
\end{equation}
The accepted state is \(N_j^{\rm h}\), because it uses the smaller radial step.
Empty cells do not set the relative-error scale.  If
\(\epsilon_{\rm adapt}\le\epsilon_{\rm tol}\), the trial step is accepted.  If the
error is larger, the trial step is reduced down to
\((R_i-R_{i-1})/N_{\rm sub,max}\).  Here \(\epsilon_{\rm tol}\) and
\(N_{\rm sub,max}\) are the public electron-substep tolerance and maximum substep
count.  The controller changes only the radial step size; it does not modify the
electron spectrum after the solve.

\subsection{Coupled \texttt{fullhide} electron--photon pass}

\subsubsection{Coupled input state}

The coupled electron--photon pass is used when inverse-Compton cooling is computed
from a supplied photon field.  The electron grid uses
\(x=\log_{10}\gamma_e\).  The pass receives the cooling photon density
\(n_\nu^{\rm cool}\) and the secondary pair source \(Q_{x,j}^{\rm sec}\).  It
uses \(n_\nu^{\rm cool}\) for the inverse-Compton cooling rate.  Synchrotron
self-absorption heating still uses the local synchrotron seed field, so this
branch changes the cooling and source terms but not the observer projection.  In
one substep, the electron source is
\begin{equation}
Q_{x,j}=Q_{x,j}^{\rm inj}+Q_{x,j}^{\rm sec},
\end{equation}
where \(Q_{x,j}^{\rm inj}\) is the primary shell source on the same finite
radial shell.

\subsubsection{Coupled triangular solve}

The coupled pass advances the electrons with the same high-to-low triangular
solve used by \texttt{fullhide\_step}.  The face speed and signed step factor are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
w_{j+1/2}&=\bar D_{j+1/2}+\frac{1}{R\ln 10},\\
\lambda_{j+1/2}&=-\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta x}w_{j+1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\bar D_{j+1/2}\) is the face-averaged radiative cooling coefficient, and
\(1/(R\ln10)\) is the adiabatic drift in log energy.  The diagonal coefficient
and source-loaded right-hand side are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
p_j&=1-\lambda_{j-1/2}\quad (j\ge2),\qquad p_1=p_2,\\
r_j&=\frac{N_j^n+\Delta R\,Q_{x,j}}{p_j},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and adjacent cells are coupled by
\begin{equation}
c_j=\frac{2\lambda_{j+1/2}}{p_j+p_{j+1}} .
\end{equation}
The updated spectrum follows from the high-to-low sweep in
Equation~\eqref{eq:triangular-cooling-sweep}.

\subsubsection{Coupled output refresh}

The coupled pass returns diagnostics from the final electron spectrum.  At the
last output radius, the code re-evaluates the same synchrotron optical-depth grid
used by the separated \texttt{fullhide} path.  The returned \(\nu_m\), \(\nu_c\),
and \(\nu_a\) arrays therefore describe the final coupled state.

\subsection{Hybrid thermal \texttt{fullhide} branch}

\subsubsection{Hybrid coordinate and state}

The \texttt{fullhide\_1d\_hz} branch follows the same shell state and the same
finite-volume update as \texttt{fullhide\_1d}.  Its difference is the source
shape when \texttt{thermal\_electrons} is enabled.  It evolves the same cell
average \(N_{y,j}\) defined in Equation~\eqref{eq:fullhide-cell-average}.  The
coordinate is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\varpi_e&=\gamma_e^2-1,\qquad s=\gamma_s^2-1,\\
y&=\log_{10}\!\left(1+\frac{\varpi_e}{s}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(s\) sets the transition scale of the hybrid coordinate.  Radiation calls
still use \(\gamma_e\), but the transported content is stored in \(y\).  Since
\(x=\log_{10}\gamma_e\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_e(y)&=\left[1+s(10^y-1)\right]^{1/2},\\
\frac{dx}{dy}&=\frac{s\,10^y}{2\gamma_e^2},\\
\frac{dN_e}{dy}=\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma_e}\gamma_e\ln 10\,\frac{dx}{dy}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Hybrid source cells}

The hybrid branch uses the same finite-shell factor \(q_R(R_s,\Delta R)\) to
normalize new electrons.  If thermal electrons are disabled, the source is the
ordinary nonthermal shape written on the \(y\) grid:
\begin{equation}
Q_{y,j}^{\rm S}
=\frac{q_R(R_s,\Delta R)a_p(R_s)}{\Delta y_j}
\int_{y_{j-1/2}}^{y_{j+1/2}}\phi_y(R_s,y)\,dy .
\end{equation}
If thermal electrons are enabled, the same swept electrons are distributed over a
unit-normalized hybrid density \(\phi_{\rm h}\):
\begin{equation}
Q_{y,j}^{\rm h}=
\frac{q_R(R_s,\Delta R)}{\Delta y_j}
\int_{y_{j-1/2}}^{y_{j+1/2}}
\phi_{\rm h}(\gamma_e)\,
\gamma_e\ln 10\,\frac{dx}{dy}\,dy .
\end{equation}
The Jacobian \(\gamma_e\ln10\,dx/dy\) converts the physical density in
\(\gamma_e\) into the transported \(y\) coordinate.

\subsubsection{Hybrid density}

The hybrid density puts newly swept electrons into two parts.  A fraction
\(1-f_e\) is thermal below \(\gamma_m\), and a fraction \(f_e\) is a cutoff
power law above \(\gamma_m\):
\begin{equation}
\phi_{\rm h}(\gamma)=
\begin{cases}
(1-f_e)\phi_{\rm th}(\gamma), & 1<\gamma<\gamma_m,\\
f_e\phi_{\rm pl}(\gamma), & \gamma\ge\gamma_m .
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
The two normalized pieces are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\phi_{\rm th}(\gamma)&=
\frac{\gamma\sqrt{\gamma^2-1}\exp(-\gamma/\Theta)}
{I_{\rm th}},\\
\phi_{\rm pl}(\gamma)&=
\frac{\gamma^{-p}\exp(-\gamma/\gamma_{\max})}{I_{\rm pl}},\\
I_{\rm th}(\gamma_m,\Theta)&=
\int_1^{\gamma_m}\gamma\sqrt{\gamma^2-1}
\exp(-\gamma/\Theta)\,d\gamma,\\
I_{\rm pl}(\gamma_m,\gamma_{\max},p)&=
\gamma_{\max}^{1-p}\Gamma\!\left(1-p,\frac{\gamma_m}{\gamma_{\max}}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(I_{\rm th}\) normalizes the thermal part below \(\gamma_m\), and
\(I_{\rm pl}\) normalizes the nonthermal tail above \(\gamma_m\).  The temperature
is chosen so that the two pieces meet continuously at \(\gamma_m\).

\subsubsection{temperature continuity solve}

The dimensionless temperature \(\Theta\) is fixed by continuity, not by tuning.
At the joining Lorentz factor \(\gamma_m\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
(1-f_e)\phi_{\rm th}(\gamma_m)
=
f_e\phi_{\rm pl}(\gamma_m).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This condition is solved through the scalar equation
\begin{equation}
F(\Theta)=\frac{\gamma_m}{\Theta}+\ln I_{\rm th}-\ln C=0,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\ln C=
\ln\!\left[\left(\frac{1}{f_e}-1\right)
\sqrt{\gamma_m^2-1}\right]
+(1+p)\ln\gamma_m\\
+\ln I_{\rm pl}+\frac{\gamma_m}{\gamma_{\max}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Newton updates use the analytic derivative
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
I_2(\gamma_m,\Theta)&=
\int_1^{\gamma_m}\gamma^2\sqrt{\gamma^2-1}
\exp(-\gamma/\Theta)\,d\gamma,\\
\frac{dF}{d\Theta}&=
-\frac{\gamma_m}{\Theta^2}
+\frac{I_2}{\Theta^2I_{\rm th}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and therefore
\begin{equation}
\Theta_{r+1}=\Theta_r-\frac{F(\Theta_r)}
{\left.dF/d\Theta\right|_{\Theta_r}} .
\end{equation}
The solved \(\Theta\) sets the thermal width of the hybrid source.

\subsubsection{Special-function backend}

The special-function layer only evaluates the functions needed by the hybrid
source normalization.  It does not add another electron population.  The upper
incomplete gamma function is
\begin{equation}
\Gamma(s,x)=\int_x^\infty t^{s-1}e^{-t}\,dt
\end{equation}
and the needed modified Bessel functions are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
K_\nu(z)&=\int_0^\infty e^{-z\cosh u}\cosh(\nu u)\,du,\\
\nu&\in\{0,1\},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
with \(\nu=0\) and \(1\).  The implementation evaluates \(\Gamma(s,x)\) with
\texttt{dgamic} and evaluates \(K_\nu\) with the scaled SLATEC routines
\texttt{dbsk0e} and \texttt{dbsk1e}.  The electron solver uses these calls to get
the cutoff power-law normalization,
\begin{equation}
\int_{\gamma_m}^{\infty}\gamma^{-p}e^{-\gamma/\gamma_{\max}}\,d\gamma
=\gamma_{\max}^{1-p}
\Gamma\!\left(1-p,\frac{\gamma_m}{\gamma_{\max}}\right),
\end{equation}
and the modified-Bessel moments of the Maxwell--Juttner part.  These calls are
only numerical evaluations of the same analytic source terms.

\subsubsection{Thermal-tail moment subtraction}

The thermal source needs moments only below \(\gamma_m\).  The Bessel functions
first give complete thermal moments over the full relativistic Maxwellian.  With
\(\gamma=\cosh u\),
\begin{equation}
\sqrt{\gamma^2-1}\,d\gamma=\sinh^2u\,du .
\end{equation}
The complete moments are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
J_1(\Theta)&=2\Theta^2K_1(\Theta^{-1})
+\Theta K_0(\Theta^{-1}),\\
J_2(\Theta)&=(6\Theta^3+\Theta)K_1(\Theta^{-1})
+3\Theta^2K_0(\Theta^{-1}).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The code then subtracts the part above \(\gamma_m\).  With
\(a=\gamma_m/\Theta\), the tail expansion is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\sqrt{\gamma^2-1}
&=\gamma\sum_{r=0}^{N_b} c_r\gamma^{-2r},\\
c_r&=(-1)^r\binom{1/2}{r},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
so \(b_r=-c_r\) are the subtraction coefficients.  The retained finite moments are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
T_m(\Theta)&=\sum_{r=0}^{N_b}
b_r\Theta^{m+2-2r}\Gamma(m+2-2r,a),\\
I_1(\Theta)&=J_1(\Theta)+T_1(\Theta),\\
I_2(\Theta)&=J_2(\Theta)+T_2(\Theta).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(N_b\) is the fixed thermal-tail expansion order, not a fit parameter.
The terms are accumulated in increasing \(r\).  The series stops when the next
correction is smaller than \(\epsilon_{\rm th}\max(I,I_{\rm floor})\) for the
moment \(I\) being evaluated.  The quantities \(\epsilon_{\rm th}\) and
\(I_{\rm floor}\) are numerical controls of this evaluation.  For non-positive
orders, the recurrence
\begin{equation}
\Gamma(s-1,a)=\frac{\Gamma(s,a)-a^{s-1}\exp(-a)}{s-1}
\end{equation}
steps downward from \(\Gamma(0,a)\).

\subsubsection{temperature seed and update}

The root solve starts from a logarithmic mesh in temperature.  The trial values
are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Theta_r&=s_r\gamma_m,\\
s_r&=s_{\min}
\left(\frac{s_{\max}}{s_{\min}}\right)^{(r-1)/(N_s-1)},\\
r&=1,\ldots,N_s .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The quantities \(s_{\min}\), \(s_{\max}\), and \(N_s\) are fixed seed controls.
The bracket search shifts the trial scales by the fixed factor \(q_{\rm br}\)
until the lower and upper signs enclose the solution.  A secant interpolation
between the adjacent sign-changing entries gives the first iterate.  The root
function and derivative are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
F(\Theta)&=\frac{\gamma_m}{\Theta}+\ln I_1(\Theta)-\ln C,\\
\frac{dF}{d\Theta}
&=-\frac{\gamma_m}{\Theta^2}
+\frac{1}{I_1(\Theta)}\frac{dI_1}{d\Theta}
=-\frac{\gamma_m}{\Theta^2}+\frac{I_2(\Theta)}{\Theta^2I_1(\Theta)} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The fractional Newton correction is
\begin{equation}
\frac{\Delta\Theta}{\Theta}
=\frac{F(\Theta)}
{\gamma_m/\Theta-\Theta^{-1}I_2(\Theta)/I_1(\Theta)} .
\end{equation}
The iteration uses \(\Theta\leftarrow\Theta-\Delta\Theta\) and stops when
\(|\Delta\Theta/\Theta|<\epsilon_\Theta\).  If the condition is not met within
\(N_\Theta\) updates, the temperature solve has failed.

\subsubsection{Pointwise hybrid density and final advance}

After \(\Theta\) is fixed, the code evaluates the hybrid density point by point.
The logarithmic normalizations are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
A_{\rm th}&=\ln(1-f_e)-\ln I_1,\\
A_{\rm pl}&=\ln f_e-\ln I_{\rm pl},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and the density is
\begin{equation}
\phi_{\rm h}(\gamma)=
\begin{cases}
\exp[L_{\rm th}(\gamma)],
&1<\gamma<\gamma_m,\\
\exp[L_{\rm pl}(\gamma)],
&\gamma\ge\gamma_m,\\
0,&\gamma\le1.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_{\rm th}&=\ln[\gamma(\gamma^2-1)^{1/2}]
-\gamma/\Theta+A_{\rm th},\\
L_{\rm pl}&=-p\ln\gamma-\gamma/\gamma_{\max}+A_{\rm pl}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(L_{\rm th}\) and \(L_{\rm pl}\) are logarithmic densities for the thermal
and nonthermal pieces.  Values below the exponent cutoff \(\ell_{\rm exp}\) lie
outside the retained floating-point range and do not contribute to the cell
