integral.  After the hybrid source is assembled, the same upper-triangular
implicit system advances the spectrum.  The final output radius is then evaluated
from the final electron spectrum and the same synchrotron optical-depth grid used
for the active shells.

\subsection{Semi-Lagrangian log-\texorpdfstring{\(\gamma\)}{gamma} solver}

\subsubsection{SLC state and radial substeps}

The \texttt{slc1\_1d} branch solves the same electron cooling problem with a
semi-Lagrangian remap.  It uses the uniform coordinate
\(x=\log_{10}\gamma_e\) and stores the conservative cell averages \(N_{x,j}\).
The local \(B'\), \(\gamma_m\), \(\gamma_{\max}\), \(\gamma_c\), synchrotron
state, and cooling array are the same as in the log-\(\gamma\) \texttt{fullhide}
path.  The shell is divided into
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta R_i&=R_i-R_{i-1},\\
L_i&=\max\!\left[
N_{\rm S,min},
\min\!\left(N_{\rm S,max},
\left\lfloor\frac{\Delta R_i}{\Delta R_{\rm S}}\right\rfloor\right)
\right],\\
\Delta R&=\frac{\Delta R_i}{L_i}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(L_i\) is the number of radial substeps, \(\Delta R\) is the substep width,
and \(\Delta R_{\rm S}\), \(N_{\rm S,min}\), and \(N_{\rm S,max}\) are fixed
transport controls.

\subsubsection{SLC source and cooling speed}

Each semi-Lagrangian substep needs a source and a cooling drift.  For a substep
from \(R_L\) to \(R_R=R_L+\Delta R\), non-uniform media are sampled at
\begin{equation}
R_m=\frac{R_L+R_R}{2},\qquad n_m=n(R_m),\qquad n_R=n(R_R),
\end{equation}
while a uniform medium uses the shell density.  The injection source in
log-\(\gamma\) cell \(j\) is
\begin{equation}
Q_{x,j}^{\rm S}=
\frac{q_R(R_m,\Delta R)a_p(R_m)}{\Delta x_j}
\int_{x_{j-1/2}}^{x_{j+1/2}}
\psi_x(x)\,dx ,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\psi_x(x)&=\ln10\,\gamma_e^{1-p}\mathcal C_{\max}(\gamma_e),\\
\gamma_e&=10^x .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\psi_x\) is the nonthermal source shape per unit \(x\).  The cooling speed
at the cell face is the shell cooling array scaled to the midpoint density:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
f_{j+1/2,m}=
\frac{1}{\ln 10}
\left[
\frac{D_{j,m}+D_{j+1,m}}{2}+\frac{1}{R_m}
\right],\\
D_{j,m}=D_{j,i-1}\frac{n_m}{n_{i-1}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{SLC source-split remap}

The semi-Lagrangian update first adds half of the source, then remaps the
population along cooling characteristics, and then adds the remaining half of the
source.  The first half-step is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
N_j^{1/2}
&=\Pi_+\left(N_j^n+\frac{\Delta R}{2}Q_{x,j}^{\rm S}\right),\\
\Pi_+(X)\equiv\max(X,0).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The branch then builds a positive PPM reconstruction of \(N^{1/2}\) and its
prefix integral \(\mathcal{A}_{1/2}(x)\).  Arrival cell faces are traced backward
along the cooling characteristics:
\begin{equation}
x_{j-1/2}^{\rm dep}=
\mathcal{C}_x\!\left(x_{j-1/2}+\Delta R\,f_{j-1/2,m}\right),
\end{equation}
where \(\mathcal{C}_x\) keeps the departure point inside
\([x_{1/2},x_{N+1/2}]\).  The remap puts into cell \(j\) the old content between
the two departure faces:
\begin{equation}
N_j^{*}=
\frac{
\mathcal{A}_{1/2}(x_{j+1/2}^{\rm dep})
-\mathcal{A}_{1/2}(x_{j-1/2}^{\rm dep})}
{\Delta x_j}.
\end{equation}
The second source half-step gives the stored post-substep spectrum:
\begin{equation}
N_j^{n+1}=\Pi_+\left(N_j^{*}+
\frac{\Delta R}{2}Q_{x,j}^{\rm S}\right).
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{SLC output diagnostics}

After the last substep, the code converts the transported \(dN_e/dx\) cells to a
centered \(dN_e/d\gamma_e\) spectrum with the same conservative exponential
cell-shape conversion used by the other solvers.  The \(\nu_m\), \(\nu_c\), and
\(\nu_a\) arrays are shell-left diagnostics for the active output slots
\(1,\ldots,N_R-1\).  The self-absorption frequency \(\nu_a\) comes from the same
synchrotron optical-depth grid used for the emitted spectrum.

\subsection{Three-level log-\texorpdfstring{\(\gamma\)}{gamma} solver}

\subsubsection{t2g state and source}

The \texttt{t2g1\_1d} branch keeps the same log-\(\gamma\) state, source shape,
and shell-left diagnostics as \texttt{slc1\_1d}.  Its difference is the radial
discretization: it uses a three-level implicit upwind solve instead of a
semi-Lagrangian remap.  Each output shell uses the same fixed substep count
\(L_i\), and each substep advances the source radius to
\(R_\ell=R_{\ell-1}+\Delta R\).  The injection source is
\begin{equation}
Q_{x,j}^{\rm tg}=
\frac{q_R(R_\ell,\Delta R)a_p(R_\ell)}{\Delta x_j}
\int_{x_{j-1/2}}^{x_{j+1/2}}
\psi_x(x)\,dx ,
\end{equation}
where \(q_R\) and \(a_p\) are evaluated at \(R_\ell\).

\subsubsection{t2g cooling speed}

The three-level solver uses the same density-scaled cooling speed as
\texttt{slc1\_1d}, evaluated at \(R_\ell\):
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
f_{j+1/2,\ell}=
\frac{1}{\ln 10}
\left[
\frac{D_{j,\ell}+D_{j+1,\ell}}{2}+\frac{1}{R_\ell}
\right],\\
D_{j,\ell}=D_{j,i-1}\frac{n(R_\ell)}{n_{i-1}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The signed face step factor is
\begin{equation}
\lambda_{j+1/2}=-\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta x}f_{j+1/2,\ell}.
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{t2g startup sweep}

The first substeps do not yet have two previous spectra, so the branch starts
with backward Euler sweeps.  The diagonal coefficients are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
p_j^{(1)}&=1-\lambda_{j-1/2}\quad (j\ge2),\\
p_1^{(1)}&=p_2^{(1)} ,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and the source-loaded right-hand side and coupling coefficient are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
r_j^{(1)}=\frac{N_j^n+\Delta R\,Q_{x,j}^{\rm tg}}{p_j^{(1)}},
\\
c_j^{(1)}=\frac{2\lambda_{j+1/2}}{p_j^{(1)}+p_{j+1}^{(1)}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{t2g BDF2 sweep}

After two history states are available, the solver changes to the second-order
BDF2 radial discretization:
\begin{equation}
\frac{3N_j^{n+1}-4N_j^n+N_j^{n-1}}{2\Delta R}
+\mathcal{L}_j(N^{n+1})=Q_{x,j}^{\rm tg},
\end{equation}
where \(\mathcal{L}\) is the one-sided cooling-advection operator encoded by the
face coefficients.  The diagonal coefficients are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
p_j^{(3/2)}&=\frac{3}{2}-\lambda_{j-1/2}\quad (j\ge2),\\
p_1^{(3/2)}&=p_2^{(3/2)} ,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The source-loaded right-hand side and coupling coefficient are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
r_j^{(3/2)}=
\frac{2N_j^n-\frac{1}{2}N_j^{n-1}+\Delta R\,Q_{x,j}^{\rm tg}}
{p_j^{(3/2)}},\\
c_j^{(3/2)}=
\frac{2\lambda_{j+1/2}}{p_j^{(3/2)}+p_{j+1}^{(3/2)}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Both phases use the shared high-to-low-energy sweep in
Equation~\eqref{eq:triangular-cooling-sweep} after substituting the appropriate
\(p_j\), \(r_j\), and \(c_j\) coefficients.

\subsubsection{t2g output diagnostics}

After each accepted substep, the pair \((N^n,N^{n-1})\) is shifted forward.  At
the final substep of each output shell, the code converts \(dN_e/dx\) to a
centered \(dN_e/d\gamma_e\) spectrum with the same conservative exponential
cell-shape reconstruction used by the other log-\(\gamma\) drivers.

\subsection{WENO finite-volume solver}

\subsubsection{WENO state and local shock scales}

The \texttt{weno5\_1d} branch moves the electron spectrum with an explicit
finite-volume flux.  It uses the same uniform log-\(\gamma\) coordinate as the
other one-dimensional branches:
\begin{equation}
x=\log_{10}\gamma_e,\qquad
N_{x,j}(R)=\frac{1}{\Delta x}
\int_{x_{j-1/2}}^{x_{j+1/2}}\frac{dN_e}{dx}\,dx .
\end{equation}
Here \(N_{x,j}\) is the electron number per unit \(x\) in cell \(j\).  For the
output shell \(R_{i-1}\rightarrow R_i\), the local shock state is
\begin{equation}
R_{\rm loc}=R_{i-1},\qquad
\bar\Gamma_i=\frac{\Gamma_i+\Gamma_{i-1}}{2},
\end{equation}
where \(R_{\rm loc}\) is the shell-left radius and \(\bar\Gamma_i\) is the
shell-averaged Lorentz factor.  These quantities set the magnetic field, the
upper electron energy, the cooling break, and the shock speed:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
B'_i&=\left[32\pi\epsilon_B n(R_{\rm loc})m_pc^2
\bar\Gamma_i(\bar\Gamma_i-1)\right]^{1/2},\\
\gamma_{\max,i}&=\frac{3m_ec^2}{(8B'_ie^3)^{1/2}},\\
\gamma_{c,i}&=\frac{6\pi m_ec(1+z)}
{\sigma_TB_i'^2\bar\Gamma_i t_{{\rm obs},i}},\\
\beta_i&=(1-\bar\Gamma_i^{-2})^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The corresponding synchrotron cooling coefficient is
\begin{equation}
a_i\equiv \frac{\sigma_TB_i'^2}{6\pi m_ec^2\beta_i\bar\Gamma_i}.
\end{equation}
Here \(a_i\gamma_e\) is the synchrotron energy drift per unit radius.  The local
cooling array stores the total radiative drift through
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\gamma_j}{dR}=-D_j\gamma_j .
\end{equation}
The explicit WENO update uses this drift as a face velocity.

\subsubsection{WENO conservative equation and substeps}

The WENO update solves the conservative log-energy equation with an explicit
face flux.  In continuum form,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{\partial N_x}{\partial R}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(v_xN_x\right)+Q_x,\\
v_{x,j+1/2}
&=
\frac{(D_j+D_{j+1})/2+R^{-1}}{\ln10},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(Q_x\) is the injected source per unit radius and \(v_x\) is the drift in
the log-energy coordinate.  The outer high-energy face is extrapolated from the
last interior cooling face.  The first trial radial step is set by the largest
synchrotron and adiabatic drift,
\begin{equation}
\Delta R_{W,0}=
\frac{\eta_W}
{a_i\gamma_{\max,i}+\eta_{\rm ad}/(R_i+R_{i-1})},
\end{equation}
where \(\eta_W\) and \(\eta_{\rm ad}\) are fixed WENO step controls.  The final
substep count also enforces an explicit flux limit.  With
\(v_W=\max_j|v_{x,j+1/2}|\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_i=\max\left[
\left\lfloor\frac{R_i-R_{i-1}}{\Delta R_{W,0}}\right\rfloor+N_W^+,\,
\left\lceil
\frac{(R_i-R_{i-1})v_W}
{\eta_{\rm CFL,W}\,\Delta x}
\right\rceil
\right],\\
\Delta R=\frac{R_i-R_{i-1}}{L_i}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(N_W^+\) is the fixed extra-step margin and \(\eta_{\rm CFL,W}\) is the
WENO flux step control.

\subsubsection{WENO finite-shell source}

The explicit WENO source uses the same finite-shell swept-particle count as the
other electron solvers.  Inside a substep, the source radius is first advanced to
\(R_s+\Delta R\), and the density is sampled at that radius.  The injected
cell-average source is
\begin{equation}
Q_{x,j}^{\rm W}=
\frac{q_R(R_s,\Delta R)a_{p,i}}{\Delta x_j}
\int_{x_{j-1/2}}^{x_{j+1/2}}
\psi_x(x)\,dx ,
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
q_R(R_s,\Delta R)&\equiv
n(R_s)\frac{V(R_s+\Delta R)-V(R_s)}{\Delta R},\\
a_{p,i}&\equiv
f_e\frac{1-p}{\gamma_{\max,i}^{1-p}-\gamma_{m,i}^{1-p}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(q_R\) is the number of swept upstream electrons per unit radius, and
\(a_{p,i}\) normalizes the injected power law.  The volume is
\(V(R)=4\pi R^3/3\).  For this explicit branch, \(\gamma_m\) and
\(\gamma_{\max}\) stay fixed at the shell-level shock values during the output
shell.

\subsubsection{WENO-Z interface flux}

The WENO-Z step reconstructs the face flux from neighboring cell fluxes.  The
flux variable is \(F=v_xN_x\).  Because the equation contains
\(+\partial F/\partial x\), positive \(v_x\) moves particles toward lower \(x\).
Thus positive \(v_x\) uses the right-biased stencil, and negative \(v_x\) uses
the left-biased stencil.  For a left-biased interface, let \(f_m=F_{i+m}\).  The
three candidate fifth-order fluxes are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\hat f_0&=\frac{1}{3}f_{-2}-\frac{7}{6}f_{-1}+\frac{11}{6}f_0,\\
\hat f_1&=-\frac{1}{6}f_{-1}+\frac{5}{6}f_0+\frac{1}{3}f_1,\\
\hat f_2&=\frac{1}{3}f_0+\frac{5}{6}f_1-\frac{1}{6}f_2 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The Jiang--Shu smoothness indicators measure how smooth each candidate stencil is:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\beta_0&=\frac{13}{12}(f_{-2}-2f_{-1}+f_0)^2
+\frac{1}{4}(f_{-2}-4f_{-1}+3f_0)^2,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\beta_1&=\frac{13}{12}(f_{-1}-2f_0+f_1)^2
+\frac{1}{4}(f_1-f_{-1})^2,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\beta_2&=\frac{13}{12}(f_0-2f_1+f_2)^2
+\frac{1}{4}(3f_0-4f_1+f_2)^2 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
With linear weights \(d=(1/10,3/5,3/10)\), the nonlinear WENO-Z weights are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\tau_5&=|\beta_0-\beta_2|,\\
\alpha_k&=d_k\left[1+
\left(\frac{\tau_5}{\beta_k+\epsilon_W}\right)^2\right],
\\
\omega_k&=\frac{\alpha_k}{\sum_m\alpha_m}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\epsilon_W\) is the smoothness-weight floor of the WENO-Z kernel.  The
left-biased face flux is
\begin{equation}
F_{i+1/2}^{-}=\sum_{k=0}^{2}\omega_k\hat f_k .
\end{equation}
The right-biased flux used for \(v_x>0\) is the mirror of the same formula over
\((f_{i-1},f_i,f_{i+1},f_{i+2},f_{i+3})\).  Constant ghost cells at both energy
ends provide the five-point WENO stencil; they are numerical boundary states and
are not returned as physical electron bins.

\subsubsection{WENO Runge--Kutta update}

The explicit radial step applies the WENO flux in a third-order
strong-stability-preserving Runge--Kutta update.  Define
\begin{equation}
\lambda_W=\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta x},
\qquad
\mathcal{D}_j(N)=F_{j+1/2}(N)-F_{j-1/2}(N),
\end{equation}
where \(\mathcal{D}_j\) is the net flux entering cell \(j\).  The three stages
are
\begin{equation}
N_j^{(1)}=N_j^n+\lambda_W\mathcal{D}_j(N^n)+\Delta R\,Q_j,
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
N_j^{(2)}=\frac{3}{4}N_j^n+
\frac{1}{4}\left[N_j^{(1)}+
\lambda_W\mathcal{D}_j(N^{(1)})\right]
+\frac{1}{4}\Delta R\,Q_j,
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
N_j^{n+1}=\frac{1}{3}N_j^n+
\frac{2}{3}\left[N_j^{(2)}+
\lambda_W\mathcal{D}_j(N^{(2)})\right]
+\frac{2}{3}\Delta R\,Q_j .
\end{equation}
The same substep source \(Q_j\) is used in all three stages.

\subsubsection{WENO output diagnostics}

After each substep, the stored conservative spectrum is projected to
\(N_x\ge0\).  This projection acts only on the transported electron cell
averages.  It does not smooth a light curve.  At the last substep in an output
shell, the same exponential cell-shape reconstruction used by the log-\(\gamma\)
solvers gives
\begin{equation}
\left.\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma_e}\right|_j
=\frac{N_{x,j}^{\rm exp}}{\gamma_j\ln10}.
\end{equation}
Here \(N_{x,j}^{\rm exp}\) is the reconstructed log-energy cell content.  The
radiation arrays written during shell preparation are shell-left fields used to
build the cooling seed.  The final output point has no following shell, so the
WENO driver refreshes \(L_\nu^{\rm syn}\) and \(n_\nu^{\rm syn}\) from the final
transported electron spectrum and the final dynamics-grid field.

\subsection{Direct characteristic-integration solver}

\subsubsection{Characteristic state}

The \texttt{charint\_1d} solver follows electron cooling by tracing
characteristics backward in energy.  It evolves the same conservative
log-\(\gamma\) cell averages as the other one-dimensional solvers:
\begin{equation}
N_{x,j}(R)=\frac{1}{\Delta x_j}
\int_{x_{j-1/2}}^{x_{j+1/2}}\frac{dN_e}{dx}\,dx,
\qquad x=\log_{10}\gamma_e,
\end{equation}
where \(N_{x,j}\) is the stored electron content per unit \(x\).  A new cell gets
the old electron content from the interval reached by the backward
characteristic.

\subsubsection{Characteristic local shock scales}

Each output shell first supplies the local shock state.  For
\(R_{i-1}\rightarrow R_i\), the solver uses
\begin{equation}
R_{\rm loc}=R_{i-1},\qquad
\Gamma_{\rm sh}=\frac{\Gamma_i+\Gamma_{i-1}}{2},\qquad
n_{\rm ext}=n(R_{\rm loc}),
\end{equation}
where \(R_{\rm loc}\) is the shell-left radius and \(n_{\rm ext}\) is the upstream
density.  The magnetic field and maximum electron Lorentz factor are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
B'&=\left[32\pi\epsilon_B n_{\rm ext}m_pc^2
\Gamma_{\rm sh}(\Gamma_{\rm sh}-1)\right]^{1/2},\\
\gamma_{\max}&=\frac{3m_ec^2}{(8B'e^3)^{1/2}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and the cooling Lorentz factor and shock speed are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_c&=\frac{6\pi m_ec(1+z)}
{\sigma_TB'^2\Gamma_{\rm sh}t_{{\rm obs},i}},\\
\beta_{\rm sh}&=(1-\Gamma_{\rm sh}^{-2})^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The minimum injection Lorentz factor uses the same \(\gamma_m\) solution as the
other forward-shock branches.  Its available energy per accelerated electron is
\begin{equation}
\frac{\epsilon_e}{f_e}\frac{m_p}{m_e}(\Gamma_{\rm sh}-1)
\end{equation}
in units of \(m_ec^2\).  These shell quantities set the diagnostics and the first
trial substep.  In a non-uniform density profile, the same \(B'\),
\(\gamma_{\max}\), and \(\gamma_m\) formulae are re-evaluated at
\begin{equation}
R_m=\frac{R_i+R_{i-1}}{2},
\qquad n_m=n(R_m) .
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Characteristic radial substeps}

The characteristic map is accurate only if the radial lag is short enough to
resolve cooling.  Without adaptive substeps, the step length is set by the shorter
of the high-energy synchrotron and adiabatic cooling lengths:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
a_{\rm syn}&=\frac{\sigma_TB'^2}{6\pi m_ec^2\beta_{\rm sh}\Gamma_{\rm sh}},\\
\Delta R_{\rm ch}&=\frac{\eta_{\rm CFL}}
{a_{\rm syn}\gamma_{\max}+\eta_{\rm ad}/R_m},\\
N_i^{\rm ch}&=\left\lceil\frac{R_i-R_{i-1}}{\Delta R_{\rm ch}}\right\rceil,\\
L_i&=\max\left[
N_{\rm char,min},
\min\left(N_{\rm char,max},N_i^{\rm ch}\right)
\right],\\
\Delta R&=\frac{R_i-R_{i-1}}{L_i}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(a_{\rm syn}\gamma_{\max}\) estimates the fastest synchrotron drift,
\(\eta_{\rm ad}/R_m\) estimates the adiabatic drift, and \(L_i\) is limited by
the declared characteristic substep bounds.

\subsubsection{Characteristic adaptive acceptance}

Adaptive characteristic substeps choose the radial lag from a local density and
radius-change measure.  The allowed trial interval is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta R_{\min}&=\frac{R_i-R_{i-1}}{N_{\rm char,max}},\\
\Delta R_{\max}&=\frac{R_i-R_{i-1}}{N_{\rm char,min}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and a trial step is accepted when
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_R\le\eta_{\rm tol}\epsilon_{\rm sub}
\end{equation}
or when the lower bound has been reached.  For a trial beginning at \(R_s\), a
uniform medium uses
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_R=\Delta R/R_s,
\end{equation}
whereas density jumps use
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_R=\max\left[
\frac{\Delta R}{R_s},
\frac{|n(R_s+\Delta R)-n(R_s)|}{n(R_s)}
\right].
\end{equation}
This criterion selects the radial quadrature of the characteristic map.  It does
not change the density profile.

\subsubsection{Characteristic finite-shell source}

The characteristic source is normalized by the finite shell swept at the substep
radius.  At evaluation radius \(R_s\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
n_s&\equiv n(R_s),\\
q_R(R_s,\Delta R)&=
n_s\frac{V(R_s+\Delta R)-V(R_s)}{\Delta R},\\
a_p(R_s)&=f_e\frac{1-p}{\gamma_{\max}^{1-p}(R_s)-\gamma_m^{1-p}(R_s)} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(q_R\) is the number of swept upstream electrons per unit radius,
\(a_p\) normalizes the accelerated power law, and \(V(R)=4\pi R^3/3\).  The
source placed on the \(x\) grid is
\begin{equation}
Q_{x,j}^{\rm C}=\frac{q_Ra_p}{\Delta x_j}
\int_{x_{j-1/2}}^{x_{j+1/2}}
\psi_{\rm C}(x)\,dx,
\end{equation}
where \(q_R=q_R(R_s,\Delta R)\), \(a_p=a_p(R_s)\), and
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\psi_{\rm C}(x)&=
\begin{cases}
\ln10\,\gamma_e^{1-p}\mathcal C_{\max}(\gamma_e),
&\gamma_e\ge\gamma_m,\\
0,&\gamma_e<\gamma_m,
\end{cases}\\
\gamma_e&=10^x .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The high-energy cutoff is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma_e)=
\begin{cases}
1, & \gamma_e\le\gamma_{\max},\\
\exp(1-\gamma_e/\gamma_{\max}), & \gamma_e>\gamma_{\max}.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Thus the source is zero below \(\gamma_m\), follows \(\gamma_e^{-p}\) above
\(\gamma_m\), and turns over smoothly above \(\gamma_{\max}\).

\subsubsection{Characteristic cooling map}

The characteristic map is simple in \(u=1/\gamma_e\).  In the synchrotron-only
branch, one substep obeys
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\gamma_e}{dR}=-a_{\rm syn}\gamma_e^2-b\gamma_e,
\qquad b=R_s^{-1}.
\end{equation}
This gives
\begin{equation}
\frac{du}{dR}=a_{\rm syn}+bu .
\end{equation}
For a face that arrives at \(u\) after radial lag \(\ell\), the departure point is
\begin{equation}
u_{\rm back}=
\begin{cases}
u-a_{\rm syn}\ell, & b=0,\\
(u+a_{\rm syn}/b)e^{-b\ell}-a_{\rm syn}/b, & {\rm otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
The general cooling branch first computes the loss coefficients \(D_j\) from
synchrotron, inverse-Compton, and synchrotron-self-absorption terms.  In a
non-uniform external medium, the shell loss array is rescaled by
\(n_s/n_{\rm ext}\) before the characteristic map is built.  With
\(u_j=1/\gamma_{e,j}\), the code reconstructs
\begin{equation}
w(u)=uD(u)
\end{equation}
inside each \(u\) cell as \(w(u)=A_u+S_u u\).  Adding adiabatic cooling gives
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{du}{dR}&=A_u+(S_u+b_{\rm ad})u,\\
b_{\rm ad}&=R_s^{-1}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The same analytic backward map is applied inside the current \(u\) cell.  If the
traced point reaches a cell edge, the remaining radial lag continues in the
adjacent cell with the new coefficients.

\subsubsection{Characteristic conservative remap}

The remap conserves electron number by integrating between the two traced faces.
Let \(M_N(x)\) be the prefix integral of the positive PPM reconstruction of
\(N_x\), and let \(M_Q(x)\) be the exponential-source prefix integral of
\(Q_x^{\rm C}\).  For arrival cell \(j\), define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
{\cal I}_N(j,\ell)&=
\frac{M_N[x_{j+1/2}^{\rm back}(\ell)]
-M_N[x_{j-1/2}^{\rm back}(\ell)]}{\Delta x_j},\\
{\cal I}_Q(j,\ell)&=
\frac{M_Q[x_{j+1/2}^{\rm back}(\ell)]
-M_Q[x_{j-1/2}^{\rm back}(\ell)]}{\Delta x_j}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The old electrons use the full radial lag \(\Delta R\).  The source is integrated
along the same characteristic with Gauss points:
\begin{equation}
N_{x,j}^{n+1}={\cal I}_N(j,\Delta R)
+\Delta R\sum_{q=1}^{N_{\rm G}^{\rm C}}w_q{\cal I}_Q(j,s_q\Delta R).
\end{equation}
The quadrature order is \(N_{\rm G}^{\rm C}\).  The clustered source coordinate is
\begin{equation}
s_q=t_q^{q_{\rm C}},\qquad
w_q=q_{\rm C}W_qt_q^{q_{\rm C}-1},
\end{equation}
where \(q_{\rm C}\) is the declared source-time clustering index and
\(t_q,W_q\) are the Gauss--Legendre nodes and weights on \([0,1]\).  This is
the source integral along the characteristic.

\subsubsection{Characteristic output diagnostics}

After each output shell, the solver converts the stored log-energy cells back to
a centered physical spectrum:
\begin{equation}
\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma_e}\bigg|_j
=\frac{N_{x,j}^{\rm exp}}{\gamma_j\ln 10}.
\end{equation}
Here \(N_{x,j}^{\rm exp}\) is the local exponential reconstruction of
\(dN_e/dx\).  The reported break frequencies are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\nu_m=\frac{3eB'\gamma_m^2}
{4\pi m_ec\Gamma_{\rm sh}(1-\beta_{\rm sh})(1+z)},\\
\nu_c=\frac{3eB'\gamma_c^2}
{4\pi m_ec\Gamma_{\rm sh}(1-\beta_{\rm sh})(1+z)}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The self-absorption diagnostic comes from the final synchrotron optical-depth
grid:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\tau_\nu(\nu_a)&=1,\\
\nu_a^{\rm obs}&=\frac{\nu_a}
{\Gamma_{\rm sh}(1-\beta_{\rm sh})(1+z)}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Discontinuous-Galerkin solver}

\subsubsection{DG state and nodal representation}

The \texttt{dg\_1d} solver represents the electron spectrum with local
polynomials instead of one value per cell.  It uses the same coordinate \(y\) as
the finite-volume branch and evolves the conservative density
\begin{equation}
N_y(y,R)=\frac{dN_e}{dy}
=\frac{dN_e}{dx_g}\frac{dx_g}{dy}.
\end{equation}
Here \(x_g\) is the log-energy coordinate used for radiation diagnostics.  On an
element \(K_a=[y_{a,L},y_{a,R}]\), we map position to
\(r\in[-1,1]\) through \(y=y_a+\Delta y_ar/2\).  The local polynomial is
\begin{equation}
N_y^h(y,R)\big|_{K_a}=
\sum_{\ell=0}^{p_{\rm DG}}N_{a\ell}(R)L_\ell(r),
\end{equation}
where \(L_\ell\) are Lagrange polynomials on Legendre--Gauss--Lobatto nodes and
\(p_{\rm DG}\) is the DG polynomial order.  At each shell, the mesh is rebuilt
around the active break positions \(\log_{10}\gamma_m\),
\(\log_{10}\gamma_c\), and \(\log_{10}\gamma_{\max}\).  Each active interval is
split into \(N_{\rm seg}\) elements.  Breaks outside the electron grid, or closer
than the declared mesh-separation tolerance, are not used as element boundaries.

\subsubsection{DG shell scales and active support}

The DG solver needs a fixed diagnostic grid and a moving active grid.  The fixed
diagnostic grid is the shared four-velocity grid with scale \(\gamma_{\rm sc}\).
Its upper edge is chosen from a high-energy coverage estimate:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{\max}^{\rm grid}
&=\eta_{\rm tail}
\frac{3m_ec^2}{(8B'_{\rm ref}e^3)^{1/2}},\\
B'_{\rm ref}&=(8\pi\epsilon_B e'_{\rm ref})^{1/2},\\
e'_{\rm ref}
&=\kappa_{\rm FS}\Gamma_{\rm end}(\Gamma_{\rm end}-1)n(R_1)m_pc^2,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(\Gamma_{\rm end}\) is the last dynamics-grid Lorentz factor,
\(\eta_{\rm tail}\) sets the high-energy coverage, and \(\kappa_{\rm FS}\) is the
forward-shock energy-density factor used for this diagnostic grid estimate.  At
each output shell, the solver uses
\begin{equation}
\bar\Gamma_i=\frac{\Gamma_i+\Gamma_{i-1}}{2}
\end{equation}
and computes the current shell break values:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{\max,i}&=\frac{3m_ec^2}{(8B_i'e^3)^{1/2}},\\
\gamma_{m,i}&=\gamma_m\!\left[
\frac{\epsilon_e}{f_e}\frac{m_p}{m_e}(\bar\Gamma_i-\!1),
\gamma_{\max,i},p\right],\\
\gamma_{c,i}&=\frac{6\pi m_ec(1+z)}
{\sigma_TB_i'^2\bar\Gamma_i t_{{\rm obs},i}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
These values set the local break-frequency diagnostics and the injection source
for each DG substep.

\subsubsection{DG active-support remesh}

The active DG mesh tracks the part of the electron spectrum that contains source
or surviving high-energy electrons.  For a trial upper cutoff \(\gamma_u\), the
source support endpoint is
\begin{equation}
x_{\rm src}^{\max}=
\log_{10}\left[\min(\gamma_{\max}^{\rm grid},\eta_{\rm tail}\gamma_u)\right],
\end{equation}
and the active high-energy edge is kept if the old DG state still carries a
significant high-energy moment:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{\mathcal{M}_2(x_{\rm src}^{\max})}{\mathcal{M}_2^{\rm tot}}
&>\epsilon_{\rm tail},\\
\mathcal{M}_2(x_0)&\equiv
\int_{\gamma_e\ge10^{x_0}}N_y\gamma_e^2\,dy,\\
\mathcal{M}_2^{\rm tot}&\equiv\int N_y\gamma_e^2\,dy .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\mathcal{M}_2\) is a high-energy electron moment, and
\(\epsilon_{\rm tail}\) is the active-tail threshold.  If a substep source extends
beyond the current active DG edge, the mesh is rebuilt with the current
\(\gamma_{m,i}\), \(\gamma_{c,i}\), and \(\gamma_{\max,i}\).

\subsubsection{DG radial substeps and density jumps}

The DG radial grid is refined near density jumps.  Away from jumps, the regular
substep is
\begin{equation}
\Delta R_{\rm DG}=\frac{R_i-R_{i-1}}{N_{\rm DG}}.
\end{equation}
For a Gaussian density jump with width \(\sigma_j=w_jR_j\), candidate steps are
cut at the marker radii
\begin{equation}
R_j+\sigma_j\mathcal{Z}_{\rm J},
\end{equation}
and inside the active jump window the step also obeys
\begin{equation}
\Delta R\le \frac{\sigma_j}{N_{\rm J}}.
\end{equation}
The final limiter uses the local density-gradient scale.  With jump enhancement
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
g_n(R)&=1+\sum_j(f_j-1)
\exp\left[-\frac{(R-R_j)^2}{2\sigma_j^2}\right],\\
\Delta R&\le \frac{\eta_{\nabla n}}{|d\ln g_n/dR|}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The parameters \(N_{\rm DG}\), \(\mathcal Z_{\rm J}\), \(N_{\rm J}\), and
\(\eta_{\nabla n}\) are declared radial-step controls for this branch.

\subsubsection{DG finite-shell source and cooling}

The DG source is normalized so that its polynomial representation carries the
same injected electron number as the finite-cell source.  At a DG substep radius
\(R_s\), the solver re-evaluates \(n(R_s)\), \(B'\), \(\gamma_{\max}\), and
\(\gamma_m\) unless the medium is strictly uniform.  It first builds a
unit-amplitude source template \(\widetilde Q_y^{\rm D}\), then rescales it so
that its DG integral matches the conservative source integral in the same
\(y\) measure:
\begin{equation}
\widetilde Q_y^{\rm D}\leftarrow
\widetilde Q_y^{\rm D}
\frac{\sum_j \bar Q_{y,j}\Delta y_j}
{\int \widetilde Q_y^{\rm D}\,dy}.
\end{equation}
The source entering the implicit DG step is
\begin{equation}
Q_y^{\rm D}(R_s)=
q_R(R_s,\Delta R)a_p(R_s)\,\widetilde Q_y^{\rm D}.
\end{equation}
Here \(q_R\) is the finite-shell swept-particle source and \(a_p\) is the
power-law normalization.  The cooling array is computed once from the shell
radiation field.  For a non-uniform medium, it is scaled locally as
\begin{equation}
D_j(R_s)=
D_j(R_{i-1})\frac{n(R_s)}{n(R_{i-1})}.
\end{equation}
The DG substep solves
\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial N_y}{\partial R}
+\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
\left[
-\frac{D(R_s)+R_s^{-1}}
{\ln 10\,dx_g/dy}N_y
\right]
=Q_y^{\rm D}(R_s).
\end{equation}
The velocity term contains both radiative cooling and the adiabatic drift
\(R_s^{-1}\).

\subsubsection{DG output projection}

After each output radius, the moving DG polynomial is projected back to the fixed
diagnostic grid.  The projection uses cell-overlap quadrature:
\begin{equation}
\bar N_{y,j}^{\rm out}=
\frac{1}{\Delta y_j}
\sum_a\int_{[y_{j-1/2},y_{j+1/2}]\cap K_a}
N_y^h(y)\,dy .
\end{equation}
The reported electron spectrum is then
\begin{equation}
\left.\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma_e}\right|_j=
\frac{N_{y,j}^{\rm exp}}
{\gamma_j\ln 10\,(dx_g/dy)_j},
\end{equation}
where \(N_{y,j}^{\rm exp}\) is the center value of the local exponential
reconstruction of the fixed-grid cell average.  The solver requires both the DG
integral and the projected fixed-grid integral to remain positive:
\begin{equation}
\int N_y^h\,dy>0,\qquad
\sum_j\frac{dN_e}{d\gamma_e}\bigg|_j
\gamma_j\ln 10\,\Delta x_j>0,
\end{equation}
as state-domain conditions for the DG output.  The final output radius is
refreshed through the same synchrotron optical-depth grid used for \(\nu_a\), so
the returned \(\nu_m\), \(\nu_c\), and \(\nu_a\) arrays describe the final
electron spectrum.

\subsubsection{DG source projection}

DG source projection converts a log-energy source into the local polynomial
basis.  For a source written in log-energy form \(Q_{x_g}\), the density in the
DG coordinate is
\begin{equation}
Q_y(y)=Q_{x_g}[x_g(y)]\frac{dx_g}{dy}.
\end{equation}
The source coefficients in element \(a\) are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\widehat Q_{am}&=
\frac{2m+1}{\Delta y_a}
\int_{y_{a,L}}^{y_{a,R}}Q_y(y)P_m[r(y)]\,dy,\\
Q_{a,i}&=\sum_{m=0}^{p_{\rm DG}}\widehat Q_{am}P_m(r_i),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(P_m\) are Legendre polynomials and the integral is evaluated by
Gauss--Legendre quadrature.  The reverse-shock kinetic source uses the normalized
shape
\begin{equation}
\Phi_y^{\rm K}=A_{\rm K}\gamma_e\ln 10\,
(\gamma_e-1)^{-p}\mathcal{C}_{\max}(\gamma_e)
\frac{dx_g}{dy}\,\Theta(\gamma_e-\gamma_m),
\end{equation}
where \(A_{\rm K}\) is fixed by the requested shell-integrated kinetic-source
content.  The forward source uses the shared cutoff power-law profile and is
scaled so that the DG source carries the same shell-integrated injected
electron number as the edge-integrated source.

\subsubsection{DG implicit transport solve}

The implicit DG step moves electrons through the \(y\) coordinate by cooling.  In
that coordinate, the equation is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{\partial N_y}{\partial R}
+\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(v_yN_y\right)&=Q_y,\\
v_y&=-\frac{D_{\rm rad}+D_{\rm ad}}
{\ln 10\,dx_g/dy}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(D_{\rm rad}\) is the radiative loss coefficient and \(D_{\rm ad}\) is the
adiabatic loss coefficient.  The forward path uses \(D_{\rm ad}=R^{-1}\).  The
reverse path uses either \(R^{-1}\) before crossing or the stored post-crossing
volume loss \((d\ln V_3/dR)/3\).  In the public \texttt{dg\_1d} path, this local
advection problem is advanced with a fully implicit nodal DG step.  In each
element, the solver assembles a \((p_{\rm DG}+1)\times(p_{\rm DG}+1)\) block,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
A_{a,ij}&=\delta_{ij}
-\Delta R
\frac{2}{\Delta y_a}
\frac{w_j}{w_i}D_{ji}v_{y,j},\\
\sum_j A_{a,ij}N_{a,j}^{n+1}
&=N_{a,i}^{n}+\Delta R\,Q_{a,i}
+\mathcal{F}_{a,i}^{\rm up}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(D_{ji}=\left.dL_i/dr\right|_{r_j}\), \(w_i\) are the LGL quadrature
weights, and \(\mathcal{F}_{a,i}^{\rm up}\) is the upwind boundary
contribution.  Radiative and adiabatic losses usually give \(v_y<0\).  The
element blocks are then solved from high energy to low energy, and the right-face
state from the upstream element enters the next block.  If any node has
\(v_y>0\), the same DG flux matrix is assembled as one global dense implicit
system with upwind face states on both sides.  In four-velocity DG coordinates,
the first low-energy face is closed.  Cooled particles that trace below the
finite grid are therefore kept in the first element.  Density jumps activate the
extra radial substeps described above, so one radial solve does not cross a sharp
density change.

\subsubsection{DG conservative remap and positivity}

Mesh changes must conserve the number of electrons.  If a new coordinate
\(y_{\rm n}\) and an old coordinate \(y_{\rm o}\) represent the same
\(\gamma_e\), the remapped density is
\begin{equation}
N_{\rm n}(y_{\rm n})=
N_{\rm o}(y_{\rm o})
\frac{dy_{\rm o}}{dy_{\rm n}}
=
N_{\rm o}(y_{\rm o})
\frac{(dx_g/dy)_{\rm n}}{(dx_g/dy)_{\rm o}},
\end{equation}
and a final scalar rescaling enforces equality of the total electron number,
\begin{equation}
N_{\rm tot}=\sum_a\frac{\Delta y_a}{2}
\sum_i w_iN_{a,i}.
\end{equation}
After a projection or radial step, the element-local positivity limiter keeps the
element average
\begin{equation}
\bar N_a=\frac{1}{2}\sum_iw_iN_{a,i}
\end{equation}
fixed and replaces nodal values by
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
N_{a,i}&\leftarrow
\bar N_a+\theta_a(N_{a,i}-\bar N_a),\\
\theta_a&=\min\left[1,\frac{\bar N_a}{\bar N_a-N_{a,\min}}\right],
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
when \(N_{a,\min}<0<\bar N_a\).  This operation keeps the element average fixed.
The optional Jackson or Fejer modal filter multiplies only high-order modal
coefficients in flagged DG elements.  It is a positivity operation on the DG
representation, not a light-curve smoothing step.

\subsubsection{DG characteristic projector}

The DG kernel also contains a characteristic projector for conservative remaps.
For an affine loss law
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\gamma_e}{dR}=-a_{\rm rad}\gamma_e^2-b_{\rm ad}\gamma_e,
\end{equation}
define \(\eta_b=\exp(-b_{\rm ad}\Delta R)\) and
\(\zeta_b=1-\eta_b\).  The forward cooling map over radial lag \(\Delta R\) is
\begin{equation}
\gamma_e(R+\Delta R)=
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle \frac{\gamma_e(R)}
{1+a_{\rm rad}\gamma_e(R)\Delta R}, &
b_{\rm ad}=0,\\[1.1ex]
\displaystyle
\frac{\gamma_e(R)\eta_b}
{1+(a_{\rm rad}/b_{\rm ad})\gamma_e(R)
\zeta_b}, & {\rm otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
The inverse map gives the departure energy for an electron observed at
\(\gamma_{\rm now}\):
\begin{equation}
\gamma_{\rm back}=
\begin{cases}
\displaystyle \frac{\gamma_{\rm now}}
{1-a_{\rm rad}\gamma_{\rm now}\Delta R}, &
b_{\rm ad}=0,\\[1.1ex]
\displaystyle \frac{\gamma_{\rm now}}
{\eta_b-(a_{\rm rad}/b_{\rm ad})\zeta_b\gamma_{\rm now}}, &
{\rm otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
The inverse map projects upstream DG polynomials onto the arrival element with
Gauss--Legendre quadrature and Legendre modal moments.  Any finite-grid content
whose characteristic exits below the first four-velocity element is accumulated
as \(M_{\rm low}\) and written back as a uniform first-element density
\(M_{\rm low}/\Delta y_1\).  This projector is part of the DG transport kernel
and is used for conservative remapping and diagnostic characteristic advances.
The public \texttt{dg\_1d} forward light-curve path uses the fully implicit block
solve described above for the shell-local transport step.

\subsection{Cooling assembler}

\subsubsection{Radial loss state}

The cooling assembler gives each electron node a radial energy-loss rate.  It
combines synchrotron cooling, inverse-Compton cooling, and
synchrotron-self-absorption heating before the transport solver adds adiabatic
drift.  With \(x=\log_{10}\gamma_e\), the stored radial loss coefficient is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d\gamma_i}{dR}&=-D_i\gamma_i,\\
v_{x,i+1/2}
&=\frac{(D_i+D_{i+1})/2+R^{-1}}{\ln 10},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(D_i>0\) means cooling and positive \(v_x\) moves the distribution toward
lower \(\gamma_e\).  Synchrotron cooling uses the physical coefficient
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
a_{\rm syn}&=\frac{\sigma_T B'^2}{6\pi m_e c^2\,\beta\Gamma},\\
\left.\frac{d\gamma_i}{dR}\right|_{\rm syn}
&=-a_{\rm syn}\gamma_i^2 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(B'\) is the comoving magnetic field and \(\beta\Gamma c\) converts
comoving cooling rates to radial rates.

\subsubsection{Cooling-branch assembly}

The assembler has four supported cooling branches.  Photon-mediated terms use
the conversion \(dt'/dR=(\beta\Gamma c)^{-1}\).  Let \(A_i^{\rm IC}\) be the
tabulated inverse-Compton and Klein--Nishina coefficient from the seed photon
field, \(A_i^{\rm abs}\) be the synchrotron-self-absorption heating coefficient,
and \(Y_i\) be an analytic Compton factor.  The stored loss coefficient is
\begin{equation}
D_i=
\begin{cases}
a_{\rm syn}\gamma_i, & \mathrm{synchrotron\ only},\\[0.6ex]
\left[a_{\rm syn}+
\dfrac{A_i^{\rm IC}-A_i^{\rm abs}}{\beta\Gamma c}
\right]\gamma_i, & \mathrm{tabulated\ IC/KN},\\[2ex]
\left[a_{\rm syn}(1+Y_i^{\rm N})
-\dfrac{A_i^{\rm abs}}{\beta\Gamma c}
\right]\gamma_i, & \mathrm{Nakar},\\[2ex]
\left[a_{\rm syn}(1+Y_i^{\rm F})
-\dfrac{A_i^{\rm abs}}{\beta\Gamma c}
\right]\gamma_i, & \mathrm{Fan}.
\end{cases}
\label{eq:electron-forward-cooling-assembly}
\end{equation}
The tabulated branch uses the local photon field directly.  The Nakar and Fan
branches replace that photon integral by analytic estimates of the Compton
factor.

\subsection{Nakar analytic Compton closure}

The Nakar branch estimates inverse-Compton cooling from the synchrotron
luminosity below the Klein--Nishina threshold.  For electron node \(i\), the
threshold frequency and sub-threshold luminosity are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\hat\nu_i&=\frac{m_e c^2}{h\gamma_i},\\
L_i^{<}&=
\int_{\nu_{\min}}^{\min(\hat\nu_i,\nu_{\max})}
L_\nu^{\rm syn}\,d\nu,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
following the Nakar-style prescription \citep{NakarAndoSari2009}.  The assembler
then converts this luminosity to
\begin{equation}
Y_i^{\rm N}=
\frac{L_i^{<}}
{(4\pi R^2 c)(4\Gamma^2 n m_p c^2)},
\end{equation}
where the denominator is the shocked-shell energy flux scale.  The seed-frequency
integral uses the fixed logarithmic Gauss order \(N_{\rm G}\) in every frequency
cell.

\subsection{Fan Klein--Nishina Compton closure}

The Fan branch estimates the Klein--Nishina correction from the fraction of
synchrotron photons below the electron-dependent threshold
\citep{FanPiran2008}.  For each electron node,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\eta_{\rm rad}&=\min\left[1,\left(\frac{\gamma_m}{\gamma_c}\right)^{p-2}\right],\\
\hat\gamma_i&=
\left(\frac{m_e c^2}{hC_\nu B'\gamma_i}\right)^{1/2},\\
\gamma_M&=\gamma_{\max},\\
C_\nu&=\frac{3e}{4\pi m_e c}.
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:synch-frequency-coeff}
\end{equation}
Here \(\eta_{\rm rad}\) is the radiative electron-energy fraction,
\(\hat\gamma_i\) is the electron Lorentz factor whose synchrotron photons enter
the Klein--Nishina threshold for node \(i\), and \(C_\nu B'\gamma^2\) is the
synchrotron characteristic frequency.  The analytic Compton factor is the
positive solution of
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Xi_i&=\eta_{\rm rad}\eta_i^{\rm KN}\frac{\epsilon_e}{\epsilon_B},\\
Y_i^{\rm F}(1+Y_i^{\rm F})&=\Xi_i,\\
Y_i^{\rm F}=
\frac{-1+\sqrt{1+4\Xi_i}}{2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The remaining quantity is \(\eta_i^{\rm KN}\), the photon-energy fraction below
the Klein--Nishina limit.  For fast cooling, \(\gamma_m>\gamma_c\), the helper
first evaluates
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
A_f&=\frac{(p-1)\gamma_m}{p-2}-\gamma_c,\\
B_f&=\gamma_m^{p-1}\gamma_M^{2-p}
-(p-1)\gamma_m-(2-p)\gamma_c,\\
C_f&=(p-1)\gamma_m-(p-2)\gamma_c,\\
H_f(\hat\gamma_i)&=
\gamma_m^{p-1}(\gamma_M^{2-p}-\hat\gamma_i^{2-p}),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and then uses
\begin{equation}
\eta_i^{\rm KN}=
\begin{cases}
0, & \hat\gamma_i<\gamma_c,\\[0.6ex]
\dfrac{\hat\gamma_i-\gamma_c}{A_f},
& \gamma_c\le\hat\gamma_i<\gamma_m,\ p>2,\\[2ex]
\dfrac{(2-p)(\hat\gamma_i-\gamma_c)}{B_f},
& \gamma_c\le\hat\gamma_i<\gamma_m,\ p\le2,\\[2ex]
1-\dfrac{\gamma_m^{p-1}\hat\gamma_i^{2-p}}{C_f},
& \gamma_m\le\hat\gamma_i<\gamma_M,\ p>2,\\[2ex]
1-\dfrac{B_f}{H_f(\hat\gamma_i)},
& \gamma_m\le\hat\gamma_i<\gamma_M,\ p\le2,\\[2ex]
1, & \hat\gamma_i\ge\gamma_M.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
For slow cooling, \(\gamma_m\le\gamma_c\), the helper instead evaluates
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
A_s&=\frac{\gamma_c^{3-p}}{p-2}-\gamma_m^{3-p},\\
B_s&=(3-p)\gamma_c\gamma_M^{2-p}
-\gamma_c^{3-p}-(2-p)\gamma_m^{3-p},\\
C_s&=\gamma_c^{3-p}-(p-2)\gamma_m^{3-p},\\
U_i&=\hat\gamma_i^{3-p}-\gamma_m^{3-p},\\
V_i&=(3-p)\gamma_c\hat\gamma_i^{2-p},\\
W_i&=(3-p)\gamma_c(\gamma_M^{2-p}-\hat\gamma_i^{2-p}),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and uses
\begin{equation}
\eta_i^{\rm KN}=
\begin{cases}
0, & \hat\gamma_i<\gamma_m,\\[0.6ex]
\dfrac{U_i}{A_s},
& \gamma_m\le\hat\gamma_i<\gamma_c,\ p>2,\\[2ex]
\dfrac{(2-p)U_i}{B_s},
& \gamma_m\le\hat\gamma_i<\gamma_c,\ p\le2,\\[2ex]
1-\dfrac{V_i}{C_s},
& \gamma_c\le\hat\gamma_i<\gamma_M,\ p>2,\\[2ex]
1-\dfrac{W_i}{B_s},
& \gamma_c\le\hat\gamma_i<\gamma_M,\ p\le2,\\[2ex]
1, & \hat\gamma_i\ge\gamma_M.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Synchrotron-self-absorption appears with the opposite sign in
Equation~\eqref{eq:electron-forward-cooling-assembly} because absorbed
synchrotron photons heat the same electron grid.

\subsection{Cooling-branch boundary}

The cooling branches have a hard physics boundary.  Joint hadronic feedback uses
only the tabulated IC/KN path, because the secondary source
\(Q_{e,R}^{\rm sec}\) and the attenuated photon field must be evaluated against
the same local \(N_e,n_\gamma\) state.  An analytic \(Y\) closure would separate
these two objects.  The public Python cooling modes expose the synchrotron-only,
tabulated IC/KN, and Nakar-style branches.  The Fan-style branch is an internal
Fortran closure.

\subsection{Synchrotron-self-absorption heating kernel}

\subsubsection{SSA target photon state}

The SSA heating kernel measures how absorbed synchrotron photons heat the
electron grid.  It computes \(A_i^{\rm abs}\) in
Equation~\eqref{eq:electron-forward-cooling-assembly} from the local seed photon
density \(n_\nu\).  For an electron with Lorentz factor \(\gamma_i\), the
frequency scales are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\nu_B&=\frac{eB'}{2\pi m_e c},&
\nu_{\min,i}&=\frac{\nu_B}{\gamma_i},\\
\nu_{u,i}&=\frac{3eB'\gamma_i^2}{4\pi m_e c},&
B_Q&=\frac{m_e^2 c^3}{e\hbar},
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:ssa-frequency-scales}
\end{equation}
where \(\nu_B\) is the gyrofrequency, \(\nu_{\min,i}\) is the low-frequency
cutoff of the fit, \(\nu_{u,i}\) is the synchrotron turnover scale for that
electron, and \(B_Q=m_e^2c^3/(e\hbar)\) with \(\hbar=h/(2\pi)\).

\subsubsection{SSA single-particle fit}

The single-particle absorption fit gives the heating cross section used in the
frequency integral.  It is split at \(\nu_{u,i}\):
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
S_{L,i}&=\kappa_1\frac{B_Q}{B'}
(\chi_{\rm L}\nu_{\min,i})^{5/3},\\
S_{H,i}&=\kappa_2\frac{B_Q}{B'}\gamma_i^{-3},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\Sigma_i(\nu)=
\begin{cases}
S_{L,i}\nu^{-5/3},
& \nu_{\min,i}<\nu\le\nu_{u,i},\\[2ex]
S_{H,i}\dfrac{\nu_B}{\nu}\exp(-\nu/\nu_{u,i}),
& \nu>\nu_{u,i},
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
where \(\kappa_1\), \(\kappa_2\), and \(\chi_{\rm L}\) are the fixed coefficients
of the adopted synchrotron-absorption asymptotic fit.  They belong to the
single-particle kernel and are not additional physical parameters.  The heating
coefficient returned to the cooling assembler is
\begin{equation}
A_i^{\rm abs}
=c\int_{\nu_{\min,i}}^{\nu_{\max}}
h\nu\,n_\nu\,\Sigma_i(\nu)\,d\nu .
\end{equation}
This integral counts the photon energy absorbed per unit time by electrons at
\(\gamma_i\).

\subsubsection{SSA quadrature}

The SSA quadrature separates the low- and high-frequency parts of the absorption
fit.  It locates the first seed-frequency node above \(\nu_{\min,i}\), the first
node above \(\nu_{u,i}\), and clips only the two boundary cells.  Complete
low-frequency cells use the prefix sum
\begin{equation}
\mathcal P_k=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}
hc\sum_{q=1}^{N_{\rm G}}w_{\ell q}
n_\nu(\nu_{\ell q})\nu_{\ell q}^{-2/3},
\end{equation}
so their contribution to \(A_i^{\rm abs}\) is
\begin{equation}
\kappa_1\frac{B_Q}{B'}(\chi_{\rm L}\nu_{\min,i})^{5/3}
\left(\mathcal P_{k_2}-\mathcal P_{k_1-1}\right).
\end{equation}
The high-frequency cells use the same logarithmic Gauss rule and evaluate
\begin{equation}
\kappa_2\frac{B_Q}{B'}\gamma_i^{-3}\nu_B hc
\sum_{\ell,q} w_{\ell q}n_\nu(\nu_{\ell q})
\exp(-\nu_{\ell q}/\nu_{u,i}).
\end{equation}
Here \(N_{\rm G}\) is the fixed Gauss order.  The nodes \(\nu_{\ell q}\) and
weights \(w_{\ell q}\) are obtained by applying the logarithmic Gauss--Legendre
rule in \(x=\ln\nu\), so \(w_{\ell q}\) already includes the \(d\nu=\nu\,dx\)
Jacobian.  Within each frequency cell the shared interpolation primitive
supplies the local \(n_\nu\); for positive endpoints this is a log--log
power-law interpolation.

\subsection{Grid-level inverse-Compton cooling kernel}

\subsubsection{IC seed grid}

The grid-level inverse-Compton kernel computes the cooling coefficient
\(A_i^{\rm IC}\) from the local seed photon spectrum.  The seed field is first
placed at logarithmic cell centers:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\nu_{0,k+1/2}&=(\nu_{0,k}\nu_{0,k+1})^{1/2},\\
\Delta\nu_{0,k+1/2}&=\nu_{0,k+1/2}
\left(\ln\nu_{0,k+1}-\ln\nu_{0,k}\right),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and the photon number density \(n_{\nu_0}\) is evaluated there by power-law
interpolation.

\subsubsection{IC kinematic kernel}

The IC kinematic kernel decides which scattered photon bins are allowed for an
electron at \(\gamma_i\).  The dimensionless seed and scattered photon energies
are
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_0=\frac{h\nu_0}{m_e c^2},\qquad
\epsilon=\frac{h\nu}{m_e c^2},
\end{equation}
with \(\xi=4\gamma_i\epsilon_0\).  The largest scattered energy is
\begin{equation}
\epsilon\le
\epsilon_{\max}=\frac{4\gamma_i^2\epsilon_0}
{1+4\gamma_i\epsilon_0},
\end{equation}
and the Jones variable is
\begin{equation}
q=\frac{\epsilon}{\xi(\gamma_i-\epsilon)} .
\end{equation}
Allowed scattered bins in the low-frequency Thomson branch,
\(\nu_0/(4\gamma_i^2)<\nu\le\nu_0\), use
\begin{equation}
G_{\rm L}=\frac{\nu}{\nu_0}-\frac{1}{4\gamma_i^2},
\end{equation}
and the remaining allowed bins use the Jones kernel \(G_{\rm J}(q,\xi)\) from
Equation~\eqref{eq:ssc-kernel}.

\subsubsection{IC loss coefficient}

The loss coefficient passed to the cooling assembler is
\begin{equation}
A_i^{\rm IC}=
\frac{3h\sigma_T}{4m_e c\gamma_i^3}
\int d\nu_0\,\frac{n_{\nu_0}}{\nu_0}
\int d\nu\,\nu\,G_{\rm loss}(\nu,\nu_0,\gamma_i),
\end{equation}
where \(G_{\rm loss}\) is the branch-selected kernel above.  This coefficient is
positive for inverse-Compton cooling.

\subsubsection{Coupled-budget IC coefficient}

The coupled electron--photon budget path evaluates the same IC kernel on the
log-spaced seed and scattered-frequency grids.  With Simpson weights, it uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
A_i^{\rm IC,b}&=
\frac{3h\sigma_T}{4m_e c\gamma_i^3}
\left(\frac{\Delta x_\nu}{3}\right)^2\\
&\quad\times
\sum_l w_l\nu_l^2
\left[\sum_k w_k n_{\nu_k}K_{lk}(\gamma_i)\right],
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(x_\nu=\ln\nu\).  The kernel uses \(K_{lk}=G_{\rm J}(q,\xi)\) when the
seed bin is below the scattered bin and
\(K_{lk}=\max[\nu_l/\nu_k-1/(4\gamma_i^2),0]\) for the low-frequency branch.
This path supplies the auxiliary coefficient used by the implicit cooling solve
in the joint feedback calculation.

\subsection{Synchrotron emissivity quadrature}

\subsubsection{Local synchrotron emissivity}

The synchrotron emissivity is computed from the transported electron spectrum.
Using the standard single-particle kernel,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_\nu^{\rm syn}(R)&=
\frac{\sqrt{3}e^3B'}{m_e c^2}
\int N_e(\gamma_e,R)
F\!\left(\frac{\nu}{\nu_c}\right)d\gamma_e,\\
\nu_c&=C_\nu B'\gamma_e^2,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(C_\nu\) is defined in Eq.~(\ref{eq:synch-frequency-coeff}).  Self-absorption
is applied after the emitted luminosity has been assembled
\citep{RybickiLightman1979,Sari1998,Granot2002}.

\subsubsection{Single-particle fit}

The fast synchrotron paths replace \(F(x)\) by a fixed analytic fit:
\begin{equation}
\Phi(x)=
\begin{cases}
A_\Phi x^{1/3}, & x<x_{\rm L},\\
A_\Phi e^{-x}\left(x^{-2/3}+B_\Phi^2\right)^{-1/2},
& x_{\rm L}\le x\le x_{\rm H},\\
0, &x>x_{\rm H},
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
where \(x=\nu/\nu_c\).  The constants \(A_\Phi\), \(B_\Phi\), \(x_{\rm L}\), and
\(x_{\rm H}\) are the fixed coefficients of this kernel fit.  They set the
approximation to the single-particle synchrotron function and are not physical
model parameters.

\subsubsection{Fixed-grid emissivity quadrature}

The fixed-grid emissivity branch integrates over the transported electron grid.
On \(y_i=\ln\gamma_i\) with spacing \(\Delta y\), Simpson weights
\(w_i^{\rm S}\), and \(N_i=dN_e/d\gamma_e(\gamma_i)\), it evaluates
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_{\nu,{\rm em}}&=
C_{\rm syn}B'\frac{\Delta y}{3}
\sum_i w_i^{\rm S}N_i\gamma_i\\
&\quad\times
\Phi\!\left(\frac{\nu}{C_\nu B'\gamma_i^2}\right),\\
C_{\rm syn}&=\frac{\sqrt{3}e^3}{m_e c^2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here the factor \(N_i\gamma_i\,dy\) is the electron number in a logarithmic
energy interval.

\subsection{Synchrotron self-absorption shell transfer}

\subsubsection{Self-absorption depth}

The self-absorption depth measures how many synchrotron photons are removed
while they cross one shocked shell.  The code uses the electron column in that
shell, not a volume emissivity, because the shell already carries its total
electron number.  For adjacent electron points we define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
q_i=\frac{N_i}{\gamma_i^2},\qquad
\bar\gamma_{i+1/2}=\frac{\gamma_i+\gamma_{i+1}}{2},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(N_i=dN_e/d\gamma_e\) and \(\bar\gamma_{i+1/2}\) is the midpoint Lorentz
factor of the cell face.  The finite-volume form of the absorption coefficient
then gives
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\tau_\nu&=
\frac{C_\tau B'}{4\pi R^2\nu^2}
\sum_i
\bar\gamma_{i+1/2}^2
\left(q_i-q_{i+1}\right)\\
&\quad\times
\Phi\!\left[
\frac{\nu}{C_\nu B'\bar\gamma_{i+1/2}^2}
\right],\\
C_\tau&=\frac{\sqrt{3}e^3}{8\pi m_e^2 c^2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(R\) is the shock radius, \(B'\) is the comoving magnetic field, and
\(\Phi\) is the same synchrotron kernel used for emission.  The endpoint
difference \(q_i-q_{i+1}\) is the discrete form of the derivative that appears
in synchrotron self-absorption.

\subsubsection{Finite-cell transfer and seed photons}
\label{sec:syn-shell-transfer}

The finite-cell transfer factor converts intrinsic synchrotron power into the
power that leaves the shell.  For a uniform emitting and absorbing cell, that
factor is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\psi(\tau_\nu)&=
\begin{cases}
1,&\tau_\nu=0,\\
\dfrac{1-\exp(-\tau_\nu)}{\tau_\nu},&\tau_\nu>0,
\end{cases}
\label{eq:finite-cell-transfer}\\
L_\nu^{\rm syn}&=L_{\nu,{\rm em}}\psi(\tau_\nu),\\
n_\nu^{\rm syn}&=\frac{L_\nu^{\rm syn}}{4\pi R^2 c h\nu}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(L_{\nu,{\rm em}}\) is the luminosity before absorption, \(L_\nu^{\rm syn}\)
is the escaping synchrotron luminosity, and \(n_\nu^{\rm syn}\) is the photon
number density used by the inverse-Compton kernel.  The state grid evaluates the
same analytic factor with its small-\(\tau_\nu\) series in the optically thin
limit.  The adaptive solution for \(\nu_a\) below uses the optical depth
\(\tau_\nu\) itself.

\subsection{Cyclotron-bin synchrotron extension}

The cyclotron-bin extension adds the radiation from mildly relativistic
electrons that sit below the synchrotron branch.  Electrons above the transition
\(\gamma_{\rm cyc}\) still use the synchrotron quadrature.  Electrons below it
deposit their power into the frequency bin that contains the fundamental
cyclotron frequency.  For an electron segment,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta N_i=\frac{N_i+N_{i+1}}{2}(\gamma_{i+1}-\gamma_i),
\qquad
\bar\gamma_i=\frac{\gamma_i+\gamma_{i+1}}{2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Delta N_i\) is the number of electrons in the segment and
\(\bar\gamma_i\) is its midpoint Lorentz factor.  The branch places their power
at
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\nu_0&=\frac{\nu_B}{\bar\gamma_i},\\
\nu_B&=\frac{eB'}{2\pi m_e c}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(\nu_B\) is the nonrelativistic gyrofrequency.  The luminosity added to
the selected bin is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta L_{\nu_k,{\rm em}}&=
\frac{\Delta N_i}{\Delta\nu_k}
\frac{4}{3}\sigma_{\rm es}cU'_B
\bar\gamma_i^2
\left(1-\bar\gamma_i^{-2}\right),\\
U'_B&=\frac{B'^2}{8\pi},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(\sigma_{\rm es}\) is the electron-scattering cross section and
\(\Delta\nu_k\) is the bin width.  The same factor \(\psi(\tau_{\nu_k})\)
converts this emitted luminosity into the escaping luminosity and the local seed
photon density.

\subsection{Positive-field radiation interpolation}
\label{sec:positive-field-interpolation}

\subsubsection{Power-law positive branch}

The positive-field interpolation keeps radiation fields positive between
frequency bins.  When both endpoints are positive, the code assumes a local
power law,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
Y(\nu)&=Y_0
\left(\frac{\nu}{\nu_0}\right)^{s_Y},\\
s_Y&=\frac{\ln(Y_1/Y_0)}{\ln(\nu_1/\nu_0)},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
between the adjacent bins \(\nu_0\) and \(\nu_1\).  Here \(Y\) can be an
emissivity, a luminosity, or a photon density.  If either endpoint is not
positive, the code uses a linear interpolation of the same tabulated field; it
does not invent a positive value.

\subsection{Log-Gauss radiation quadrature}

\subsubsection{Reduced frequency grid}

The reduced frequency grid gives the radiation integrals enough points near the
low-frequency self-absorbed part and still reaches \(\nu_{\max}\).  For a table
with at least \(N_{\rm red}\) points, the grid is logarithmic from
\(x_0=\ln\nu_{\min}\) to
\(x_t=x_0+f_{\rm tail}(x_1-x_0)\), and the last point is
\(x_1=\ln\nu_{\max}\).  Smaller reduced tables use ordinary logarithmic
spacing.  On each logarithmic interval the quadrature is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\int_{\nu_0}^{\nu_1}Y(\nu)d\nu
&\simeq
\sum_{a=1}^{N_{\rm G}} \Delta x\,\nu_a\,Y(\nu_a),\\
\nu_a&=\exp(x_m+\Delta x\,\xi_a).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(x_m=(\ln\nu_0+\ln\nu_1)/2\), \(\Delta x=(\ln\nu_1-\ln\nu_0)/2\), and
\(\xi_a\) are the nodes of the fixed Gauss rule.  The factor \(\nu_a\) appears
because \(d\nu=\nu\,dx\).

\subsection{SSA heating segment quadrature}

The self-absorption heating segment adds absorbed photon energy back to the
electrons in one frequency interval.  For a local photon density \(n_\nu\), the
heating rate is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
H_{\rm SSA}&=
\int_{\nu_0}^{\nu_1}\sigma_{\rm SSA}(\nu)n_\nu h\nu c\,d\nu,\\
\psi_u(\nu)&=\frac{\nu_B}{\nu}\exp(-\nu/\nu_u).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\sigma_{\rm SSA}\) is the self-absorption cross section used by the same
electron segment, and \(\psi_u\) is the high-frequency cutoff shape.  The
cross-section branch is
\begin{equation}
\sigma_{\rm SSA}(\nu)=
\begin{cases}
\Sigma_{\rm L}\nu^{-5/3}, & \nu<\nu_u,\\
\Sigma_{\rm H}\psi_u(\nu), & \nu\ge\nu_u.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
The constants \(\Sigma_{\rm L}\), \(\Sigma_{\rm H}\), and \(\nu_u\) are fixed by
the local electron segment and magnetic field.  The integral uses the same
log-Gauss rule and the same local photon interpolation as the radiation
integrals above.

\subsection{Self-absorption frequency solver}

\subsubsection{Adaptive emissivity and opacity quadrature}

The adaptive \(\nu_a\) solver finds the frequency where the shell becomes
optically thick.  It evaluates the optical-depth integral away from the stored
frequency grid because \(\nu_a\) need not fall on a tabulated point.  On an
electron cell \([x_i,x_{i+1}]\), with \(x=\ln\gamma\), it linearly reconstructs
\(N_e\) and \(q=N_e/\gamma^2\).  It then compares two- and three-point Gauss
estimates for both emissivity and optical depth, and splits the cell at its
midpoint if
\begin{equation}
\max\left(
\frac{|P^{(3)}-P^{(2)}|}{|P^{(3)}|},
\frac{|\tau^{(3)}-\tau^{(2)}|}{|\tau^{(3)}|}
\right)>\epsilon_a .
\end{equation}
Here \(P^{(m)}\) and \(\tau^{(m)}\) are the emissivity and optical-depth
estimates from an \(m\)-point rule.  The tolerance \(\epsilon_a\) controls only
this quadrature refinement.

\subsubsection{Self-absorption root}

The self-absorption frequency is the root of \(\tau_\nu(\nu_a)=1\).  The search
starts from a fixed trial frequency \(\nu_{a,0}\).  It expands the bracket by a
logarithmic factor \(q_a\) toward lower or higher frequency until the bracket
contains \(\tau_\nu=1\).  The lower search bound is \(\nu_{a,\min}\), and the
upper bound is
\begin{equation}
\nu_{\rm cap}=\max\left[\nu_{a,\rm floor},
\min\left(\nu_{a,\max},\eta_a\max_i\nu_{c,i+1/2}\right)\right].
\end{equation}
Here \(\nu_{c,i+1/2}\) is the characteristic synchrotron frequency of an
electron-cell face, and \(\eta_a\) sets how far above the largest local
characteristic frequency the solver may search.  Inside the bracket, the
log-secant update is
\begin{equation}
\ln\nu_\ast=
\ln\nu_l-\ln\tau_l
\frac{\ln\nu_h-\ln\nu_l}{\ln\tau_h-\ln\tau_l},
\end{equation}
where \(\nu_l\) and \(\nu_h\) are the bracket endpoints.  The update stays inside
the current bracket and stops when the logarithmic width is below
\(\epsilon_{\ln\nu}\), or after \(N_a\) iterations.  If an optical-depth grid is
already available, the same log-log formula is applied between the adjacent bins
that straddle \(\tau_\nu=1\).  If the supplied grid does not contain the crossing,
the code returns the nearest boundary frequency.

\subsection{Synchrotron polarization kernel}

\subsubsection{Intrinsic polarization kernels}

The polarization kernel computes the intrinsic synchrotron polarization at fixed
transferred intensity.  It does not change the self-absorbed luminosity.  For a
single electron,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
P_\perp&\propto\frac{F(x)+G(x)}{2},\\
P_\parallel&\propto\frac{F(x)-G(x)}{2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
F(x)&=x\int_0^\infty
e^{-x\cosh u}\frac{\cosh(a_F u)}{\cosh u}\,du,\\
G(x)&=x\int_0^\infty
e^{-x\cosh u}\cosh(a_G u)\,du .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(P_\perp\) and \(P_\parallel\) are the two linear-polarization emissivity
components, \(x\) is the usual synchrotron frequency ratio, and \(a_F=5/3\) and
\(a_G=2/3\) are the standard kernel constants.

\subsubsection{Asymptotic and quadrature branches}

The local polarization kernel uses analytic limits where the integrals have
simple forms.  At low \(x\), it uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
F(x)&=2^{a_G}\Gamma(a_G)x^{1/3},\\
G(x)&=2^{-1/3}\Gamma(a_G)x^{1/3},
\qquad x<x_{\rm p,L}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
At high \(x\), it uses
\begin{equation}
b_F=\frac{55}{72},\qquad b_G=\frac{7}{72},
\end{equation}
so that
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
F(x)&=\left(\frac{\pi x}{2}\right)^{1/2}e^{-x}
\left(1+\frac{b_F}{x}\right),\\
G(x)&=\left(\frac{\pi x}{2}\right)^{1/2}e^{-x}
\left(1+\frac{b_G}{x}\right),
\qquad x>x_{\rm p,H}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(b_F\) and \(b_G\) are the first correction coefficients in the large-\(x\)
expansion.  For \(x_{\rm p,L}\le x\le x_{\rm p,H}\), it integrates the two
\(u\)-space definitions on
\begin{equation}
0\le u\le \sinh^{-1}(\zeta_{\rm p}/x)
\end{equation}
with \(N_{\rm p}\) uniform cells and an \(N_{\rm G,p}\)-point Gauss rule in each
cell.  The upper limit sets the retained part of the exponential tail for the
adopted kernel accuracy.  The single-particle components returned to the
compiled synchrotron callers are \((F+G)/2\) and \((F-G)/2\).

\subsubsection{Polarized luminosity assembly}

The polarized luminosity assembly weights the intrinsic polarization by the
local electron distribution.  At each frequency, the intrinsic fraction is
\begin{equation}
\Pi_\nu=
\frac{\int N_e(\gamma_e)[P_\perp(x)-P_\parallel(x)]d\gamma_e}
{\int N_e(\gamma_e)[P_\perp(x)+P_\parallel(x)]d\gamma_e},
\end{equation}
and the returned polarized luminosities are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_{\nu,\perp}&=\frac{1+\Pi_\nu}{2}L_\nu^{\rm syn},\\
L_{\nu,\parallel}&=\frac{1-\Pi_\nu}{2}L_\nu^{\rm syn}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For \(\chi\)-resolved states, the code also tracks how absorption accumulates
through the ordered shell zones.  The cumulative path is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\tau_{\nu,\le b}&=\sum_{a=1}^b\tau_{\nu,a},\\
\tau_{\nu,\le b}(\nu_{{\rm a},b})&=1.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and the shell-reduced spectra are log-\(\chi\) integrals,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_{\nu,{\rm sh}}&=\sum_a \chi_a\Delta\eta\ln10\,L_{\nu,a},\\
n_{\nu,{\rm sh}}&=\sum_a \chi_a\Delta\eta\ln10\,n_{\nu,a}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Thermal Bessel table}

The thermal Bessel table supplies the modified Bessel function needed for
thermal electron normalizations.  It uses \(K_2(z)=2/z^2\) at small \(z\), a
tabulated piecewise-linear value for \(z_{\rm L}\le z\le z_{\rm H}\), and the
large-\(z\) expansion
\begin{equation}
K_2(z)\simeq
\left(\frac{\pi}{2z}\right)^{1/2}e^{-z}
\left(1+\frac{15}{8z}+\frac{105}{128z^2}\right)
\end{equation}
outside the table at high \(z\).

\subsection{Adaptive radiation-grid resampling}

\subsubsection{Active support selection}

The adaptive radiation-grid module selects support points for a synchrotron
quadrature.  It does not evolve, renormalize, or smooth the transported electron
spectrum \(N_e\).  Its input is the already computed list of pairs
\((\gamma_i,N_{e,i})\), and its output is a smaller list used only by the
emissivity integral.  The direct wrapper
\texttt{electron\_radiation\_grid} is compiled with the electron common kernels,
but the inspected production light-curve kernels do not call this wrapper.  The
equations below therefore define the resampling algorithm itself.

The active support is the positive part of the transported electron spectrum.
The wrapper takes the first and last positive bins, \(i_-\le i\le i_+\), and
assumes that \(\gamma_i\) is ordered and \(N_{e,i}>0\) on that interval.  This
matches the contiguous spectra generated by the one-dimensional electron
solvers.  If \(N_{\rm act}=i_+-i_-+1\le N_{\rm rad}\), the full active grid is
kept.  Otherwise the wrapper asks for \(m_{\rm rad}\) samples and relabels the
active grid by \(1\le i\le N_g\).  The resampler then works on
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
x_i&=\log_{10}\gamma_i,\\
y_i&=\log_{10}N_{e,i}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(x_i\) and \(y_i\) are coordinates used only to choose quadrature support.
They do not replace the physical electron spectrum.

\subsubsection{Log-slope and curvature estimator}

The curvature estimator finds where the electron spectrum bends in log-log
space.  It first estimates \(dy/dx\) with one-sided edge differences and
two-sided interior differences,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
D_1&=\frac{y_2-y_1}{x_2-x_1},\\
D_{N_g}&=\frac{y_{N_g}-y_{N_g-1}}{x_{N_g}-x_{N_g-1}},\\
D_i&=\frac{y_{i+1}-y_{i-1}}{x_{i+1}-x_{i-1}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(D_i\) is the local finite-difference estimate of \(dy/dx\).  The same
stencil applied to \(D_i\) gives a local estimate of \(d^2y/dx^2\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
K_1&=\frac{D_2-D_1}{x_2-x_1},\\
K_{N_g}&=\frac{D_{N_g}-D_{N_g-1}}
{x_{N_g}-x_{N_g-1}},\\
K_i&=\frac{D_{i+1}-D_{i-1}}{x_{i+1}-x_{i-1}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(K_i\) is the curvature estimate.  The associated local log-grid width is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
h_1&=x_2-x_1,\\
h_{N_g}&=x_{N_g}-x_{N_g-1},\\
h_i&=\frac{x_{i+1}-x_{i-1}}{2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The product \(|K_i|h_i\) is therefore large near a cooling break, an injection
turnover, or a cutoff.

\subsubsection{Curvature-weighted support selection}

The support selector places more quadrature points where the electron spectrum
has large curvature.  It starts from
\(q_i=|K_i|h_i\), averages this measure over \(N_{\rm sm}\) neighboring points
to obtain \(\bar q_i\), and assigns the sampling weight
\begin{equation}
w_i=\max\left(\bar q_i,\frac{\sum_j\bar q_j}{m_{\rm rad}g_{\rm rad}}\right).
\end{equation}
Here \(g_{\rm rad}\) sets the minimum weight relative to the mean selected
weight.  The point weights are then converted to log-cell integral weights,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
W_1&=w_1(x_2-x_1),\\
W_{N_g}&=w_{N_g}(x_{N_g}-x_{N_g-1}),\\
W_i&=w_i\frac{x_{i+1}-x_{i-1}}{2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The cumulative coordinate is \(C_i=\sum_{j=1}^i W_j\).  Uniform targets in this
coordinate are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
s_\ell&=\frac{(\ell-1)C_{N_g}}{m_{\rm rad}-1},\\
\ell&=1,\ldots,m_{\rm rad},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and each \(s_\ell\) is mapped back to the first source index with
\(C_i\ge s_\ell\).  Duplicate indices are removed.  If the curvature is
concentrated into too few distinct indices, the remaining slots are filled by
uniformly chosen absent indices, and the final list is sorted.  If all curvature
weights vanish, the selector returns a uniform subset of the original log grid.

The selected radiation grid preserves the ends of the emitting electron support.
It keeps the first and last positive active bins, the selected interior active
bins, and one neighboring bin on each side when such a bin exists.  This choice
serves only the synchrotron quadrature.  Power-law portions of
\(N_e(\gamma)\) are nearly straight in \((\log\gamma,\log N_e)\), while cooling
breaks, injection turnovers, and cutoffs need denser support.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\(\chi\)}{chi}-resolved downstream electron state}

\subsubsection{Downstream coordinate and cell averages}

The two-dimensional forward-electron branch resolves where electrons sit behind
the shock.  It advances the electron content on a finite downstream mass
coordinate \(q\), and it keeps \(\chi\) as a derived diagnostic coordinate.  For
a gas with adiabatic index \(\hat\gamma\), the strong-shock compression is
\begin{equation}
r_{\rm sh}=\frac{\hat\gamma+1}{\hat\gamma-1}
\end{equation}
and the active downstream domain is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
q_\ast&=1-\left(1-\frac{1}{r_{\rm sh}}\right)^{r_{\rm sh}},\\
\Delta q&=\frac{q_\ast}{N_\chi},\\
q_{a+1/2}&=a\Delta q.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(a\) labels downstream cells and \(q=0\) is the shock front.  The default
two-dimensional finite-volume branch uses the four-velocity coordinate \(y\)
defined above and advances the cell average
\begin{equation}
U_{ia}(R)=\frac{1}{\Delta y_i\Delta q}
\int_{\Delta y_i}\int_{\Delta q_a}
\frac{\partial^2 N_e}{\partial y\,\partial q}\,dq\,dy .
\end{equation}
The characteristic and optional pulsar-wind branches use
\(x=\log_{10}\gamma_e\) in the same \(q\) domain.  The initial state places the
already computed shell spectrum into the shock-front cell,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
U_{i1}(R_1)&=\frac{N_i^0}{\Delta q},\\
U_{ia}(R_1)&=0,\qquad a>1,\\
N_i(R_j)&=\sum_{a=1}^{N_\chi}U_{ia}(R_j)\Delta q .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(N_i^0\) is the one-zone shell spectrum at the start of the
two-dimensional branch.  With this normalization, summing over \(q\) returns the
shell-reduced spectrum used by the one-zone radiation modules.

\subsubsection{Finite-speed downstream geometry}

The finite-speed downstream geometry maps the mass coordinate \(q\) to a local
radius and Lorentz factor.  The diagnostic coordinate \(\chi\) is derived from
this map, while the transport itself remains on \(q\).  For an external density
profile \(n\propto R^{-k}\), the ultra-relativistic Blandford--McKee coordinate
is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\bar q&=1-q,\\
\alpha_\chi&=\frac{4-k}{3-k},\\
\chi_{\rm BM}&=\bar q^{-\alpha_\chi}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(k\) is the external density index and \(q=0\) is the shock front.  With
shock-front four-speed \(u_f=(\Gamma_f^2-1)^{1/2}\), speed
\(\beta_f=u_f/\Gamma_f\), and finite-speed weight \(\omega_f=\beta_f^2\), the
two limiting logarithmic radius offsets are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\lambda_{\rm BM}&=-\frac{\chi_{\rm BM}-1}
{4(4-k)\Gamma_f^2},\\
\lambda_{\rm pl}&=\frac{\ln \bar q}{r_{\rm sh}(3-k)} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The label ``pl'' denotes the planar strong-shock limit.  The cell radius is
assigned as
\begin{equation}
r(q)=R\exp\!\left[\omega_f\lambda_{\rm BM}
+(1-\omega_f)\lambda_{\rm pl}\right],
\end{equation}
so the relativistic branch is used when \(\beta_f\) is close to unity and the
planar branch is used when the shock slows down.  The downstream rapidity, not
\(\Gamma\) itself, is interpolated:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
u_{\rm BM}&=\frac{u_f}{\chi_{\rm BM}^{1/2}},\\
\beta_{\rm pl}&=\beta_f e^{\lambda_{\rm pl}},\\
u_{\rm pl}&=\frac{\beta_{\rm pl}}
{(1-\beta_{\rm pl}^2)^{1/2}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
u(q)&=\sinh\!\left[\omega_f\sinh^{-1}u_{\rm BM}
+(1-\omega_f)\sinh^{-1}u_{\rm pl}\right],\\
\Gamma(q)&=\left[1+u(q)^2\right]^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The output of this block is the cell radius \(r(q)\), speed, and Lorentz factor
used by the downstream radiation and cooling modules.

\subsubsection{Pattern speed and magnetic decay}

The magnetic-decay model needs the time since each downstream cell left the
shock front.  The code first builds the shock-pattern speed from the same two
limits used in the geometry map:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
u_{\rm sh,BM}&=\left(2\Gamma_f^2-1\right)^{1/2},\\
u_{\rm sh,pl}&=\frac{r_{\rm sh}}{r_{\rm sh}-1}u_f,\\
\Psi_{\rm sh}&=\omega_f\sinh^{-1}u_{\rm sh,BM}
+(1-\omega_f)\sinh^{-1}u_{\rm sh,pl},\\
u_{\rm pat}&=\sinh\Psi_{\rm sh}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The corresponding pattern speed and the relative speed between the pattern and
cell \(a\) are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\beta_{\rm pat}&=\frac{u_{\rm pat}}{(1+u_{\rm pat}^2)^{1/2}},\\
\beta_{{\rm rel},a}&=
\frac{\beta_{\rm pat}-\beta_a}{1-\beta_{\rm pat}\beta_a}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The comoving cell width, cumulative distance behind the shock, and
microturbulent age are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta x'_a&=\Gamma_a\Delta x_{{\rm lab},a},\\
x'_a&=\sum_{b=1}^a\Delta x'_b,\\
t'_a&=\frac{x'_a}{\beta_{{\rm rel},a}c}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(t'_a\) is the comoving time available for microturbulent field decay in
cell \(a\).  When the magnetic-decay option is active,
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_{B,a}=\epsilon_{B,\min}
+(\epsilon_B-\epsilon_{B,\min})
\left(1+\frac{t'_a}{t_B}\right)^{\alpha_t},
\end{equation}
and otherwise \(\epsilon_{B,a}=\epsilon_B\).  Here \(t_B\) and \(\alpha_t\) are
the magnetic-decay controls.  The local comoving internal energy density and
magnetic field used by synchrotron emission, self-absorption, cooling, and
diffusion are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
e'_a&=r_{\rm sh}\Gamma_f(\Gamma_f-1)n(R)m_p c^2,\\
B'_a&=\left(8\pi\epsilon_{B,a}e'_a\right)^{1/2},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(n(R)\) is the upstream external density at the shock.  The Bohm diffusion
coefficient for electron bin \(i\) in downstream cell \(a\) is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
r_{{\rm L},ia}&=\frac{\gamma_i m_e c^2}{eB'_a},\\
\kappa_{ia}&=\frac{c\,r_{{\rm L},ia}}{3}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Cell synchrotron and absorption state}

The cell radiation state gives each downstream cell its own synchrotron
luminosity and self-absorption depth.  The optical-depth weight is the cell
width in the transported \(q\) coordinate,
\begin{equation}
w_a=\Delta q .
\end{equation}
For adjacent electron bins in cell \(a\), the code defines
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\bar\gamma_i&=\frac{\gamma_i+\gamma_{i+1}}{2},\\
E_{ia}&=\frac{N_{ia}+N_{i+1,a}}{2}(\gamma_{i+1}-\gamma_i),\\
A_{ia}&=\bar\gamma_i^2
\left(\frac{N_{ia}}{\gamma_i^2}
-\frac{N_{i+1,a}}{\gamma_{i+1}^2}\right),\\
\nu_{ia}&=C_\nu B'_a\bar\gamma_i^2,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(E_{ia}\) is the electron number in the segment, \(A_{ia}\) is the
self-absorption difference, and \(\nu_{ia}\) is the characteristic synchrotron
frequency.  The cell emissivity and optical depth are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_{\nu,a}^{\rm em}&=C_{\rm syn}B'_a
\sum_iE_{ia}\Phi\!\left(\frac{\nu}{\nu_{ia}}\right),\\
\tau_{\nu,a}&=
\frac{C_\tau B'_a w_a}{4\pi R^2\nu^2}
\sum_iA_{ia}\Phi\!\left(\frac{\nu}{\nu_{ia}}\right),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and the transferred luminosity and photon density are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_{\nu,a}&=L_{\nu,a}^{\rm em}\psi(\tau_{\nu,a}),\\
n_{\nu,a}&=\frac{L_{\nu,a}}{4\pi R^2 c h\nu}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\psi\) is the finite-cell transfer factor defined in
Eq.~(\ref{eq:finite-cell-transfer}).  Cells with uniform \(B'_a\) reuse one
\(\Phi\) table, while nonuniform cells evaluate the low-, main-, and
high-frequency branches of the same synchrotron kernel.  The output of this
stage is the local radiation state supplied to photon-history cooling and later
observer projection.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\(\chi\)}{chi}-resolved photon-history cooling}

\subsubsection{Causal light-cone support}

The photon-history field tells a downstream cell which earlier synchrotron
photons can reach it before the cooling update.  For each radial interval
\([R_{j-1},R_j]\), the wrapper constructs this causal field on the finite
\(q\) grid and then passes it to
Eq.~(\ref{eq:electron-forward-cooling-assembly}).  Frequencies in this
subsection are comoving and are written with primes.  The comoving shock time is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
t'_1&=0,\\
t'_j&=t'_{j-1}+
\frac{R_j-R_{j-1}}
{\bar\beta_j\bar\Gamma_j c},\\
\bar\Gamma_j&=\frac{\Gamma_j+\Gamma_{j-1}}{2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(j\) labels the target radius and \(m\) labels an earlier source radius.
For a source cell \(s=(m,a)\) and a target cell \(t=(j,b)\), radiation is used
only when the source is farther behind the shock and lies inside the comoving
light cone:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
x'_{m,a}&\ge x'_{j,b},\\
c(t'_j-t'_m)&\ge x'_{m,a}-x'_{j,b}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The finite emitting fraction of the source cell is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
w_s&=\min\left(1,\frac{c\Delta t'_m}{\Delta x'_{m,a}}\right),\\
\Delta t'_m&=t'_m-t'_{m-1}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The first interval uses \(\Delta t'_1=t'_2-t'_1\).  Thus \(w_s\) is the fraction
of a finite source cell whose photons can enter the target light cone during the
current step.

\subsubsection{Frequency mapping and positive-field interpolation}

The frequency mapping converts radiation from the source-cell frame to the
target-cell frame.  The relative velocity and Doppler factor are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\beta_{st}&=\frac{\beta_t-\beta_s}{1-\beta_t\beta_s},\\
{\cal D}_{st}&=\frac{1+\beta_{st}}{(1-\beta_{st}^2)^{1/2}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(s\) and \(t\) denote the source and target cells.  A target frequency
\(\nu'\) therefore samples the source spectrum at
\begin{equation}
\nu'_s=\frac{\nu'}{{\cal D}_{st}} .
\end{equation}
The contribution is used only when \(\nu'_s\) lies inside the stored
cooling-frequency table.  Between two positive source bins
\(\nu'_l<\nu'_s<\nu'_{l+1}\), a source luminosity or photon-density field \(Q\)
is interpolated as
\begin{equation}
Q_s(\nu'_s)=Q_{s,l}
\exp\!\left[
\frac{\ln\nu'_s-\ln\nu'_l}
{\ln\nu'_{l+1}-\ln\nu'_l}
\ln\!\left(\frac{Q_{s,l+1}}{Q_{s,l}}\right)
\right],
\end{equation}
and a non-positive endpoint gives no contribution.  The interpolation therefore
uses only the positive radiation field that is present in the stored source
cell.

\subsubsection{Path attenuation}

Path attenuation removes source photons that are absorbed before they reach the
target cell.  The path uses the same finite-cell transfer factor as the local
synchrotron calculation,
\begin{equation}
\psi(\tau)=
\begin{cases}
1, & \tau=0,\\
(1-e^{-\tau})/\tau, & \tau>0,
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
with \(\psi(0)=1\).  For a partial comoving path of length \(\Delta x'\) inside
downstream cell \(a\),
\begin{equation}
\tau_{\nu',a}^{\rm seg}
=\tau_{\nu',a}^{\rm prop}
\frac{\Delta x'}{\Delta x'_a}.
\end{equation}
Let \(a_+\) and \(a_-\) denote the cells containing the upstream and downstream
endpoints \(x'_+\) and \(x'_-<x'_+\).  Complete intervening cells are accumulated
through prefix sums of \(\ln\psi\), giving
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
{\cal A}_{\nu'}(x'_+,x'_-)
&=\psi(\tau_{\nu',a_+}^{\rm seg})
\psi(\tau_{\nu',a_-}^{\rm seg})e^{S_{\nu'}},\\
S_{\nu'}
&=\sum_{a=a_-+1}^{a_+-1}
\ln\psi(\tau_{\nu',a}^{\rm prop}) .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \({\cal A}_{\nu'}\) is the survival factor along the source-to-target path.
The propagation optical depth on the reduced cooling grid is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\tau_{\nu',a}^{\rm prop}
&=\tau_{\nu',a}^{\rm SSA}
+\tau_{\nu',a}^{\gamma\gamma},\\
\tau_{\nu',a}^{\gamma\gamma}
&=\Delta x'_a\int \sigma_{\gamma\gamma}(\nu',\tilde\nu')
n_{\tilde\nu',a}\,d\tilde\nu' .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
using the same \(\gamma\gamma\) cross section as the photon-absorption stage.

\subsubsection{History contribution to cooling}

The full-history contribution adds the attenuated source radiation to the target
cooling field.  For target cell \(t\), the contribution is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta L_{\nu',t}^{\rm hist}
&=w_s{\cal D}_{st}^3
{\cal A}_{\nu'} Q_{L,s}\!\left(\frac{\nu'}{{\cal D}_{st}}\right),\\
\Delta n_{\nu',t}^{\rm hist}
&=w_s{\cal D}_{st}^2
{\cal A}_{\nu'} Q_{n,s}\!\left(\frac{\nu'}{{\cal D}_{st}}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The factor \({\cal D}_{st}^3\) transforms luminosity, while
\({\cal D}_{st}^2\) transforms photon density.  Adding these terms to the local
synchrotron seed gives the cell-wise cooling field.  The cooling assembler then
returns \(D_{ib}\) in
\(d\gamma_i/dR=-D_{ib}\gamma_i\), using the same synchrotron, IC/KN, Nakar, Fan,
and self-absorption terms as the one-dimensional forward solver.

\subsubsection{Streamed light-cone update}

The streamed light-cone update stores the photon history without rescanning all
older shells.  It advances two arrays,
\(P_{\nu',b}^{\rm stream}\) and \(n_{\nu',b}^{\rm stream}\), from radial step
\(j-1\) to step \(j\).  For target cell \(b\), the one-step light-cone interval
in the previous shell is
\begin{equation}
[x'_{j,b},\,x'_{j,b}+c(t'_j-t'_{j-1})].
\end{equation}
The old streamed field is sampled at the upper endpoint when that point lies
inside the previous shell.  Newly emitted photons from previous-shell cells
\(a\) are added with the overlap weight
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
x'_{{\rm L},ab}
&=\max(x'_{a-1/2,j-1},x'_{j,b}),\\
x'_{{\rm R},ab}
&=\min(x'_{a+1/2,j-1},x'_{j,b}+c\Delta t'_j),\\
w_{a\to b}^{(j)}
&=\frac{[x'_{{\rm R},ab}-x'_{{\rm L},ab}]_+}
{\Delta x'_{a,j-1}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \([z]_+=\max(z,0)\).  Each source term is then mapped with the same
\({\cal D}_{st}\), positive-field frequency interpolation, and current-shell path
attenuation.  The cooling field used by the two-dimensional electron solve is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_{\nu',b}^{\rm cool}(R_j)
&=L_{\nu',b}^{\rm local}(R_{j-1})
+P_{\nu',b}^{\rm stream}(R_j),\\
n_{\nu',b}^{\rm cool}(R_j)
