&=n_{\nu',b}^{\rm local}(R_{j-1})
+n_{\nu',b}^{\rm stream}(R_j).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
on the reduced cooling-frequency grid.  These history arrays enter only the
local cooling calculation.  Observer-time projection still uses the stored
synchrotron luminosity, optical depth, radius, Lorentz factor, and volume-weight
arrays described below.

\subsection{Downstream \texorpdfstring{\(q\)}{q}-space advection--diffusion}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\(q\)}{q}-space conservation law}

The \(q\)-space transport step moves electrons through the finite downstream
layer before their energy is cooled.  For one electron-energy bin \(i\), the
cell average \(U_i(q,R)\) obeys a conservative radial equation,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{\partial U_i}{\partial R}
+\frac{\partial}{\partial q}\!\left[A_q(q,R)U_i\right]
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial q}\!\left[
\mathcal K_i(q,R)\frac{\partial U_i}{\partial q}
\right]
+S_i(q,R),\\
\mathcal K_i(q,R)
&=\frac{\kappa_i(q,R)}{\beta_{\rm sh}c}
\left(\frac{\partial q}{\partial s}\right)^2,\\
A_q(q,R)&=\frac{(3-k)(q_\ast-q)}{R}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(A_q\) advects electrons with the expanding downstream flow, and
\(\mathcal K_i\) is the diffusion coefficient after changing variables from
shock-frame distance \(s\) to \(q\).  The symbol \(k\) is the external density
index, and \(\beta_{\rm sh}\) is the shock speed.  The advection speed vanishes
at the finite active outer face \(q=q_\ast\), so the transport domain ends at the
computed downstream edge.

\subsubsection{Shock-front source placement}

The shock-front source puts newly accelerated electrons into the first
downstream cell.  The discrete update is
\begin{equation}
U_{ia}^{\rm src}-U_{ia}^{n}
=\frac{Q_i(R,\Delta R)}{\Delta q}\,\delta_{a1},
\end{equation}
where \(a\) labels the downstream cell and \(Q_i(R,\Delta R)\) is the finite
radial source in electron-energy bin \(i\).  The factor \(\delta_{a1}\) places
that source only at the shock front.  After summing over \(q\), this source is
identical to the one-zone forward-shock source.

\subsubsection{Characteristic advection remap}

The characteristic remap moves cell faces along the \(q\)-space flow without
changing the total electron number.  It solves
\begin{equation}
\frac{dq}{dR}=A_q(q,R).
\end{equation}
Inside downstream cell \(a\), the face speed is reconstructed as a linear
function of \(q\),
\begin{equation}
A_q(q)=\alpha_a+\zeta_a q ,
\end{equation}
so a face at \(q_f^{n+1}\) is traced back over \(\Delta R\) by
\begin{equation}
q_f^n=
\begin{cases}
\left(q_f^{n+1}+\alpha_a/\zeta_a\right)e^{-\zeta_a\Delta R}
-\alpha_a/\zeta_a, & \zeta_a\ne0,\\
q_f^{n+1}-\alpha_a\Delta R, & \zeta_a=0 .
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
If the back-traced characteristic crosses a cell face, the same analytic
relation is restarted in the adjacent cell until the full step is consumed.
Before this remap, the shock-front source is added to the first cell,
\begin{equation}
U_{i1}^{\rm src}=U_{i1}^n+\frac{Q_i(R,\Delta R)}{\Delta q} .
\end{equation}
The remap uses the positive PPM reconstruction from the common transport layer.
If
\begin{equation}
M_i(q)=\int_0^q U_i(q')\,dq'
\end{equation}
is the prefix integral of that reconstructed profile, conservation gives the
remapped cell average
\begin{equation}
U_{ia}^{\rm adv}
=\frac{M_i(q_{a+1/2}^n)-M_i(q_{a-1/2}^n)}{\Delta q}.
\end{equation}
This step changes where the electrons sit in \(q\).  It does not change their
energy.

\subsubsection{Diffusion metric and implicit solve}

The diffusion metric converts Bohm diffusion in physical downstream distance
into diffusion on the \(q\) grid.  Using the bridged radius law, define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
x(q)&=R-r(q),\\
J_q(q)&\equiv\frac{\partial q}{\partial s}
=\left[-R\frac{\partial(r/R)}{\partial q}\right]^{-1},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(x(q)\) is the distance behind the shock and \(J_q\) is the coordinate
Jacobian.  The diffusive flux on the \(q\) grid is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal G_{i,a+1/2}
&=\mathcal K_{i,a+1/2}
\frac{U_{i,a+1}-U_{ia}}{\Delta q},\\
\mathcal K_{i,a+1/2}
&=\frac{\kappa_{i,a+1/2}}{\beta_{\rm sh}c}
J_{q,a+1/2}^{2},\\
\left.\frac{\partial U_i}{\partial R}\right|_{\mathcal K}
&=\frac{\partial \mathcal G_i}{\partial q}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The centered implicit diffusion step is therefore
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\delta_{a+}U_i^{n+1}
&=U_{i,a+1}^{n+1}-U_{ia}^{n+1},\\
\mathcal G_{a+}^{n+1}
&=\mathcal K_{i,a+1/2}\frac{\delta_{a+}U_i^{n+1}}{\Delta q},\\
\Delta_a\mathcal G^{n+1}
&=\mathcal G_{a+}^{n+1}-\mathcal G_{a-}^{n+1},\\
U_{ia}^{n+1}
-\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta q}
\,\Delta_a\mathcal G^{n+1}
=U_{ia}^{\rm rhs}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(U_{ia}^{\rm rhs}\) is the state after the non-diffusive source and
advection operations required by the selected branch.  The default implicit
\(q\) step combines advection and diffusion in one tridiagonal solve.  Its
upwind advective face flux is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
A_{a+}^{+}&=\max(A_{a+1/2},0),\\
A_{a+}^{-}&=\min(A_{a+1/2},0),\\
\mathcal H_{a+}^{A}
&=A_{a+}^{+}U_{ia}^{n+1}
+A_{a+}^{-}U_{i,a+1}^{n+1}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The full matrix equation is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
S_{ia}&=\frac{Q_i(R,\Delta R)}{\Delta q}\delta_{a1},\\
\Delta_a\mathcal H^A&=\mathcal H_{a+}^{A}-\mathcal H_{a-}^{A},\\
R_{ia}&=U_{ia}^{n}+S_{ia},\\
U_{ia}^{n+1}
&=R_{ia}
-\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta q}
\left(\Delta_a\mathcal H^A-\Delta_a\mathcal G^{n+1}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This tridiagonal solve conserves the downstream electron content except for the
explicit shock-front source and any chosen outer escape boundary.  The direct
implicit driver uses the joint \(q\) solve before the energy solve.  The split
finite-width driver applies the conservative PPM advection remap and the
implicit diffusion solve as half steps around the energy update.  The
characteristic diagnostic branch uses the same remap-plus-diffusion operator.

The optional pulsar-wind research branch can open the outer diffusive boundary.
When that boundary is active, the last cell adds
\begin{equation}
\mathcal K_{i,N_g+1/2}U_{i,N_g}^{n+1}
\end{equation}
to the diagonal.  This is the absorbing form \(U_{i,N_g+1}=0\).  Without that
boundary, the outer diffusive flux is closed.

\subsection{Downstream energy-space update}

\subsubsection{Four-velocity branch}

The energy-space update cools each downstream column after the \(q\)-transport
step.  In the default four-velocity branch, the face displacement is the sum of
radiative cooling and adiabatic drift over the radial step:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta R_j&=R_j-R_{j-1},\\
J_{i+1/2}&=(dx_g/dy)_{i+1/2},\\
\bar D_{i+1/2,a}&=\frac{D_{ia}+D_{i+1,a}}{2},\\
\eta_{i+1/2,a}
&=\Delta R_j\bar D_{i+1/2,a}
+\ln\!\left(\frac{R_j}{R_{j-1}}\right),\\
{\cal F}_{i+1/2,a}
&=\frac{\eta_{i+1/2,a}}{(\ln 10)J_{i+1/2}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(D_{ia}\) is the cooling coefficient returned by the local photon-field
assembler, \(J_{i+1/2}\) is the grid Jacobian, and \({\cal F}_{i+1/2,a}\) is the
signed face displacement in the transported energy coordinate.  The logarithmic
term is the integrated adiabatic loss, \(d\ln\gamma_e/dR=-1/R\).  The conservative
tridiagonal solve is the same face-flux system used by the one-dimensional
fullhide kernel, but applied independently in each downstream column \(a\).

\subsubsection{Log-gamma backward sweep}

The log-gamma backward sweep is the split energy update used by another
two-dimensional branch.  It updates each downstream column on
\(x=\log_{10}\gamma_e\).  The local energy-space speed and Courant factor are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\xi_{i+1/2,a}
&=D_{i+1/2,a}+\frac{1}{R\ln 10},\\
\lambda_{i+1/2,a}
&=-\frac{\Delta R}{\Delta x}\xi_{i+1/2,a}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(D_{i+1/2,a}\) contains the radiative cooling and \(1/(R\ln10)\) is the
adiabatic term in log-gamma coordinates.  The implicit upper-triangular
coefficients are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
H_{ia}&=1-\lambda_{i-1/2,a},\\
C_{ia}&=\frac{2\lambda_{i+1/2,a}}{H_{ia}+H_{i+1,a}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The solution is then recovered from high to low energy:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\rho_{ia}&=\frac{U_{ia}^{n}}{H_{ia}},\\
U_{N_\gamma,a}^{n+1}&=\rho_{N_\gamma,a},\\
U_{ia}^{n+1}=\Pi_+\!\left(\rho_{ia}
-C_{ia}U_{i+1,a}^{n+1}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Pi_+(X)=\max(X,0)\) is the non-negative projection of the transport
primitive.  It is applied to the solved cell average and does not smooth
neighboring energy bins.

\subsubsection{Characteristic energy branch and optional diffusion}

The characteristic energy branch follows electron cooling trajectories in
\(x=\log_{10}\gamma_e\).  Each \(q\) column uses the same piecewise cooling map as
the one-dimensional characteristic solver.  The source term is supplied only to
the shock-front column,
\begin{equation}
S_{ia}^{x}=S_i^x\delta_{a1}.
\end{equation}
When only affine synchrotron cooling is selected, the cooling law is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d\gamma_e}{dR}
&=-a_{{\rm syn},a}\gamma_e^2-\frac{\gamma_e}{R},\\
a_{{\rm syn},a}
&=\frac{\sigma_{\rm es} B_a'^2}{6\pi m_e c^2\beta_{\rm sh}\Gamma_{\rm sh}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The first term is synchrotron cooling in cell \(a\), and the second term is
adiabatic cooling.  Here \(\sigma_{\rm es}\) is the electron-scattering cross
section.  Otherwise the tabulated piecewise loss array \(D_{ia}\) supplies the
characteristic map.  In the optional stochastic-transport branch, the adiabatic
coefficient is local,
\begin{equation}
\left.\frac{d\log_{10}\gamma_e}{dR}\right|_{\rm ad}
=
\frac{1}{3\beta_f\ln10}
\left(\frac{2\beta_a}{r_a}
+\left.\frac{\partial\beta}{\partial r}\right|_{a}\right),
\end{equation}
which is evaluated by finite differences of the bridged \(\beta_a(r_a)\) profile.
A positive stochastic-acceleration normalization adds a zero-flux diffusion step
in \(x\).  The branch applies this step half before and half after the cooling
solve:
\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial U_a}{\partial R}
=
\frac{\alpha_{\rm st}}{R}
\frac{\partial^2 U_a}{\partial x^2},
\end{equation}
The implicit matrix for that step is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\lambda_x&=\frac{\Delta R\,\alpha_{\rm st}}{R\,\Delta x^2},\\
{\cal L}_{ia}^{n+1}
&=- U_{i-1,a}^{n+1}+2U_{ia}^{n+1}-U_{i+1,a}^{n+1},\\
U_{ia}^{n+1}+\lambda_x{\cal L}_{ia}^{n+1}
&=U_{ia}^{n}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The edge diagonals are \(1+\lambda_x\).  They enforce zero stochastic flux
through the low- and high-energy boundaries.  This branch is a research
transport option for the \(\chi\)-resolved electron path.  It is not a general
pulsar-wind-nebula or cosmic-ray propagation model.

\subsubsection{Returned \texorpdfstring{\(\chi\)}{chi}-resolved state}

The returned \(\chi\)-resolved state converts the transported cell averages back
to physical electron spectra.  After each radius is stored, the wrapper converts
\(U_{ia}\) to \(dN_e/d\gamma_e\) with the coordinate Jacobian and sums over \(q\)
for the shell-level state.  The projection arrays kept for each downstream cell
are \(r_a\), \(\Gamma_a\), \(\Delta q\), \(B'_a\), the unabsorbed synchrotron
luminosity, the local seed field, and the cell optical depth.

The wrapper has a clear boundary.  If only the one-dimensional shell state is
requested, the shell synchrotron field is recomputed from the summed spectrum.
If direct \(\chi\)-resolved projection is requested, local luminosities and
optical depths are retained so foreground absorption can be evaluated during
observer projection.  SSC, hadronic transport, pair-cascade feedback, and
reverse-shock coupling remain shell-level or one-dimensional.  This wrapper does
not make them \(q\)-local physics.

\begin{figure*}[!tp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig3_transport_projection.pdf}
  \caption{Electron and photon state diagnostics before observer projection.  (a) The one-dimensional \(N_e(\gamma_e,R)\) state remains visible on the radius grid.  (b) Radius slices of \(\gamma_e^2 dN_e/d\gamma_e\) show the transported electron spectrum.  (c) A two-dimensional electron run stores the downstream \(\chi\)-resolved volume weight.  (d) The same run stores the synchrotron optical-depth field used by the \(\chi\)-resolved projection.  The figure documents the exposed state used by the implemented solver paths; it is not a convergence proof for every electron transport option.}
  \label{fig:electron-transport}
\end{figure*}

Thermal electrons have a narrow solver boundary.  They are implemented only for
\texttt{fullhide\_1d} and \texttt{fullhide\_1d\_hz}; other solvers reject
thermal-electron transport.  Adaptive electron substeps are implemented for
selected one-dimensional separated paths by comparing one full radial step with
two half steps.  Joint electron-photon coupling uses fixed substeps because the
secondary source and cooling seed are iterated with the hadronic stage.

\section{Photon Fields and Absorption} \label{sec:photon}

\subsection{Comoving target photon state}

The photon-field module builds the comoving photon density seen by the next
radiation process.  The input is the transported electron spectrum and the local
synchrotron emissivity.  The output is a seed-frequency grid, a synchrotron
photon field, an optional SSC photon field, a hadronic target field, and the seed
arrays used by synchrotron self-absorption and SSC absorption.  In a separated
electron-photon run, the target photon density is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
n_{\nu'}^{\rm tar}
&=n_{\nu'}^{\rm syn}
+I_{\rm SSC}\,n_{\nu'}^{\rm SSC},\\
I_{\rm SSC}
&=
\begin{cases}
1, & \text{SSC target active},\\
0, & \text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(n_{\nu'}^{\rm syn}\) and \(n_{\nu'}^{\rm SSC}\) are comoving photon
number densities per unit frequency at frequency \(\nu'\).  The switch
\(I_{\rm SSC}\) only decides whether forward SSC photons enter the hadronic
target field.  In the joint branch, the \(p\gamma\), Bethe--Heitler, and
\(\gamma\gamma\) survival factors act on \(n_{\nu'}^{\rm tar}\) before the next
electron solve.

\subsubsection{ABI binding}

The radiation initializer only connects public names to compiled kernels.  It
binds \texttt{annihilation}, \texttt{cal\_ebl}, \texttt{ssc\_spec}, and
\texttt{ssc\_spec\_nonuniform} on first access.  The public products are the SSC
luminosity \(L_{\nu'}^{\rm SSC}\), the local pair-absorption factor
\(\mathcal{A}_{\gamma\gamma}\), and the external EBL factor
\(\mathcal{A}_{\rm EBL}\).  The scattering kernel, the pair-production cross
section, the EBL table, and the photon-field normalization are defined by the
kernel equations below.

\subsection{Radiation interpolation reuse}

Shared radiation quadratures use one interpolation rule for all photon spectra.
Let \(X=\ln\nu'\) and \(Y=\ln n_{\nu'}\).  If both endpoint photon densities are
positive, the cell is reconstructed as a straight line in \((X,Y)\), which is a
local power law in frequency.  If either endpoint is non-positive, the cell is
reconstructed linearly in \(n_{\nu'}\).  Non-increasing frequency intervals are
outside the supported grid domain.

\subsection{Composite Simpson radiation quadrature}

Uniform radiation grids use Simpson weights for logarithmic integrals.  For a
grid \(x_i=x_1+(i-1)\Delta x\) with the supported parity, define the weights
\(w_i^{\rm S}\).  The integral is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\int_{x_1}^{x_N} f(x)\,dx \simeq
\frac{\Delta x}{3}\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_i^{\rm S} f_i .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This rule is used for the seed-frequency and electron-energy integrals in the
uniform SSC branch.

\subsection{Uniform-grid SSC emissivity}

\subsubsection{Jones--Klein--Nishina variables}

Forward SSC uses the local electrons to scatter the local seed photons.  The
uniform-grid branch evaluates the Jones--Klein--Nishina kernel on logarithmic
electron-energy and seed-frequency grids.  For an emitted comoving frequency
\(\nu'\), define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\epsilon&=\frac{h\nu'}{m_ec^2},&
\epsilon_s&=\frac{h\nu_s'}{m_ec^2},\\
b&=4\gamma\epsilon_s,&
q&=\frac{\epsilon}{b(\gamma-\epsilon)},\\
\eta&=bq=\frac{\epsilon}{\gamma-\epsilon}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\epsilon\) is the emitted photon energy in units of \(m_ec^2\),
\(\epsilon_s\) is the seed-photon energy in the same units, and \(\gamma\) is
the electron Lorentz factor.  For seed photons with \(\nu_s'<\nu'\), the allowed
electron energies satisfy
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma>
\frac{1}{2}\left[\epsilon+
\left(\epsilon^2+\frac{\nu'}{\nu_s'}\right)^{1/2}\right],
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and the scattering kernel is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
G(q,\eta)=
2q\ln q+(1+2q)(1-q)
+\frac{\eta^2(1-q)}{2(1+\eta)}.
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:ssc-kernel}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Uniform-grid Simpson integration}

The uniform SSC luminosity is split by the seed frequency.  For the
\(\nu_s'<\nu'\) part, the contribution is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
C_{\rm IC}(\nu')&=\frac{3}{4}ch\sigma_{\rm es}\nu',\\
n_s&\equiv n_{\nu_s'}^{\rm tar},\\
L_{\nu'}^{{\rm SSC},<}
&=C_{\rm IC}
\int_{\nu_s'<\nu'} d\ln\nu_s'\,n_s
\int d\ln\gamma\,\frac{N_e(\gamma)}{\gamma}
G(q,\eta).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\sigma_{\rm es}\) is the electron-scattering cross section, \(n_s\) is
the seed photon density, and \(N_e(\gamma)\) is the transported electron
spectrum.  For \(\nu_s'\ge\nu'\), precomputed tail moments evaluate the
elastic-scattering branch,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{\rm es}(\nu_s')
&=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\nu_s'}{\nu'}\right)^{1/2},\\
\Phi_{\rm es}(\gamma,\nu_s')
&=\frac{\nu'}{\nu_s'}-\frac{1}{4\gamma^2},\\
L_{\nu'}^{{\rm SSC},>}
&=C_{\rm IC}
\int_{\nu_s'\ge\nu'}d\ln\nu_s'\,n_s
\int_{\gamma>\gamma_{\rm es}}d\ln\gamma\,
\frac{N_e(\gamma)}{\gamma}
\Phi_{\rm es}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Both logarithmic integrals use the Simpson weights above.  The stored SSC photon
density follows from the emitted luminosity,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L_{\nu'}^{\rm SSC}
&=L_{\nu'}^{{\rm SSC},<}+L_{\nu'}^{{\rm SSC},>},\\
n_{\nu'}^{\rm SSC}
&=\frac{L_{\nu'}^{\rm SSC}}{4\pi R^2ch\nu'}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Nonuniform-grid SSC emissivity}

The nonuniform-grid SSC branch computes the same scattering physics on an
electron grid with unequal cell widths.  Let \(x=\log_{10}\gamma\), and let
\(N_e(x)\) be the transported electron spectrum on that grid.  Inside each
electron cell the branch reconstructs \(N_e\) as a linear function of \(x\) and
limits the slope with the minmod operator.  It then uses an \(N_{\rm G}\)-point
Gauss rule in \(\ln\nu_s'\) and in each electron cell that can scatter
\(\nu_s'<\nu'\) seed photons to \(\nu'\)
\citep{Jones1968,BlumenthalGould1970}.  This branch changes the quadrature
support, not the Jones--Klein--Nishina kernel.

\subsection{Finite-cell photon transfer}

\subsubsection{Shell transfer factor}

Photon survival is applied as an average over an emitting shell cell.  A photon
emitted uniformly inside an absorbing cell does not see the same factor as a
photon that crosses a foreground screen.  The finite-cell factor is
\(\psi(\tau)\), defined in Eq.~(\ref{eq:finite-cell-transfer}), with
\(\psi(0)=1\).  The \(\gamma\gamma\) branch uses this same factor after replacing
the synchrotron self-absorption depth by the local pair-production optical
depth.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\(\gamma\gamma\)}{gamma-gamma} shell absorption}

\subsubsection{Soft-photon cell measure}

The pair-absorption kernel uses the target photon density on the seed grid.  For
a photon of comoving frequency \(\nu_1'\) and a target frequency cell
\((\nu_j',\nu_{j+1}')\), define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\epsilon_1&=\frac{h\nu_1'}{m_ec^2},\\
\nu_{2,j}'&=(\nu_j'\nu_{j+1}')^{1/2},\\
\epsilon_{2,j}&=\frac{h\nu_{2,j}'}{m_ec^2},\\
\Delta\nu_j'&=\nu_{2,j}'\Delta\ln\nu' .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\epsilon_1\) and \(\epsilon_{2,j}\) are photon energies in units of
\(m_ec^2\), and \(\Delta\nu_j'\) is the frequency width represented by the
logarithmic target cell.

\subsubsection{Angular pair-production kernel}

The pair-production cross section depends on the collision angle.  The
angle-averaged shell kernel uses midpoint angles
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta\mu&=\frac{2}{N_\mu},\\
\mu_a&=-1+\left(a-\frac{1}{2}\right)\Delta\mu,\\
z_a&=\frac{1-\mu_a}{2},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
so that the pair-production invariant is
\begin{equation}
s_{aj}\equiv\epsilon_1\epsilon_{2,j}z_a .
\end{equation}
Only cells with \(1<s_{aj}<s_{\max}\) enter the tabulated kernel.  The lower
bound is the physical pair-production threshold.  The upper bound \(s_{\max}\)
is the finite domain of the precomputed kernel, not a new physical threshold.
The cross section is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\beta_s&=(1-s^{-1})^{1/2},\\
\Xi(\beta_s)&=(3-\beta_s^4)\ln\frac{1+\beta_s}{1-\beta_s}
-2\beta_s(2-\beta_s^2),\\
\sigma_{\gamma\gamma}(s)&=
\begin{cases}
\dfrac{3\sigma_{\rm es}}{16}(1-\beta_s^2)\Xi(\beta_s), & s>1,\\
0, & s\le1 .
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\sigma_{\rm es}\) is the electron-scattering cross section.

\subsubsection{Shell optical-depth assembly}

The shell optical depth sums the target photons over frequency and angle.  For a
shell at \((R_i,\Gamma_i)\), with \(\beta_i=(1-\Gamma_i^{-2})^{1/2}\), define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta'_{\gamma\gamma,i}&=\frac{R_i}{\eta_{\gamma\gamma}\Gamma_i},\\
W_{ia}&=\Delta\mu(1-\beta_i\mu_a),\\
n_j&=n_{\nu_{2,j}'}^{\rm tar}(R_i),\\
P_{ia}&=\sum_j\sigma_{\gamma\gamma}(s_{aj})n_j\Delta\nu_j',\\
\tau_{\gamma\gamma}(\nu_1',R_i)
&=\frac{\Delta'_{\gamma\gamma,i}}{2}
\sum_{a=1}^{N_\mu}W_{ia}P_{ia}
+\tau_{\rm ex}(\nu_1',R_i).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Delta'_{\gamma\gamma,i}\) is the comoving shell depth,
\(\eta_{\gamma\gamma}\) is its fixed geometry factor, \(W_{ia}\) is the angular
weight, and \(n_j\) is the target photon density in cell \(j\).  The factor
\(1/2\) performs the average over \(-1\le\mu\le1\).  The returned survival factor
is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{A}_{\gamma\gamma}(\nu_1',R_i)
&=\psi[\tau_{\gamma\gamma}(\nu_1',R_i)] .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
It is the survival factor for photons emitted throughout the absorbing cell, not
a foreground screen factor \(e^{-\tau}\)
\citep{GouldSchreder1967,Svensson1987}.

\subsection{Head-on path-opacity helper}

The head-on helper gives a simpler opacity for photon-history transport.  It
keeps only the head-on invariant
\(s_j^{\rm ho}\equiv\epsilon_1\epsilon_{2,j}\).  For a comoving path length
\(\Delta x'\), it computes
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\tau_{\gamma\gamma}^{\rm ho}(\nu_1')=
\Delta x'
\sum_j \sigma_{\gamma\gamma}(s_j^{\rm ho})
n_{\nu_{2,j}'}^{\rm tar}\Delta\nu_j' .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This helper returns a path opacity.  It does not replace the angle-averaged
finite-cell survival calculation used for shell emission.

\subsection{External EBL attenuation}

\subsubsection{Table boundary}

The EBL factor attenuates photons after they leave the burst environment.  It is
therefore kept separate from local shell absorption.  For the default
Domínguez et al. table \citep{Dominguez2011EBL}, the first column gives photon
energy \(E_i\), and the header gives source redshift \(z_j\).  The table energy
is converted to observed frequency by
\(\nu_i=E_i/h\).  The zero-redshift boundary is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\tau_{\rm EBL}(E,0)=0 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This boundary states that no cosmological EBL path exists at zero distance.

\subsubsection{Redshift and frequency interpolation}

The EBL optical depth is interpolated first in redshift and then in observed
frequency.  For a source redshift between \(z_j\) and \(z_{j+1}\), define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\lambda_z&=\frac{z-z_j}{z_{j+1}-z_j},\\
\tau_i(z)&=(1-\lambda_z)\tau_{ij}
+\lambda_z\tau_{i,j+1}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
For an observed frequency between \(\nu_i\) and \(\nu_{i+1}\), define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\lambda_\nu&=\frac{\nu_{\rm obs}-\nu_i}{\nu_{i+1}-\nu_i},\\
\tau_{\rm EBL}(\nu_{\rm obs},z)=
(1-\lambda_\nu)\tau_i(z)+\lambda_\nu\tau_{i+1}(z).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Below the lowest tabulated gamma-ray energy, the EBL optical depth is set to
zero.  Above the maximum tabulated redshift or energy, the selected EBL model is
not defined.

\subsubsection{External attenuation factor}

The external attenuation factor multiplies the observer-side flux.  It is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{A}_{\rm EBL}(\nu_{\rm obs},z)
=\exp[-\tau_{\rm EBL}(\nu_{\rm obs},z)].
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Inputs above the table maximum are rejected because the chosen EBL model gives no
optical depth there.

\subsection{Observer-side photon component assembly}

\subsubsection{Component ordering}

The observer-side photon assembly adds only components that were produced by
active local modules.  The forward synchrotron component is present in the
standard forward-shock run.  Forward SSC is added only when its solver is active.
Reverse-shock synchrotron, reverse SSC, and cross-zone inverse-Compton emission
are added only when the reverse-shock branch supplies them.  Hadronic
luminosities are added after forward synchrotron self-absorption transfer.
Pair-production luminosity and pair seed photons are added only when the pair
branch is active.  This ordering is component accounting; it does not create a
missing physical channel.

\subsubsection{Distance and attenuation prefactor}

The distance and attenuation prefactor converts a local spectral luminosity into
an observed flux contribution before equal-arrival-time interpolation.  For an
active local luminosity \(\widetilde L_{\nu'}\), the code uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal{P}_\nu(R)=
\frac{1+z}{4\pi d_L^2}\,
\mathcal{A}_{\rm abs}(\nu,R),\\
\Delta F_\nu(R)&=\mathcal{P}_\nu(R)\,\widetilde L_{\nu'}(R).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(d_L\) is the luminosity distance and \(\mathcal{A}_{\rm abs}\) contains
the active local survival factors and the external EBL factor.  The projection
module then maps \(\Delta F_\nu(R)\) onto observer time.

\section{Hadronic Transport and Secondary Pairs} \label{sec:hadronic}

\subsection{One-dimensional activation boundary}

\subsubsection{Active physics boundary}

Hadronic transport is a one-dimensional forward-shock feedback branch.  It is
activated only when the hadronic module is enabled and the proton energy fraction
\(\epsilon_p\) is positive.  The active branch evolves protons, optional
secondary species, and photon-survival factors on the same radius grid as the
forward-shock electrons and photons.  The formal feedback path remains
shell-averaged.  Non-1D forward electron solvers are rejected because the target
photon field and secondary feedback are not defined as \(\chi\)-local hadronic
quantities.

\subsubsection{Supported hadronic branches}

The supported hadronic branches are explicit.  The legacy branch transports
protons and computes proton synchrotron emission only.  The formal AM3/Hummer
branch adds \(p\gamma\) interactions, neutrino production, Bethe--Heitler pair
production, hadronic inverse-Compton emission, \(pp\) interactions, secondary
species, and pair-production feedback
\citep{Murase2007,Hummer2010,KelnerAharonian2008,Chodorowski1992,GouldSchreder1967,IceCube2017GRBNeutrinos,Sahu2022GRB190829AHadronic,Sahu2024GRB221009AHadronic}.
Inactive switches remove their operators before observer assembly.

\subsection{Hadronic ABI boundary}

\subsubsection{Package binding}

The hadronic package initializer is an ABI boundary, not a particle-transport
model.  It defines two compiled kernel handles,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
H_{\rm FS}&\equiv\texttt{hadronic\_forward\_1d},\\
H_{\rm RS}&\equiv\texttt{hadronic\_reverse\_1d},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and maps the public names \texttt{hadronic\_1d} and
\texttt{reverse\_hadronic\_1d} to \(H_{\rm FS}\) and \(H_{\rm RS}\),
respectively.  This layer only selects the implemented one-dimensional forward-
or reverse-shock hadronic kernel.  The particle equations, interaction rates,
and coupling terms are defined inside those kernels.

\subsection{Hadronic base grid}

\subsubsection{Log-Lorentz grid}

The hadronic base grid stores particle spectra as functions of Lorentz factor.
All process kernels use the same rest masses for electrons, protons, neutrons,
charged pions, neutral pions, and muons.  This keeps the hadronic rates on the
same mass scale as the radiation and dynamics layers.  Define the logarithmic
coordinate \(x_i=\log_{10}\gamma_i\).  For the formal \(N_\gamma>1\) grid,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta x&=\frac{x_{\max}-x_{\min}}{N_\gamma-1},\\
x_i&=x_{\min}+(i-1)\Delta x,\\
\gamma_i&=10^{x_i},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(x_{\min}=\log_{10}\gamma_{\min}\), \(x_{\max}=\log_{10}\gamma_{\max}\),
\(\gamma_{\min}>1\), and \(\gamma_{\max}>\gamma_{\min}\).  A source term only
acts over its allowed part of the grid.  Its support is stored as
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
i_{\rm L}&=\min\{i\mid \gamma_i\ge\gamma_{\min}^{\rm src}\},\\
i_{\rm H}&=\max\{i\mid \gamma_i\le\gamma_{\max}^{\rm src}\}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
These two indices define the active injection or interaction range; they do not
change the global transport grid.

\subsubsection{Cell faces and shell time}

Hadronic transport uses geometric faces between neighboring Lorentz-factor
cells.  For interior faces,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{i+1/2}=\sqrt{\gamma_i\gamma_{i+1}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and the two boundary faces are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{1/2}=\gamma_1\sqrt{\gamma_1/\gamma_2},\qquad
\gamma_{N_\gamma+1/2}=\gamma_{N_\gamma}
\sqrt{\gamma_{N_\gamma}/\gamma_{N_\gamma-1}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The \(N_\gamma=1\) mode is a low-level diagnostic path and is not used for the
formal transport branch.  The comoving shell time step is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta t_i' =
\begin{cases}
t'_1, & i=1,\\
t'_i-t'_{i-1}, & i>1,
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
with \(\Delta t_i'>0\).  The time \(t'_i\) comes from the stored radius-ordered
shell state, not from the observer grid.  The comoving dynamical time used by the
base module is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
t'_{\rm dyn}=\frac{R}{\Gamma c}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Legacy maximum-energy estimate}

The legacy proton upper bound compares acceleration with dynamical escape and
synchrotron cooling.  The assumed acceleration time is proportional to
\(\eta_{\rm acc}\), where smaller \(\eta_{\rm acc}\) means faster acceleration.
Equating this acceleration time to \(t'_{\rm dyn}\) and to the synchrotron
cooling time gives
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{\rm dyn}
&=\frac{eB't'_{\rm dyn}}{\eta_{\rm acc}m_pc},\\
\gamma_{\rm syn}
&=\frac{m_p}{m_e}
\left(\frac{6\pi e}{\eta_{\rm acc}\sigma_{\rm es}B'}\right)^{1/2},\\
\gamma_{\max}=\min(\gamma_{\rm dyn},\gamma_{\rm syn}) .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(B'\) is the comoving magnetic field, \(m_p\) and \(m_e\) are the proton
and electron masses, and \(\sigma_{\rm es}\) is the electron-scattering cross
section.  Other hadronic kernels use the same base module and require positive,
strictly increasing, logarithmically uniform energy grids.

\subsection{Quantum-synchrotron helper}

\subsubsection{QED invariant}

The quantum-synchrotron helper reduces the classical cooling rate when the
emission approaches the QED regime.  For a species of mass \(m_s\), define
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
B_Q&=\frac{m_e^2c^3}{e\hbar},&
\hbar&=\frac{h}{2\pi},\\
\chi&=\gamma\,\frac{B'}{B_Q}\,\frac{m_e}{m_s}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The multiplicative cooling factor is then
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
f_q(\gamma,B',m_s)=\mathcal{Q}(\chi).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(B_Q\) is the critical QED field.  The same definition is used by the
radiation helper.

\subsubsection{Cooling suppression factor}

The suppression function has a classical branch and a quantum branch.  The
implemented AM3-style factor is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{Q}(\chi)=
\begin{cases}
1, & \chi\le\chi_Q,\\
\left[1+\sqrt{2}\,\chi^{2/3}\right]^{-2}, & \chi>\chi_Q,
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
where \(\chi_Q\) is the small-\(\chi\) threshold used by this tabulated kernel.
The first branch is the classical limit, and the whole factor is set to unity
when the quantum-synchrotron option is disabled \citep{Klinger2024AM3}.

\subsection{Hadronic species state}

\subsubsection{Returned species state}

The active hadronic branch returns the particle and secondary-source state.  The
formal state vector is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
{\cal Y}_h=\{&
N_p,N_n,N_{\pi^0},N_{\pi^\pm},N_{\mu^\pm},\\
&N_\nu,Q_e^{\rm BH},Q_e^{pp},Q_e^{\gamma\gamma}\}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(N_p\), \(N_n\), \(N_\pi\), \(N_\mu\), and \(N_\nu\) are spectra of the
active hadronic species, and the three \(Q_e\) terms are electron-positron
source spectra from Bethe--Heitler, \(pp\), and \(\gamma\gamma\) channels.  These
arrays couple to photons and pairs only through the local target field and
photon-survival factors.

\subsection{Proton process-operator equation}

The proton equation collects injection, losses, and reinjection on the radius
grid.  In compact operator form,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{\partial N_p}{\partial R}
&=Q_{p,R}
-\mathcal{L}_{p,{\rm syn}}
-\mathcal{L}_{p\gamma}
-\mathcal{L}_{\rm BH}
-\mathcal{L}_{pp}
+\mathcal{R}_{p\gamma},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(Q_{p,R}\) is the radial proton source, \(\mathcal{L}_{p,{\rm syn}}\) is
proton synchrotron cooling, \(\mathcal{L}_{p\gamma}\) is photohadronic loss,
\(\mathcal{L}_{\rm BH}\) is Bethe--Heitler loss, \(\mathcal{L}_{pp}\) is
hadronuclear loss, and \(\mathcal{R}_{p\gamma}\) is proton and neutron
reinjection from the photohadronic response.  This equation is compressed only in
notation.  Each term corresponds to a separate implemented process operator, and
inactive switches remove the corresponding operator before observer assembly.

\subsection{Hadronic species identifiers}

\subsubsection{Mass and charge map}

The acceleration helper needs only the species mass and charge.  For the
supported hadrons and leptonic secondaries, it uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
(m_s,Z_s)&\in
\{(m_p,1),(m_n,0),(m_\pi,\pm1),(m_\mu,\pm1)\},\\
|q_s|&=|Z_s|e .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(m_s\) is the rest mass, \(Z_s\) is the charge number, and \(q_s\) is the
charge magnitude.  Neutral species do not enter the acceleration-limit
calculation because \(q_s=0\).

\subsection{Forward-shock proton energy budget}

The forward-shock proton source is normalized by the energy available in one
radial shell.  For shell \(i\), the swept volume and nonthermal proton energy are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta V_i&=\frac{4\pi}{3}(R_i^3-R_{i-1}^3),\\
E_{p,i}&=
\epsilon_p(\Gamma_i-1)\Delta V_i n(R_i)m_pc^2,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
with \(R_0=0\).  Here \(\epsilon_p\) is the proton energy fraction,
\(\Gamma_i-1\) is the kinetic energy per swept proton in units of \(m_pc^2\), and
\(n(R_i)\Delta V_i\) is the swept proton number in the shell.  This budget
normalizes the proton source in the forward-shock hadronic branch.

\subsection{Hadronic injection spectrum}

The hadronic injection spectrum gives the shape before normalization.  For a
comoving species luminosity \(L_s'=E_{s,i}/\Delta t'_i\), define the optional
cutoff factor
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
C_s(\gamma)&=\exp[-I_c\gamma/\gamma_{\rm cut}],&
I_c&\in\{0,1\},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(I_c=1\) only when the exponential cutoff is active.  The unnormalized
shape is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
S_s(\gamma)=
\begin{cases}
\gamma^{-p_s}C_s(\gamma),&
\gamma_{\min}\le\gamma\le\gamma_{\rm inj},\\
0, & \gamma<\gamma_{\min}\ {\rm or}\ \gamma>\gamma_{\rm inj},
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(p_s\) is the injection index and \(\gamma_{\rm inj}\) is the upper
support of the injected particles.

\subsection{Hadronic source normalization}

The source normalization uses the relativistic energy moment implemented in the
kernel.  The moment and normalized source are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
A_s&=\int S_s(\gamma)\gamma\,d\gamma,\\
Q_s(\gamma)=
\frac{L_s'S_s(\gamma)}{m_sc^2A_s}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This normalization enforces
\(m_sc^2\int Q_s(\gamma)\gamma\,d\gamma=L_s'\).  The integral is evaluated by
the trapezoidal rule on the same Lorentz-factor grid.  The injected shell
content is \(Q_s\Delta t'_i\).

\subsection{Hadronic acceleration limits}

The upper species energy is set by comparing acceleration with loss and escape
times.  For charged species, the acceleration and synchrotron times are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
t'_{\rm acc}
&=\eta_{\rm acc}\frac{\gamma m_sc}{|q_s|B'},\\
t'_{\rm syn}
&=\frac{6\pi m_s^3c}
{\sigma_{\rm es}m_e^2B'^2\gamma},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and \(t'_{\rm dyn}=R/(\Gamma c)\).  Equating \(t'_{\rm acc}\) to the dynamical
and synchrotron times gives
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{\rm dyn}
&=\frac{|q_s|B'R}{\eta_{\rm acc}\Gamma m_sc^2},\\
\gamma_{\rm syn}
&=\left[\frac{6\pi |q_s|m_s^2}
{\eta_{\rm acc}\sigma_{\rm es}m_e^2B'}\right]^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\eta_{\rm acc}\) is the acceleration parameter, \(B'\) is the comoving
magnetic field, and \(\sigma_{\rm es}\) is the electron-scattering cross section.

\subsection{External hadronic cooling limit}

The external cooling table can add one more upper-energy bound.  If such a table
is supplied, define the positive cooling rate
\(\mathcal{C}_{{\rm ext},i}\equiv
-(d\gamma/dt')_{\rm ext}|_{\gamma_i}>0\).  The corresponding cooling time is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
t'_{\rm ext}(\gamma_i)=\frac{\gamma_i}{\mathcal{C}_{{\rm ext},i}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and the crossing of \(t'_{\rm acc}\) with \(t'_{\rm ext}\) gives
\(\gamma_{\rm ext}\).  The crossing is obtained by linear interpolation of
\(\ln(t'_{\rm acc}/t'_{\rm ext})\) against \(\ln\gamma\) on the supplied grid.
The accepted upper limit is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{\max}=
\begin{cases}
\min(\gamma_{\rm dyn},\gamma_{\rm syn},\gamma_{\rm ext}),&
\gamma_{\rm ext}\ {\rm exists},\\
\min(\gamma_{\rm dyn},\gamma_{\rm syn}),&
\gamma_{\rm ext}\ {\rm is\ absent}.
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This bound sets the top of the species grid used by the active hadronic source.

\subsection{Bethe-Heitler pair-production kernel}

\subsubsection{Kinematic variables and threshold}

The Bethe--Heitler branch describes pair creation by a relativistic proton and a
target photon.  For a proton energy \(E_p\), a photon energy \(\epsilon\), and a
secondary lepton energy \(E_e\), the branch first forms the dimensionless
variables
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_p&\equiv \frac{E_p}{m_pc^2},\\
x_\gamma&\equiv \frac{\epsilon}{m_ec^2},\\
x_e&\equiv \frac{E_e}{m_ec^2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(m_p\) and \(m_e\) are the proton and electron masses.  The interaction is
allowed only when the proton-frame photon energy is high enough to make a pair,
\(\gamma_px_\gamma>1\).

\subsubsection{Differential pair kernel}

The differential pair kernel gives the number of pairs produced at one lepton
energy by one proton-photon cell.  For an allowed cell, ASGARD integrates the
Blumenthal--Gould Bethe--Heitler cross section over the proton-frame photon
energy \(\omega\) and the intermediate lepton energy \(\bar E\)
\citep{BlumenthalGould1970}:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
C_{\rm BH}&\equiv \frac{x_e}{2\gamma_p^3x_\gamma^2},\\
I_{\rm BH}
&\equiv \int_{\omega_{\min}}^{\omega_{\max}} d\omega
\int_{\bar E_{\min}}^{\omega-1} d\bar E\,
\frac{\omega\,\sigma_{\rm BH}(\omega,\bar E,\xi)}{\bar p},\\
\mathcal{K}_{\rm BH}(x_e,\gamma_p,x_\gamma)
&\equiv C_{\rm BH}I_{\rm BH}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The prefactor \(C_{\rm BH}\) converts the integral to the chosen lepton-energy
variable, and \(\mathcal{K}_{\rm BH}\) is the cell kernel used by the source and
loss sums.  The integration limits and local angular variable are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\omega_{\max}&=2\gamma_px_\gamma,\\
\omega_{\min}&=\frac{(\gamma_p+x_e)^2}{2\gamma_px_e},\\
\bar E_{\min}&=\frac{\gamma_p^2+x_e^2}{2\gamma_px_e},\\
\bar p&=(\bar E^2-1)^{1/2},\\
\xi&=\frac{\gamma_p\bar E-x_e}{\gamma_p\bar p}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Fit-domain and quadrature controls}

The Bethe--Heitler cross-section fit has a finite photon-energy domain.  ASGARD
evaluates \(\sigma_{\rm BH}\) only for
\(\omega_{\rm BH,L}<\omega<\omega_{\rm BH,H}\), and the differential kernel is
zero outside that stated fit domain.  The two integrals are then evaluated with
fixed quadrature controls \(N_\omega\) and \(N_{\bar E}\), which set the
resolution in \(\omega\) and \(\bar E\).

\subsubsection{Secondary-pair source}

The secondary-pair source adds the Bethe--Heitler leptons to the electron and
positron grid.  On the log-uniform proton and photon grids, ASGARD writes
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
Y_i&\equiv E_{p,i}N_p(E_{p,i}),\\
Z_k&\equiv \epsilon_k n_\gamma(\epsilon_k),\\
K_{jik}&\equiv
\mathcal{K}_{\rm BH}(x_{e,j},\gamma_{p,i},x_{\gamma,k}),\\
k_{\rm BH}&\equiv \frac{3c\sigma_{\rm es}\alpha_{\rm f}}{16\pi},\\
Q_e^{\rm BH}(E_{e,j})
&=\frac{k_{\rm BH}\Delta\ln E_p\,\Delta\ln\epsilon}{E_{e,j}}
\sum_{i,k}Y_iZ_kK_{jik}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(N_p\) is the proton spectrum, \(n_\gamma\) is the target photon density,
\(\alpha_{\rm f}\) is the fine-structure constant, and
\(\sigma_{\rm es}\) is the electron-scattering cross section.  The output
\(Q_e^{\rm BH}\) is a shell-local \(e^\pm\) injection rate per lepton energy.

\subsubsection{Target-photon sink}

The target-photon sink removes the photons that take part in the same
Bethe--Heitler interactions.  For each photon bin,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
D_k&\equiv \epsilon_k n_\gamma(\epsilon_k),\\
\Lambda_{\gamma}^{\rm BH}(\epsilon_k)
&=\frac{k_{\rm BH}\Delta\ln E_p\,\Delta\ln E_e}{D_k}
\sum_{i,j}Y_iZ_kK_{jik}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The positive rate \(\Lambda_{\gamma}^{\rm BH}\) is later converted to the
optical depth of the shell cell and to the corresponding photon survival
factor.

\subsubsection{Proton cooling rate}

The proton cooling rate records the proton energy lost in Bethe--Heitler
interactions.  ASGARD uses the Chodorowski et al. energy-loss kernel
\(\phi_{\rm C}\) \citep{Chodorowski1992} and writes the positive rate as
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Phi_k^{\rm C}
&\equiv \frac{\phi_{\rm C}(2\gamma_px_{\gamma,k})}
{2\gamma_p^2x_{\gamma,k}^2},\\
k_{p{\rm BH}}&\equiv
\frac{3\alpha_{\rm f}\sigma_{\rm es}c}{8\pi}\frac{m_e}{m_p},\\
\mathcal{C}_p^{\rm BH}(\gamma_p)
&\equiv -\left(\frac{d\gamma_p}{dt'}\right)_{\rm BH}\\
&=k_{p{\rm BH}}\Delta\ln\epsilon
\sum_k\Phi_k^{\rm C}Z_k.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The rate \(\mathcal{C}_p^{\rm BH}\) enters the proton transport equation as a
loss term.

\subsubsection{Chodorowski loss fit}

The loss kernel uses the Chodorowski et al. piecewise fit.  With
\(x_{\rm th}=2\), the fitted function is
\begin{equation}
\phi_{\rm C}(x)=
\begin{cases}
0, & x<x_{\rm th},\\
\Phi_{\rm L}(x;\mathbf{a}_{\rm C}), & x_{\rm th}\le x<x_{\rm C},\\
\Phi_{\rm H}(x;\mathbf{b}_{\rm C}), & x\ge x_{\rm C},
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
where \(x_{\rm C}\) is the matching point between the low- and high-energy
branches.  The coefficient sets \(\mathbf{a}_{\rm C}\) and
\(\mathbf{b}_{\rm C}\) are the published fit parameters.  The Bethe--Heitler
branch therefore returns three shell-local quantities: a secondary \(e^\pm\)
source, a positive proton cooling rate \(\mathcal{C}_p^{\rm BH}\), and a
positive target-photon loss rate \(\Lambda_{\gamma}^{\rm BH}\).

\subsection{Proton transport operator}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Log-\(\gamma_p\)}{Log-gamma-p} conservation law}

The proton transport operator evolves the shell-local proton spectrum on the
same logarithmic Lorentz-factor grid used by the hadronic source terms.  The
cooling terms move particles from high \(\gamma_p\) to low \(\gamma_p\), so we
write all losses as a positive rate,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
C_p(\gamma_p)&\equiv
-\left(\frac{d\gamma_p}{dt'}\right)_{\rm loss},\\
\frac{\partial N_p}{\partial t'}
&-\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma_p}
\left(C_pN_p\right)
=Q_p+Q_{p,{\rm r}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(N_p=dN_p/d\gamma_p\), \(Q_p\) is the primary proton source, and
\(Q_{p,{\rm r}}\) is the proton source returned by the \(p\gamma\) response.
This conservation law sets the upwind energy-space flux used in the proton
update.

\subsubsection{Baseline proton losses}

The baseline proton losses are adiabatic cooling and proton synchrotron cooling.
Before any collision operator is added, ASGARD uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
C_{\rm ad}(\gamma_p)&=\frac{\gamma_p}{t'_{\rm dyn}},\\
C_{\rm syn}&=
\frac{\sigma_{\rm es} B'^2}{6\pi m_ec}
\left(\frac{m_e}{m_p}\right)^3\gamma_p^2.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(t'_{\rm dyn}\) is the comoving shell expansion time and \(B'\) is the
comoving magnetic field.  Bethe--Heitler, \(pp\), and \(p\gamma\) cooling rates
are added only when the corresponding physics branch is active, and the summed
\(C_p\) is then used in the conservative flux update.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\(p\gamma\)}{p-gamma} shell survival operator}

The \(p\gamma\) survival operator controls how many target photons remain inside
one shell cell.  ASGARD uses two passes: the first pass acts on the
unattenuated target photon density and returns the photon-loss depth, and the
second pass acts on the survived photon field and returns interaction rates,
secondary yields, and \(Q_{p,{\rm r}}\).  With
\(\tau_g\equiv\tau_\gamma(\nu',R)\), the finite-cell survival factor is
\begin{equation}
S_\gamma(\nu',R)=
\frac{1-e^{-\tau_g}}{\tau_g},
\end{equation}
with \(S_\gamma\rightarrow1\) as \(\tau_g\rightarrow0\).  This factor is the
cell-averaged photon density seen by the second pass.  The corresponding
threshold and shell-transfer semantics are shown in
Appendix~\ref{app:hadronic-figure}; they define the branch boundary and are not
used as a main-text result claim.

\subsection{Secondary-species source operators}

Secondary-species source operators convert hadronic interactions into local
particle source terms.  These transported secondary species are supported only
in the formal one-dimensional path.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\(p\gamma\)}{p-gamma} response products}

The \(p\gamma\) response products are the particles produced after target-photon
survival has been applied.  The survived-field response generates neutral-pion
gamma rays, charged pions, muons, neutrinos, neutron reinjection, and proton
reinjection.  These products are local shell outputs before observer projection;
the neutrino arrays are not interpreted as detection predictions without a
separate exposure calculation.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\(pp\)}{pp} delta source}

The \(pp\) source operator supplies a compact pion source when baryonic shell
targets are active.  The branch uses a delta-approximation with the fixed
parameter set
\begin{equation}
\left(\kappa_{pp},\xi_\pi,f_{\pi^0}\right),
\end{equation}
where \(\kappa_{pp}\) is the inelasticity, \(\xi_\pi\) is the pion energy
fraction, and \(f_{\pi^0}\) is the neutral-pion fraction.  The output is a local
secondary source, not an independent radiation component.

\subsubsection{Bethe-Heitler pair handoff}

The Bethe--Heitler handoff tells the code where the produced pairs are counted.
Bethe--Heitler and \(pp\) pairs are accumulated as a secondary electron source.
In the separated branch, Bethe--Heitler secondary electrons can be merged into
the forward electron distribution.  In that case, the separate Bethe--Heitler
luminosity is not assembled, so the same injected energy is counted in one
place.

\subsection{Joint electron--photon--hadron feedback iteration}

\subsubsection{Local fixed-point sequence}

The joint feedback branch updates electrons, photons, and hadrons inside one
shell before observer projection.  One local iteration has the order
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
N_e^{(r)}
&\rightarrow n_\gamma^{(r)}
\rightarrow
\left\{Q_e^{{\rm sec},(r)},\tau_{\gamma\gamma}^{(r)}\right\}
\rightarrow N_e^{(r+1)}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(r\) is the local iteration index.  The secondary source enters
\(Q_e^{\rm sec}\), the updated electron solution rebuilds the photon field, and
the hadronic operators are evaluated again on the survived target field.  The
observer calculation sees only the state after this local sequence has been
completed.

\subsubsection{Cascade branch boundary}

The cascade boundary states which electromagnetic feedback path is supported.
When the requested cascade count satisfies \(N_{\rm cas}>1\), ASGARD uses the
shell-sequence time-dependent \(\gamma\gamma\) pair/synchrotron cascade path.
The single-shell path is a diagnostic mode, and this branch does not implement
an inverse-Compton-mediated electromagnetic cascade.

\subsection{Shell-sequence pair-cascade algorithm}

\subsubsection{Pair grid and escape time}

The shell-sequence cascade evolves photons and secondary pairs inside each shell
before observer projection.  The calculation first writes the seed photon field
as \(n_\epsilon=dN_\gamma/d\epsilon\), then places the pairs on an energy grid
aligned with the photon grid:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
E_{e,j}&\equiv E_{\gamma,1}\exp[(j-1+j_0)\Delta\ln E],\\
E_{e,1}&\ge m_ec^2,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(E_{\gamma,1}\) is the first photon-bin energy, \(\Delta\ln E\) is the
common logarithmic spacing, and \(j_0\) is the alignment offset.  In shell \(i\),
photons remain in the shell for the escape time
\begin{equation}
t'_{{\rm esc},i}=\frac{R_i}{\eta_{\rm esc}\Gamma_i c},
\end{equation}
where \(R_i\) and \(\Gamma_i\) are the shell radius and Lorentz factor, and
\(\eta_{\rm esc}\) is the fixed shell-escape geometry factor.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\(\gamma\gamma\)}{gamma-gamma} source evaluation}

The \(\gamma\gamma\) source evaluation converts absorbed photons into secondary
pairs.  At substep \(m\), the current photon field gives
\begin{equation}
n_\epsilon^{(m)}
\rightarrow
\left\{\lambda_{\gamma\gamma}(\epsilon),
q_{\pm,\gamma\gamma}(E_e),P_{\rm abs},P_{\rm inj}\right\},
\end{equation}
where \(\lambda_{\gamma\gamma}\) is the local photon loss rate,
\(q_{\pm,\gamma\gamma}\) is the combined \(e^-+e^+\) injection rate per energy,
and \(P_{\rm abs}\) and \(P_{\rm inj}\) are the absorbed and injected powers used
for diagnostics.  The pair source added in one substep is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
S_{\pm,j}^{(m)}&=\Delta V_i\,m_ec^2\,
q_{\pm,\gamma\gamma}(E_{e,j})\,\Delta t'_{\rm sub},\\
\Delta t'_{\rm sub}&=\frac{\Delta t'_i}{N_{\rm sub}} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Delta V_i\) is the shell volume, \(\Delta t'_i\) is the comoving shell
time width, and \(N_{\rm sub}\) is the declared cascade substep count.

\subsubsection{Finite-grid pair cooling}

The finite-grid pair-cooling step moves the newly injected pairs to lower
Lorentz factor.  In shell \(i\), the positive cooling rate is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
t'_{{\rm dyn},i}&=\frac{R_i}{\Gamma_i c},\\
C_{{\rm syn},i}(\gamma)
&=\frac{\sigma_{\rm es} B_i'^2}{6\pi m_ec}\gamma^2,\\
\mathcal{C}_{\pm,i}(\gamma)
&\equiv -\left(\frac{d\gamma}{dt'}\right)_{\pm,i}\\
&=C_{{\rm syn},i}(\gamma)+\frac{\gamma}{t'_{{\rm dyn},i}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(t'_{{\rm dyn},i}\) is the comoving expansion time and \(B_i'\) is the
comoving magnetic field.  The discrete update stores the pair content in a cell
as \(C_j=N_j\Delta\gamma_j\), cools it to
\begin{equation}
\gamma_j^{\rm c}=\gamma_j-
\left[
\frac{\sigma_{\rm es} B_i'^2}{6\pi m_ec}\gamma_j^2
+\frac{\gamma_j}{t'_{{\rm dyn},i}}
\right]\Delta t'_{\rm sub},
\end{equation}
and deposits that content in the finite cell that contains
\(\gamma_j^{\rm c}\).  After deposition and injection, the updated cell density
is
\begin{equation}
N_j^{(m+1)}=
\frac{1}{\Delta\gamma_j}
\sum_{\ell\rightarrow j} C_\ell^{(m)}
+S_{\pm,j}^{(m)}.
\end{equation}
If \(\gamma_j^{\rm c}\) lies outside the finite pair grid, that content leaves
the represented cascade population.

\subsubsection{Photon-density transfer}

The photon-density transfer closes the local pair-photon loop in one shell.  The
updated pair distribution is passed to the same synchrotron state operator used
by the electron solver, producing a pair synchrotron luminosity
\(L_{\nu',\pm}'\) and a local seed photon density \(n_{\nu',\pm}'\).  The photon
field then solves the constant-coefficient substep equation
\begin{equation}
\frac{dn_\epsilon}{dt'}=
S_\epsilon-\Lambda_\epsilon n_\epsilon,
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Lambda_\epsilon&=\lambda_{\gamma\gamma}(\epsilon)+\frac{1}{t'_{\rm esc}},\\
S_\epsilon&=\frac{n_{\epsilon,{\rm prim}}+n_{\epsilon,\pm}}{t'_{\rm esc}},\\
n_{\epsilon,\pm}&=\frac{n_{\nu',\pm}'}{h},\\
A_\epsilon&=\exp(-\Lambda_\epsilon\Delta t'_{\rm sub}),\\
n_\epsilon^{(m+1)}=
A_\epsilon n_\epsilon^{(m)}
+\frac{S_\epsilon}{\Lambda_\epsilon}(1-A_\epsilon).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(n_{\epsilon,{\rm prim}}\) is the primary shell photon density, and
\(n_{\epsilon,\pm}\) is the photon density emitted by the cascade pairs.  The
recorded local optical depth is
\(\tau_{\gamma\gamma}=\lambda_{\gamma\gamma}t'_{\rm esc}\).

\subsection{Single-shell pair-synchrotron diagnostic}

\subsubsection{Cooling track}

The single-shell diagnostic follows one injected pair population without the
shell-sequence photon update.  It cools a pair from \(\gamma_{\rm inj}\) to
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{\rm cool}&=
\frac{\gamma_{\rm inj}}
{1+a_B\gamma_{\rm inj}t'},\qquad
a_B&=\frac{\sigma_{\rm es}B'^2}{6\pi m_ec},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and deposits \(dN/d\gamma\propto(a_B\gamma^2)^{-1}\) along that cooling track.
This path is a diagnostic of the synchrotron cooling kernel, not the formal
shell-sequence cascade.

\subsubsection{Diagnostic emissivity}

The diagnostic emissivity maps the cooled pairs to synchrotron photons.  It uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
C_\nu&=\frac{3e}{4\pi m_ec},\\
\nu_s&=C_\nu B'\gamma^2,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and the single-particle approximation
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
P_\nu&\simeq C_PB'\Phi_{\rm d}(\nu/\nu_s),\\
C_P&=\frac{\sqrt{3}e^3}{m_ec^2},\\
\Phi_{\rm d}(x)&=A_{\rm d}x^{1/3}e^{-x}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(A_{\rm d}\) is the fixed diagnostic kernel coefficient.

\section{Reverse Shocks and Density-Jump Secondary Shocks} \label{sec:reverse-shock}

\subsection{Primary ejecta state and activation}

\subsubsection{Activation boundary}

The reverse-shock branch needs a finite ejecta shell.  ASGARD enables this
branch only when the engine duration is supplied, because that duration sets the
shell width and the upstream region-4 density.  We denote the initial ejecta
Lorentz factor by \(\Gamma_0\); the runtime field \(\eta_0\) is this same
quantity.

\subsubsection{Ejecta shell state}

The ejecta shell state gives the mass and density that the reverse shock sees.
For an engine duration \(\Delta t_{\rm eng}\), ASGARD uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta_0&\equiv c\Delta t_{\rm eng},\\
M_{\rm ej}&\equiv \frac{E_{\rm iso}}{(1+\sigma)\Gamma_0c^2},\\
\Delta(R)&\equiv \max\left(\Delta_0,\frac{R}{\Gamma_0^2}\right),\\
n_4(R)&\equiv \frac{M_{\rm ej}}
{4\pi m_pR^2\Gamma_0\Delta(R)} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Delta_0\) is the initial shell width, \(M_{\rm ej}\) is the baryonic
ejecta mass, and \(n_4\) is the upstream baryon density in region 4.  The total
ejecta energy is \(E_{\rm iso}\).  A finite magnetization leaves only the matter
fraction \((1+\sigma)^{-1}\) in baryons, but the unshocked shell still carries
matter plus magnetic inertia.  ASGARD therefore uses \(n_4\) for shock heating
and particle injection, and uses
\(\tilde n_4=(1+\sigma)n_4\) for the inertia of unprocessed magnetized ejecta.

\subsubsection{Shock-front relative Lorentz factor}

The reverse-shock electron source is set by the local shock-front relative
Lorentz factor.  If \(\Gamma_2\) is the shocked-shell Lorentz factor and
\(\Gamma_0\) is the unshocked ejecta Lorentz factor, then
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
u_2&=(\Gamma_2^2-1)^{1/2},\\
u_0&=(\Gamma_0^2-1)^{1/2},\\
\gamma_{34}&=\Gamma_2\Gamma_0-u_2u_0.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The quantity \(\gamma_{34}\) sets the reverse-shock injection energy scale.  It
is evaluated at the shock front and is not reconstructed from observer light
curves.

\subsubsection{Magnetized activation criteria}

Magnetized ejecta form a reverse shock only after two local conditions are met.
The forward-shock pressure must exceed the upstream magnetic pressure, and the
upstream flow must be super-fast-magnetosonic in the reverse-shock frame.  The
pressure condition is written as
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
P_2&\equiv
\frac{(4\Gamma_2+3)(\Gamma_2-1)n_1m_pc^2}{3},\\
\sigma_{\rm cr}&\equiv \frac{2P_2}{n_4m_pc^2},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(P_2\) is the region-2 pressure estimate and \(n_1\) is the external
upstream density.  The reverse shock is active only when
\begin{equation}
\sigma_{\rm cr}\ge\sigma,\qquad u_{4s}^2>\sigma ,
\end{equation}
where \(u_{4s}\) is the region-4 upstream four-speed in the reverse-shock frame.
The first inequality is a pressure criterion, while the second is the
fast-magnetosonic criterion.

\subsubsection{Waiting-state inertia}

The waiting state carries ejecta inertia without adding reverse-shock heat.
Before the activation checks pass, \(dM_3/dR=0\), no region-3 thermal energy is
added, and the ordered region-3 field is zero.  The decelerating forward shell
still feels the causally connected part of the unshocked ejecta,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
M_{\rm w}&\equiv
M_{\rm ej}(1+\sigma)\frac{\Gamma_0}{\Gamma_0-1}f_{\rm f},\\
\beta_{\rm f}&\equiv \left(\frac{\sigma}{1+\sigma}\right)^{1/2},\\
f_{\rm f}&\equiv \min\left[
1,\,
\frac{R\beta_{\rm f}}
{\Gamma_0^2(1-\beta_4\beta_{\rm f})\beta_4\Delta(R)}
\right].
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(M_{\rm w}\) is the waiting ejecta inertia, \(\beta_{\rm f}\) is the
fast-magnetosonic speed in units of \(c\), \(f_{\rm f}\) is the connected shell
fraction, and \(\beta_4\) is the region-4 speed.  The waiting-stage Lorentz
factor is then evolved with
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\Gamma_2}{dR}
=-\frac{u_2^2\,dM_2/dR}
{M_{\rm w}+[\epsilon_2+2(1-\epsilon_2)\Gamma_2]M_2}.
\end{equation}
This equation uses the forward swept mass \(M_2\) and radiative efficiency
\(\epsilon_2\), but no reverse-shock source terms.  The transition from the
waiting state to the active reverse shock is located inside the current radial
step, so the first active reverse-shock state is tied to the activation
condition rather than to the next stored output radius.  The reverse-shock
framework follows early-afterglow thin-shell/reverse-shock treatments and the
magnetized-ejecta notation used in Zhang--Kobayashi-type calculations, while
using \vegas\ only as a jump-condition comparison backend
\citep{SariPiran1999,Kobayashi2000,ZhangKobayashi2005,PeerWijers2006,Giannios2008,Wang2025VegasAfterglow}.

\subsection{Finite-strength MHD jump solver}

\subsubsection{Cold upstream variables}

The MHD jump solver gives the local compression and heat of a magnetized reverse
shock.  The upstream shell is cold and carries a transverse ordered magnetic
field, so the local relative Lorentz factor is \(g=\gamma_{34}\).  ASGARD first
defines
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
g&\equiv \gamma_{34},\\
\hat{\gamma}&\equiv \frac{4}{3}+\frac{1}{3g},\\
h_1&\equiv \hat{\gamma}-1,\\
h_2&\equiv \hat{\gamma}-2 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\hat{\gamma}\) is the local adiabatic-index proxy used by the jump
relation.  For \(\sigma>\sigma_{\rm cut}\), the downstream shock-frame
four-speed is obtained from \(y=u_d^2\) by solving
\begin{equation}
Ay^3+By^2+Cy+D=0
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
A={}&-\hat{\gamma}h_2(g-1)+2,\\
B={}&-(g+1)\left[-h_2(\hat{\gamma}g^2+1)+\hat{\gamma}h_1g\right]\sigma\\
&-(g-1)\left[-\hat{\gamma}h_2(g^2-2)+2g+3\right],\\
C={}&(g+1)\left[\hat{\gamma}\left(1-\frac{\hat{\gamma}}{4}\right)
(g^2-1)+1\right]\sigma^2\\
&+(g^2-1)\left[2g+h_2(\hat{\gamma}g-1)\right]\sigma
+(g+1)(g-1)^2h_1^2,\\
D={}&-\frac{1}{4}(g-1)(g+1)^2h_2^2\sigma^2 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
\(\sigma_{\rm cut}\) is a fixed algorithmic branch threshold that selects the
MHD cubic; it is not a physical magnetization boundary.

\subsubsection{Cubic root selection}

The cubic root selection chooses the physical downstream four-speed.  The
polynomial is reduced to a depressed cubic with
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
b&\equiv \frac{B}{A},\\
c_1&\equiv \frac{C}{A},\\
d_1&\equiv \frac{D}{A},\\
p_c&\equiv c_1-\frac{b^2}{3},\\
q_c&\equiv \frac{2b^3}{27}-\frac{bc_1}{3}+d_1,\\
a_c&\equiv \left(-\frac{p_c}{3}\right)^{1/2},\\
\psi_c&\equiv
\frac{1}{3}\cos^{-1}\!\left(\frac{3q_c}{2p_ca_c}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The branch used by the reverse-shock solver is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
y&=2a_c\cos\left(\psi_c-\frac{2\pi}{3}\right)-\frac{b}{3},\\
u_d&=y^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This is the finite positive downstream four-speed root.  The other algebraic
roots are not used in the shell dynamics.

\subsubsection{Compression ratio}

The compression ratio follows from the upstream and downstream shock-frame
four-speeds.  Once \(u_d\) is known, the upstream four-speed is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
u_u&=\left[(1+u_d^2)(g^2-1)\right]^{1/2}+u_dg,\\
r_\sigma&=\frac{u_u}{u_d}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(r_\sigma\) is the active compression ratio used to compress the ordered
upstream magnetic field.

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Low-\(\sigma\)}{Low-sigma} hydrodynamic branch}

The low-\(\sigma\) branch uses a finite-strength hydrodynamic jump rather than
the ultra-relativistic compression approximation.  For
\(\sigma\le\sigma_{\rm cut}\), ASGARD uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
y_{\rm h}&=
\frac{(g-1)(\hat{\gamma}-1)^2}
{-\hat{\gamma}(\hat{\gamma}-2)(g-1)+2},\\
u_d&=y_{\rm h}^{1/2},\\
r_0&=4g .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The thermal helper returns \(g-1\) only for exactly unmagnetized ejecta.  For
\(0<\sigma\le\sigma_{\rm cut}\), it evaluates the finite-\(\sigma\) enthalpy
with \(y_{\rm h}\) and approaches \(g-1\) continuously.  The active dynamics
therefore do not use the ultra-relativistic \(4g+3\) compression approximation.
This MHD jump is used only by the primary ejecta reverse shock.  The
density-jump secondary branches below remain hydrodynamic diagnostics and do not
carry ordered-field compression or magnetic enthalpy feedback.

\subsection{Comoving region-3 field and heat state}

\subsubsection{Upstream ordered field}

The upstream ordered field is the magnetic field carried by the unshocked ejecta.
All magnetic fields in this subsection are comoving.  For the region-4 baryon
density \(n_4\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\rho_4&\equiv m_pn_4,\\
B'_4&\equiv (4\pi\sigma\rho_4c^2)^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\rho_4\) is the comoving baryon mass density and \(B'_4\) is the ordered
upstream field before compression by the reverse shock.

\subsubsection{Turbulent and ordered region-3 field}

The region-3 magnetic field has a turbulent part and a compressed ordered part.
ASGARD stores them as
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
B_{\rm t}^{\prime 2}&\equiv 8\pi\epsilon_{B,r}\frac{U_3}{V_3},\\
B'_{\rm o}&=r_\sigma B'_4 \qquad \hbox{before crossing},\\
B'_3&=\left(B_{\rm t}^{\prime 2}+B_{\rm o}^{\prime 2}\right)^{1/2},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(U_3\) and \(V_3\) are the region-3 internal energy and comoving volume.
The turbulent field \(B'_{\rm t}\) controls local synchrotron cooling.  The
ordered field \(B'_{\rm o}\) is also included in the bulk inertia through the
magnetic enthalpy of region 3.  The total field \(B'_3\) is the field reported
for the reverse-shock radiation state.

\subsubsection{Post-crossing ordered-field advection}

The post-crossing ordered field is advected after the reverse shock has crossed
the ejecta.  The stored crossing value is carried with the region-3 volume as
\begin{equation}
B'_{\rm o}(R)=B'_{{\rm o},\times}
\frac{V_{3,\times}}{V_3(R)}
\frac{R}{R_\times}.
\end{equation}
Here \(R_\times\), \(V_{3,\times}\), and \(B'_{{\rm o},\times}\) are the radius,
volume, and ordered field stored at shell crossing.  The crossing field is an
event-register state, not a post-processing fit parameter.

\subsubsection{Magnetic enthalpy inertia}

The magnetic enthalpy inertia adds the ordered-field energy to the reverse-shock
dynamics.  For the ordered component,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
p_B&\equiv \frac{B_{\rm o}^{\prime 2}}{8\pi},\\
E_B&\equiv p_BV_3 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The inertia added to the \(\Gamma_2\) equation is
\begin{equation}
M_B=\frac{E_B+p_BV_3}{c^2}+\frac{p_BV_3}{\Gamma_2^2c^2}.
\end{equation}
The first term is the magnetic enthalpy contribution.  The second term is the
finite-\(\Gamma_2\) pressure contribution kept by the shell equation.

\subsubsection{Heat state from the jump}

The heat state from the jump gives the thermal energy available for reverse-shock
electrons.  ASGARD uses the dimensionless heat
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\zeta_3&\equiv\frac{U_3}{M_3c^2},\\
\gamma_u&\equiv(1+u_u^2)^{1/2},\\
\gamma_d&\equiv(1+u_d^2)^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(M_3\) is the shocked ejecta mass, while \(u_u\) and \(u_d\) are the
upstream and downstream shock-frame four-speeds from the jump solution.  During
active pre-crossing,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\zeta_3&=\frac{h_d-1}{\hat{\gamma}},\\
h_d&\equiv (1+\sigma)\frac{\gamma_u}{\gamma_d}-r_\sigma\sigma .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Thus \(\zeta_3\), not \(\gamma_{34}-1\), sets the reverse-shock heat when
magnetization is active.  The thermal-response factor in the Lorentz equation is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal R_3=
\begin{cases}
1, & \sigma=0,\\
\zeta_3/(\gamma_{34}-1), & \sigma>0,
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
The MHD jump therefore changes two pieces of the primary reverse-shock dynamics:
the explicit magnetic inertia \(M_B\), and the coupling between shocked-ejecta
heat and bulk motion through \(\mathcal R_3\).

\subsection{Primary reverse-shock ODE}

\subsubsection{State vector and normalization}

The primary reverse-shock ODE evolves the shocked forward shell and the shocked
ejecta together.  Its state vector contains the bulk Lorentz factor, radius,
swept external mass, shocked ejecta mass, shocked ejecta heat, and shocked
ejecta volume:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
Y_{\rm R}&\equiv
\left(\Gamma_2,R,M_2,\frac{M_3}{M_{\rm ej}},
\frac{U_3}{M_{\rm ej}c^2},\frac{V_3}{V_0},\ldots\right),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where the forward swept mass and the volume normalization are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dM_2}{dR}&=4\pi R^2m_pn_1,\\
V_0&\equiv \frac{M_{\rm ej}}{n_{3,0}m_p}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(n_1\) is the external density and \(V_0\) is only the comoving-volume
normalization used by the integrator.  The first six time derivatives are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dY_1}{dt}&=\frac{d\Gamma_2}{dt},&
\frac{dY_2}{dt}&=\frac{dR}{dt},&
\frac{dY_3}{dt}&=\frac{dM_2}{dt},\\
\frac{dY_4}{dt}&=\frac{1}{M_{\rm ej}}\frac{dM_3}{dt},&
\frac{dY_5}{dt}&=\frac{1}{M_{\rm ej}c^2}\frac{dU_3}{dt},&
\frac{dY_6}{dt}&=\frac{1}{V_0}\frac{dV_3}{dt}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Pre-crossing mass flux}

The pre-crossing mass flux tells how fast unshocked ejecta enters region 3.
Before shell crossing,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dM_3}{dt}&=
4\pi R^2m_p\,\Gamma_0 n_4\,\Delta\beta_{\rm rs}
\frac{dR}{dt},\\
\frac{dR}{dt}&=\frac{\beta_2c}{1-\beta_2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Delta\beta_{\rm rs}\) is the speed of the reverse-shock front relative
to the unshocked ejecta in the lab-frame radial coordinate.

\subsubsection{Reverse-shock speed}

The reverse-shock speed is obtained from the upstream shock-frame four-velocity.
The lab-frame reverse-shock speed is
\begin{equation}
\beta_{\rm rs}=\beta_4-\Delta\beta_{\rm rs},
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
\Delta\beta_{\rm rs}
=\frac{u_u}
{\Gamma_0(\Gamma_0\gamma_u-u_0u_u)}.
\end{equation}
Here \(u_0=(\Gamma_0^2-1)^{1/2}\), while \(u_u\) and \(\gamma_u\) are supplied by
the MHD jump solution.  This definition ties the mass flux directly to the
shock-front jump state.

\subsubsection{Active pre-crossing Lorentz equation}

The active pre-crossing Lorentz equation balances forward swept mass, reverse
shock loading, thermal inertia, and magnetic inertia.  In this branch,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal A_{\rm pre}
&\equiv(\Gamma_2^2-1)n_1+\mathcal B_{\rm pre},\\
\mathcal B_{\rm pre}
&\equiv(\Gamma_2\gamma_{34}-\Gamma_0)\Delta\beta_{\rm rs}
\Gamma_0\tilde n_4,\\
\frac{d\Gamma_2}{dR}
=-\frac{4\pi R^2m_p\mathcal A_{\rm pre}}{\mathcal I_{\rm pre}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\tilde n_4=(1+\sigma)n_4\) is the inertial density of unprocessed
magnetized ejecta.  The inertia denominator is
\begin{equation}
\begin{split}
\mathcal I_{\rm pre}={}&
[\epsilon_2+2(1-\epsilon_2)\Gamma_2]M_2
+M_3+M_B+M_s \\
& +(1-\epsilon_3)\zeta_3M_3
+(1-\epsilon_3)\Gamma_2\mathcal R_3M_3\mathcal G_{34}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Here \(M_s\) is the effective inertia of active hydrodynamic secondary
reverse-shock branches.  It is zero when no density-jump branch is active.  The
last factor is the derivative of \(\gamma_{34}\) with respect to
\(\Gamma_2\),
\begin{equation}
\mathcal G_{34}=
\Gamma_0(1-\beta_4\beta_2)
-\frac{\Gamma_0\beta_4}{\Gamma_2^2\beta_2}.
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Reverse radiative fraction}

The reverse radiative fraction controls how much shocked-ejecta heat remains in
the inertia term.  ASGARD computes it with the same cooling diagnostic as the
forward dynamics,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{c,3}&=\frac{6\pi m_ec}
{\sigma_{\rm es} B_{\rm t}^{\prime 2}\Gamma_2t},\\
\gamma_{m,3}&=1+
\frac{\epsilon_{e,r}}{f_{e,r}}
\frac{p_r-2}{p_r-1}
\frac{m_p}{m_e}\zeta_3,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_3=\epsilon_{e,r}\min\left[
1,\left(\frac{\gamma_{m,3}}{\gamma_{c,3}}\right)^{p_r-2}
\right].
\end{equation}
Here \(t\) is the source-frame time used by the dynamics, \(B'_{\rm t}\) is the
turbulent reverse-shock field, and \(p_r\), \(f_{e,r}\), and \(\epsilon_{e,r}\)
are the reverse-shock electron parameters.

\subsubsection{Thermal and volume update}

The thermal update adds shock heat and subtracts adiabatic work in region 3:
\begin{equation}
\frac{dU_3}{dt}=\zeta_3c^2\frac{dM_3}{dt}
-(\hat{\gamma}_{3}-1)\frac{U_3}{V_3}q_{V,3},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{{\rm th},3}&=1+\zeta_3,\\
\hat{\gamma}_{3}&=
\frac{4\gamma_{{\rm th},3}+1}{3\gamma_{{\rm th},3}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(\gamma_{{\rm th},3}\) is the thermal Lorentz-factor proxy and
\(\hat{\gamma}_3\) is the region-3 adiabatic index.  The volume update is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dV_3}{dt}&=\frac{1}{r_\sigma n_4m_p}\frac{dM_3}{dt}
+q_{V,3},\\
q_{V,3}&\equiv V_3\left(\frac{3}{R}\frac{dR}{dt}
-\frac{1}{\Gamma_2}\frac{d\Gamma_2}{dt}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The first term adds newly shocked volume, and \(q_{V,3}\) gives the bulk
expansion of the already shocked region.

\subsubsection{Post-crossing bulk update}

The post-crossing bulk update removes the active reverse-shock source.  After
crossing, \(dM_3/dt=0\), no new reverse-shock heat is added, and the stored
\(U_3/V_3\) thermal state controls post-crossing electron cooling.  The bulk
Lorentz factor then follows
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\Gamma_2}{dR}
=-\frac{u_2^2\,dM_2/dR}
{[\epsilon_2+2(1-\epsilon_2)\Gamma_2]M_2+M_3+M_B+M_s}.
\end{equation}
This equation keeps the swept forward mass, shocked ejecta mass, magnetic
inertia, and active secondary-shock inertia in the denominator.

\subsection{Crossing and activation event splitting}

\subsubsection{Mass-fraction independent variable}

The mass-fraction variable measures how much ejecta has passed through the
reverse shock.  Before crossing, ASGARD uses
\(q\equiv M_3/M_{\rm ej}\) as the step variable, because shell crossing is then
the fixed endpoint \(q=1\).  If the physical right-hand side gives \(dY/dt\),
the RK stages use
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dY}{dq}
&=\frac{dY/dt}{d(M_3/M_{\rm ej})/dt},\\
\frac{dt}{dq}
&=\left[\frac{d(M_3/M_{\rm ej})}{dt}\right]^{-1}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(Y\) is the reverse-shock state vector and \(t\) is the source-frame time.
This step lands directly on the crossing event.  It does not assign the crossing
state after a completed step.

\subsubsection{Log-time single-branch stages}

The log-time variable gives the integrator a scale-free step size away from an
event.  For a branch whose equations do not change inside the step, ASGARD
evolves \(s=\ln(t/t_0)\), so that
\begin{equation}
\frac{dY}{ds}=t\frac{dY}{dt}.
\end{equation}
Here \(t_0\) is the time at the start of that integration stage.

\subsubsection{Event-state register}

The event-state register records which reverse-shock equations are active.  It
does not add a new physical variable to the blast wave.  The register is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Phi_{\rm RS}&=\{\phi_{\rm w},\phi_0,\phi_{\rm p},\phi_{\rm x}\},\\
\phi&\in\Phi_{\rm RS},\\
W&=(\phi=\phi_{\rm w}),\\
I_0&=(\phi=\phi_0)\land(M_3<M_{\rm ej}),\\
I_{\rm p}&=(\phi=\phi_{\rm p})\lor I_0,\\
\mathbf S_{\rm RS}=(B'_{3,c},\mathbf C_\times).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\phi_{\rm w}\), \(\phi_0\), \(\phi_{\rm p}\), and \(\phi_{\rm x}\) denote
waiting, default, pre-crossing, and post-crossing states.  The flag \(W\) selects
the waiting equations.  The flag \(I_{\rm p}\) selects the active pre-crossing
source terms.  The default state \(\phi_0\) is reset after each forced RK stage,
so it is not a separate physical branch.  The slot \(B'_{3,c}\) stores the
instantaneous reverse magnetic field returned by the right-hand side, and
\(\mathbf C_\times\) stores the shell-crossing state.

\subsubsection{Crossing-state write}

The crossing-state vector stores the physical state at shell crossing.  It is
empty before crossing.  The first post-crossing right-hand side evaluation writes
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathbf C_\times={}&(t_\times,R_\times,e_{3,\times},\Gamma_{2,\times},
U_{3,\times},V_{3,\times},\\
&M_{3,\times},\gamma_{m,\times},B'_{{\rm o},\times})\\
={}&\left(t,R,\frac{U_3}{V_3},\Gamma_2,U_3,V_3,
M_3,\gamma_{m,3},B'_{\rm o}\right),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(B'_{{\rm o},\times}\) is the ordered reverse-shock field at crossing.
Later post-crossing steps read this vector and do not rewrite it.

\subsubsection{Crossing and activation split}

The crossing split keeps one integration step from mixing two physical branches.
If a pre-crossing log-time stage reaches \(q=1\), ASGARD bisects the step to the
event.  It then writes \(\mathbf C_\times\) once and advances the remaining part
with the post-crossing right-hand side.  Waiting-to-shock activation uses the
same event logic.  A trial waiting step is first evaluated at its target time.
If the pressure and fast-mode criteria turn on inside the interval, ASGARD
bisects the activation time before it uses the active reverse-shock equations.

\subsubsection{Single-branch refinement}

The single-branch refinement test checks the RK solution only inside one active
branch.  It compares two successive RK resolutions through
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_{\rm RK,RS}=\max_a\frac{2|a_N-a_{2N}|}{a_N+a_{2N}},
\end{equation}
with \(a\in\{\Gamma_2,R,M_2\}\) for log-time steps and
\(a\in\{\Gamma_2,R,M_2,t\}\) for the \(q\)-step.  Event points are excluded from
this order check because the physical equations change at the event.

\subsection{Reverse electron injection contract}

The reverse-electron source adds particles only from ejecta that has just crossed
the reverse shock.  The transport grid is the same one-dimensional
log-\(\gamma_e\) grid used by the forward solver, but the source is a radial
mass source rather than a source-frame time rate:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dN_e^{\rm r}}{dR}
&=f_{e,r}\frac{1}{m_p}\frac{dM_3}{dR},\\
\gamma_m^{\rm r}&=1+
\frac{\epsilon_{e,r}}{f_{e,r}}
\frac{p_r-2}{p_r-1}
\frac{m_p}{m_e}
\zeta_3.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(f_{e,r}\) is the fraction of shocked ejecta electrons that enter the
nonthermal population, and \(\zeta_3=U_3/(M_3c^2)\) supplies the reverse-shock
heat per baryon.  The injected spectrum is written in the solver coordinate
\(x=\log_{10}\gamma_e\).  ASGARD uses the kinetic shape
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal C_{\max}^{\rm r}(\gamma_e)&=
\begin{cases}
1, & \gamma_e\le\gamma_{\max}^{\rm r},\\
\exp(1-\gamma_e/\gamma_{\max}^{\rm r}),&
\gamma_e>\gamma_{\max}^{\rm r},
\end{cases}\\
Q_x^{\rm r}&=
A_r\gamma_e\ln10\,(\gamma_e-1)^{-p_r}
\mathcal C_{\max}^{\rm r}(\gamma_e).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The factor \(A_r\) is chosen so that the cell integral of \(Q_x^{\rm r}\) gives
the radial source \(dN_e^{\rm r}/dR\).  This kinetic form avoids replacing
\(\gamma_e-1\) by \(\gamma_e\) at mildly relativistic energies.  Reverse
electron transport may use either \texttt{fullhide\_1d} or \texttt{dg\_1d}.  Both
paths use this same shell source and the post-crossing adiabatic rate from the
stored \(V_3(R)\).  The reverse synchrotron luminosity is computed with the
same selected synchrotron kernel as the forward shock.  Optional reverse SSC
and cross-zone IC are added only during observer assembly.  Reverse hadronic
full-chain flags reuse the formal one-dimensional hadronic kernels with the
reverse magnetic field, reverse seed photons, reverse shell energy, and reverse
baryon target density.

\subsection{Reverse radius-output sweep}

The reverse radius-output sweep inserts the electron source on each saved radial
interval.  For the interval \([R_{i-1},R_i]\), ASGARD reconstructs the local
state at the shell midpoint,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\bar\Gamma_{2,i}&=\frac{\Gamma_{2,i}+\Gamma_{2,i-1}}{2},\\
\bar B'_{3,i}&=\frac{B'_{3,i}+B'_{3,i-1}}{2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\bar\Gamma_{2,i}\) sets the local shock energy and \(\bar B'_{3,i}\) sets
the local synchrotron loss rate.  The relative Lorentz factor between shocked
fluid and unshocked ejecta is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\bar u_{2,i}&=(\bar\Gamma_{2,i}^2-1)^{1/2},\\
u_0&=(\Gamma_0^2-1)^{1/2},\\
\gamma_{34,i}
&=\frac{\bar\Gamma_{2,i}^2+\Gamma_0^2-1}
{\Gamma_0\bar\Gamma_{2,i}+\bar u_{2,i}u_0}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(\Gamma_0\) is the initial ejecta Lorentz factor.  The injection break and
synchrotron-limited cutoff used for this radial interval are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\gamma_{m,i}^{\rm r}&=
1+\frac{\epsilon_{e,r}}{f_{e,r}}
\frac{p_r-2}{p_r-1}
\frac{m_p}{m_e}(\gamma_{34,i}-1),\\
\gamma_{\max,i}^{\rm r}
&=\frac{3m_ec^2}{(8e^3\bar B'_{3,i})^{1/2}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The factor \((p_r-2)/(p_r-1)\) is evaluated with the runtime reverse-shock index
\(p_r\).  If shell crossing lies inside the output interval, only the part before
crossing receives fresh electrons:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta R_{\rm inj}&=\min(R_i,R_\times)-R_{i-1},\\
\mathcal Q^{\rm inj}_{R,i}
&=f_{e,r}\frac{M_{3,{\rm hi}}-M_{3,i-1}}
{m_p\Delta R_{\rm inj}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(M_{3,{\rm hi}}=M_{3,\times}\) when \(R_i>R_\times\), and
\(M_{3,{\rm hi}}=M_{3,i}\) otherwise.  Intervals entirely after crossing set
\(\mathcal Q^{\rm inj}_{R,i}=0\).

\subsection{Reverse cooling assembly}

The reverse cooling assembly gives the transport solver a radial energy-loss
