rate.  The synchrotron part is computed before either the finite-volume or DG
update is called:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
a_{\rm syn}(R)&=
\frac{\sigma_{\rm es} B_3^{\prime 2}}{6\pi m_ec^2\,\beta_2\Gamma_2},\\
\left.\frac{d\gamma}{dR}\right|_{\rm syn}
&=-a_{\rm syn}\gamma^2 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\sigma_{\rm es}\) is the electron-scattering cross section used throughout
the manuscript, and \(B'_3\) is the total reverse-shock magnetic field.  For the
synchrotron-only branch, this is the only radiative loss.  When the local IC/KN
coefficient from the synchrotron seed field is active,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
a_i^{\rm IC}&=\frac{A_i^{\rm IC}}{\beta_2\Gamma_2c},\\
\left.\frac{d\gamma_i}{dR}\right|_{\rm rad}
&=-(a_{\rm syn}+a_i^{\rm IC})\gamma_i^2 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(A_i^{\rm IC}\) is the local inverse-Compton loss coefficient in cell
\(i\).  For the Nakar-style Compton branch, ASGARD defines the reverse-shock
energy density and Compton factor as
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
e_{\rm N}'&=4\Gamma_2^2 n_4m_pc^2,\\
Y_i&=\frac{A_i^{\rm N}}{4\pi R^2c\,e_{\rm N}'}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The transported loss is then \(-a_{\rm syn}(1+Y_i)\gamma_i^2\).  The same
Compton factor reduces the local injection cutoff to
\begin{equation}
\gamma_{\max,{\rm IC}}^{\rm r}
\equiv \gamma_{\max}^{\rm r}(1+Y_{\max})^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Reverse finite-volume transport and substeps}

The reverse finite-volume step transports the injected electron spectrum along
radius.  The adiabatic coefficient is \(a_{\rm ad}=1/R\) before shell crossing.
After crossing, it is measured from the stored region-3 volume:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
a_{\rm ad}&=\frac{1}{3}\frac{d\ln V_3}{dR},\\
a_i^{\rm ad}&\simeq
\frac{\ln(V_{3,i}/V_{3,i-1})}{3(R_i-R_{i-1})}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
with \(V_{3,\times}\) used when the output interval contains the crossing point.
Here \(a_{\rm ad}\) gives the radial adiabatic cooling or heating rate.  In the
conservative coordinate \(y\), the \texttt{fullhide\_1d} branch advances
\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial N_y}{\partial R}
+
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
\left[
-\frac{D_{\rm rad}(\gamma)+a_{\rm ad}}
{\ln10\,dx_g/dy}N_y
\right]
=Q_y ,
\end{equation}
where \(N_y\) is the cell density in the conservative energy coordinate,
\(Q_y\) is the reverse-shock source in that coordinate, and \(D_{\rm rad}\) is
defined by \(-d\gamma/dR|_{\rm rad}=D_{\rm rad}\gamma\).  This is the same
face-flux equation as the forward shell solver, with the forward source replaced
by \(Q_y\).

The radial substep size is set by the fastest local cooling rate.  For the
primary reverse shock in the \texttt{fullhide\_1d} branch, ASGARD uses
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
R_{m,i}&=\frac{R_i+R_{i-1}}{2},\\
a_i^{\rm p}&=\frac{2}{3R_{m,i}},\\
\Delta R_{\rm cand}
&=\frac{C_R}{a_{\rm syn}\gamma_{\max}+a_i^{\rm p}},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(R_{m,i}\) is the radial midpoint, \(a_i^{\rm p}\) is the primary
adiabatic estimate, and \(C_R\) is a fixed radial-step control.  The
secondary-reservoir and reacceleration branches use
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
a_i^{\rm s}&=\frac{1}{R_{i-1}},\\
\Delta R_{\rm cand}
&=\frac{C_R}{a_{\rm syn}\gamma_{\max}+a_i^{\rm s}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(a_i^{\rm s}\) is the corresponding secondary-branch adiabatic estimate.
The accepted substep count is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
N_c&=\left\lfloor\frac{R_i-R_{i-1}}{\Delta R_{\rm cand}}\right\rfloor,\\
N_{\rm sub}&=\max\left[N_{\min},\min(N_{\max},N_c)\right],
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
for the \texttt{fullhide\_1d} branch.  The \texttt{dg\_1d} branch uses
\(N_{\rm DG}\) substeps and the DG transport formulas given above.  The
quantities \(C_R\), \(N_{\min}\), \(N_{\max}\), and \(N_{\rm DG}\) are fixed
transport controls, not fitted physical parameters.

\subsection{Post-crossing characteristic remap}

The post-crossing characteristic remap evolves the stored reverse-electron
spectrum after fresh injection has stopped.  After shell crossing, the source
term is removed.  The kernel then transports the existing spectrum by composing
energy-space characteristic maps.

\subsubsection{Synchrotron affine map}

The synchrotron characteristic map is simple when cooling is synchrotron only.
For the inverse energy \(u\equiv1/\gamma\),
\begin{equation}
\frac{du}{dR}=a_{\rm syn}+a_{\rm ad}u .
\end{equation}
The cell edge at the previous radius is obtained from
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\eta_u&=\frac{a_{\rm syn}}{a_{\rm ad}},\qquad
E_u=\exp(-a_{\rm ad}\Delta R)
\quad(a_{\rm ad}\ne0),\\
u_{\rm prev}&=
\begin{cases}
u_{\rm now}-a_{\rm syn}\Delta R, & a_{\rm ad}=0,\\
(u_{\rm now}+\eta_u)E_u-\eta_u, & a_{\rm ad}\ne0 .
\end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(u_{\rm now}\) is the edge at the current radius and \(u_{\rm prev}\) is
the back-traced edge.  The corresponding \(x_g=-\log_{10}u\) edge is then
composed with the stored crossing map.

\subsubsection{IC/KN piecewise-affine map}

The IC/KN characteristic map allows the radiative coefficient to vary with
energy.  When IC/KN cooling is active, the solver builds a local affine map on
each energy cell \(\ell\),
\begin{equation}
\frac{du}{dR}=a_\ell+b_\ell u .
\end{equation}
The same back-tracing operation is then applied cell by cell.  The diagnostic
\(dN_e/d\gamma_e\) is reconstructed from the remapped log-energy content with
the same exponential cell reconstruction used in the forward characteristic
path.

\subsection{Secondary-reservoir electron transport}

The secondary-reservoir electron path follows electrons made by density-jump
reverse shocks.  Each reservoir has its own swept mass, volume, and spectrum,
but it uses the same synchrotron cooling and radial transport machinery as the
primary reverse shock.

\subsubsection{Reservoir source and volume loss}

The reservoir source adds electrons from the mass processed by branch \(j\).  On
the output interval \([R_{i-1},R_i]\), the radial source and volume coefficient
are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal Q^{s}_{R,j}
&=f_{e,r}\frac{M_{j,i}-M_{j,i-1}}
{m_p(R_i-R_{i-1})},\\
a_{V,j}&=
\frac{\ln(V_{j,i}/V_{j,i-1})}
{3(R_i-R_{i-1})}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(M_j\) and \(V_j\) are the mass and comoving volume of reservoir \(j\).
The coefficient \(a_{V,j}\) enters the same adiabatic term used for the primary
reverse shock.  When the branch has no positive volume yet, the code sets
\(a_{V,j}=0\) and waits for the first physical reservoir volume.  The same
finite-volume or DG reverse-electron step then advances the reservoir spectrum.

\subsubsection{Reservoir radiation product}

The reservoir radiation product is computed from each evolved reservoir
spectrum.  The secondary spectra use the same synchrotron and SSA kernels as the
forward shock.  The reported observer-frame absorption break is
\begin{equation}
\nu_a^{\rm obs}=
\frac{\nu_a'}
{\Gamma_2(1-\beta_2)(1+z)} .
\end{equation}
Here \(\nu_a'\) is the comoving SSA frequency of one reservoir, and
\(\Gamma_2(1-\beta_2)\) gives the on-axis Doppler denominator used by this
diagnostic output.  The branch-summed secondary luminosity is a direct sum over
independently evolved reservoirs,
\begin{equation}
L_{\nu'}^{\prime,s}=\sum_jL_{\nu',j}^{\prime,s}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Reacceleration diagnostic}

The reacceleration diagnostic tests how a new density-jump branch reprocesses
electrons that were already shocked.  It first transfers part of a parent
reservoir into the new branch.  It then applies compression, relative motion,
and a diffusive-shock acceleration remap before the fresh mass source is added.

\subsubsection{Parent mass transfer}

The parent mass transfer selects the available electron reservoir.  If branch
\(j\) has parent label \(\pi_j\), the transferred log-energy spectrum is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta M_j^{\rm tr}&=\min(\Delta M_j,M_{\pi_j}^{\rm av}),\\
f_j^{\rm tr}&=\frac{\Delta M_j^{\rm tr}}{M_{\pi_j}^{\rm av}},\\
N_{\log,j}^{s}(\gamma)&=f_j^{\rm tr}N_{\log,\pi_j}(\gamma).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(M_{\pi_j}^{\rm av}\) is the parent mass still available for transfer,
\(\Delta M_j^{\rm tr}\) is the transferred part, and \(N_{\log,j}^{s}\) is the
seed log-energy spectrum for branch \(j\).

\subsubsection{Compression boost}

The compression boost shifts the seed spectrum to the energy scale of the new
branch.  Compression and relative motion give
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
r_j^{c}&=
\begin{cases}
r_j, & r_j>1,\\
1, & r_j\le1,
\end{cases}\\
g_j&=\Gamma_{43,j}(r_j^{c})^{1/3},\\
N_{\log,j}^{b}(\gamma)
&=N_{\log,j}^{s}(\gamma/g_j).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(r_j\) is the stored compression factor, \(r_j^c\) is the active
compression factor, \(\Gamma_{43,j}\) is the relative Lorentz factor across the
secondary shock, and \(g_j\) is the branch boost.  The boosted spectrum
\(N_{\log,j}^{b}\) keeps the same particle number in log-energy space.

\subsubsection{Diffusive-shock acceleration remap}

The diffusive-shock acceleration remap converts the boosted seed spectrum into a
power-law tail.  For \(p_r>2\), ASGARD uses
\begin{equation}
\frac{dN_{j}^{a}}{d\gamma}
=(p_r-1)\gamma^{-p_r}
\int_1^\gamma
\frac{dN_j^{b}}{d\tilde\gamma}
\tilde\gamma^{p_r-1}\,d\tilde\gamma,
\qquad p_r>2 .
\end{equation}
Here \(N_j^b\) is the boosted seed spectrum and \(N_j^a\) is the reaccelerated
spectrum.  The lower limit \(\tilde\gamma=1\) states that only physical electron
energies are included.  The condition \(p_r>2\) is the active boundary of this
diagnostic branch.

\subsubsection{Energy accounting}

The energy accounting keeps the transferred and reaccelerated electron budgets
visible.  The branch energies are computed from the same log-energy content:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
E_j^{s}&=\int(\gamma-1)m_ec^2N_{\log,j}^{s}\,d\log_{10}\gamma,\\
E_j^{a}&=\int(\gamma-1)m_ec^2N_{\log,j}^{a}\,d\log_{10}\gamma .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(E_j^s\) is the seed energy and \(E_j^a\) is the reaccelerated energy.
The remaining untransferred dissipative mass is inserted through the fresh
kinetic source above.  The diagnostic therefore separates reprocessed parent
electrons from newly injected electrons in the same branch spectrum.

\subsection{Density-jump secondary-shock solver}

Secondary reverse shocks are diagnostic branches tied to density-jump events.
They are not independent emission components.  Each branch asks whether a rising
external-density bump can drive a new reverse shock into material that has
already passed through the forward shock.

\subsubsection{Event-window density}

The event-window density selects the part of a bump that can launch the
secondary reverse shock.  For a Gaussian jump \(j\), ASGARD defines
\(\Delta R_j=w_jR_j\) and uses only the rising side:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
R_j-\eta_J\Delta R_j&\le R<R_j,\\
x_j&=\frac{R-R_j}{\Delta R_j},\\
n_j^{\rm ex}(R)&=n_{\rm b}(R)(f_j-1)\exp(-x_j^2/2).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(n_{\rm b}\) is the baseline external density, \(f_j\) is the jump factor,
and \(\eta_J\) is the fixed event-window control used by the scanner.  The total
upstream density entering the contact problem is
\begin{equation}
n_1=n_{\rm b}+\sum_{\ell}n_\ell^{\rm ex},
\end{equation}
while \(n_j^{\rm ex}\) is the excess density selected for branch \(j\).  Both
quantities are number densities in \({\rm cm^{-3}}\), not dimensionless
multipliers.

\subsubsection{Secondary inertia register}

The secondary inertia register adds active density-jump branches to the bulk
inertia.  For the relativistically hot secondary gas, ASGARD uses
\(\hat\gamma_{\rm h}=4/3\).  Let \((M_s^b,U_s,V_s)\) be the summed baryonic mass,
thermal energy, and volume of the active secondary branches.  Their comoving
pressure and enthalpy enter
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
p'_s&=(\hat\gamma_{\rm h}-1)\frac{U_s}{V_s},\\
M_s&=\frac{M_s^bc^2+U_s+p'_sV_s}{c^2}
+\frac{p'_sV_s}{\Gamma_2^2c^2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(M_s\) is the effective secondary inertia used in the primary reverse-shock
bulk equation.

\subsubsection{Region-4 upstream state}

The region-4 upstream state is the material into which the secondary reverse
shock propagates.  For the first secondary branch, this upstream is the
pre-bump forward-shock downstream state:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
n_{\rm pre}=n_1-n_j^{\rm ex},
\qquad
r_2=4\Gamma_2,
\\
n'_4=r_2 n_{\rm pre}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(n_{\rm pre}\) removes the selected excess density from the total upstream
density, and \(r_2=4\Gamma_2\) is the relativistic strong-shock density factor
used by this diagnostic branch.  The corresponding internal energy and pressure
are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
e'_4&=(\Gamma_2-1)n'_4m_pc^2,
\qquad
p'_4&=(\hat\gamma_{\rm h}-1)e'_4 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
If a previous secondary branch has finite mass, energy, and volume, that parent
branch supplies the local upstream state instead:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
n'_4&=\frac{M_p}{m_pV_p},\\
e'_4&=\frac{U_p}{V_p},
\qquad
p'_4=(\hat\gamma_{\rm h}-1)e'_4 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(M_p\), \(U_p\), and \(V_p\) are the parent branch mass, internal energy,
and comoving volume.

\subsubsection{Contact pressure root}

The contact pressure root sets the Lorentz factor of the secondary contact
surface.  For a trial contact Lorentz factor \(g_c\), the pressure behind the
new forward shock in the bump is
\begin{equation}
p'_2(g_c)=
\hat\gamma_{\rm h}(g_c^2-1)n_1m_pc^2,
\end{equation}
while the pressure behind the secondary reverse shock is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
p'_3(g_c)&=
p'_4+\hat\gamma_{\rm h}(\Gamma_{43}^2-1)(e'_4+p'_4),\\
\beta_c&=(1-g_c^{-2})^{1/2},\\
\Gamma_{43}&=g_c\Gamma_4(1-\beta_c\beta_4).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Gamma_4\) and \(\beta_4\) describe the region-4 upstream state, and
\(\Gamma_{43}\) is the relative Lorentz factor between the contact and region 4.
The solver searches the physical interval \(1<g_c<\Gamma_4\) for
\(p'_2(g_c)-p'_3(g_c)=0\).

\subsubsection{Shock-frame compression}

The shock-frame compression gives the density jump across the secondary reverse
shock.  For a trial laboratory shock speed \(\beta_s\), the upstream and
downstream velocities in the shock frame are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\beta_{4s}&=\frac{\beta_4-\beta_s}{1-\beta_4\beta_s},
\qquad
\beta_{3s}=\frac{\beta_c-\beta_s}{1-\beta_c\beta_s},\\
u_{is}&=\Gamma_{is}|\beta_{is}|\quad (i=3,4).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The stored diagnostic \(\beta_{\rm rs}\) is this laboratory-frame root
\(\beta_s\).  Baryon-flux continuity gives the compression
\begin{equation}
\chi\equiv\frac{n'_3}{n'_4}=\frac{u_{4s}}{u_{3s}} .
\end{equation}
The solver searches only compressive states with \(\chi>1\).  The accepted root
also satisfies the shock-frame momentum-flux equation
\begin{equation}
w'_4u_{4s}^2+p'_4=w'_3u_{3s}^2+p'_3,
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
w'_4&=n'_4m_pc^2+e'_4+p'_4,\\
e'_3&=\frac{p'_3}{\hat\gamma_{\rm h}-1},
\qquad
w'_3=\chi n'_4m_pc^2+e'_3+p'_3 .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(w'_4\) and \(w'_3\) are the upstream and downstream enthalpy densities.
This closure assumes relativistically hot gas in the secondary diagnostic
branch.  It is not a general finite-temperature Taub solve.  Once contact
balance fixes \(p'_3\), mass and momentum flux determine only the shock speed and
compression.

\subsubsection{Dissipated-energy source}

The dissipated-energy source decides whether the branch receives new heat.  If
no compressive momentum root exists, the branch is inactive.  If a root exists,
energy is deposited only when
\begin{equation}
\Delta e'_j=e'_3-e'_4\chi^{\hat\gamma_{\rm h}}>0,
\end{equation}
which subtracts the adiabatically compressed upstream internal energy from the
newly shocked hot-gas state.  The branch mass, energy, and volume are then
accumulated as explicit radial derivatives.  The shocked-volume and
expansion-volume sources are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dV_{q,j}}{dR}&=\frac{1}{n'_3m_p}\frac{dM_j}{dR},\\
\frac{dV_{e,j}}{dR}
&=V_j\frac{d}{dR}\ln\left(\frac{R^3}{\Gamma_2}\right),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(q\) denotes newly shocked volume and \(e\) denotes volume expansion.  The
branch source terms are
\begin{equation}
\frac{dM_j}{dR}=4\pi R^2n'_4m_p\Gamma_2
\frac{\beta_2-\beta_{\rm rs}}{\beta_c},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\frac{dU_j}{dR}=\Delta e'_j\frac{dV_{q,j}}{dR}
-(\hat\gamma_{\rm h}-1)\frac{U_j}{V_j}
\frac{dV_{e,j}}{dR}.
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\frac{dV_j}{dR}=\frac{dV_{q,j}}{dR}+\frac{dV_{e,j}}{dR}.
\end{equation}
These equations add processed mass, dissipated internal energy, and comoving
volume to branch \(j\).

\subsubsection{Normalized state-vector entries}

The normalized state-vector entries store the secondary source terms in the
augmented ODE state.  The code converts the radial derivatives above to
source-frame time rates using
\begin{equation}
\frac{d}{dt}=\frac{dR}{dt}\frac{d}{dR}.
\end{equation}
The state-vector source entries are
\begin{equation}
\begin{gathered}
D_{6+j}=\frac{1}{M_{\rm ej}}\frac{dM_j}{dt},\\
D_{6+N_J+j}=\frac{1}{M_{\rm ej}c^2}\frac{dU_j}{dt},\\
D_{6+2N_J+j}=\frac{1}{V_0}\frac{dV_j}{dt},\\
D_{6+3N_J+j}=\frac{1}{M_{\rm ej}c^2}
\frac{d\Delta U_j}{dt},\\
D_{6+4N_J+j}=
\frac{\gamma_{m,j}}{M_{\rm ej}c^2}
\frac{d\Delta U_j}{dt}.
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
Here \(M_j\), \(U_j\), and \(V_j\) are the mass, internal energy, and volume of
secondary branch \(j\).  \(\Delta U_j\) is the newly dissipated part.  The final
two entries are diagnostic weights for newly dissipated energy and its injection
Lorentz factor; they do not add a separate inertia term.

\subsection{Secondary event windows and observer diagnostics}

The secondary event windows mark the radii where a density-jump branch can
dissipate heat.  Each branch uses the same source function,
\begin{equation}
S_j(R)=e'_{3,j}-e'_{4,j}\chi_j^{\hat\gamma_{\rm h}}.
\end{equation}
Here \(e'_{3,j}\) is the shocked hot-gas energy density, and
\(e'_{4,j}\chi_j^{\hat\gamma_{\rm h}}\) is the upstream energy density after
adiabatic compression.

\subsubsection{Event-root scanner}

The event-root scanner finds where \(S_j\) changes sign inside the rising side
of a density bump.  Between two stored primary reverse-shock states, it
subdivides the overlap with \([R_j-\eta_J\Delta R_j,R_j)\) into intervals no
wider than \(\Delta R_j/N_J^{\rm scan}\).  It then linearly interpolates
\((Y,\Gamma_2,t_{\rm obs})\) inside each bracket and bisects sign changes of
\(S_j\).  A positive value, \(S_j>0\), marks an active secondary reverse-shock
window.  If a branch remains open at the last sampled radius, the scanner closes
it at the last known state and does not extrapolate beyond the stored solution.

\subsubsection{Dimensional branch state}

The dimensional branch state converts the normalized ODE variables back to
physical mass, energy, and volume:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
M_j&=Y_{m,j}M_{\rm ej},\\
U_j&=Y_{u,j}M_{\rm ej}c^2,\\
V_j&=Y_{v,j}V_0.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(Y_{m,j}\), \(Y_{u,j}\), and \(Y_{v,j}\) are the stored dimensionless
entries for branch \(j\).

\subsubsection{Dissipation-weighted diagnostics}

The dissipation-weighted diagnostics give more weight to branches that inject
more new heat.  With \(E^q_{j,i}\) denoting the cumulative shocked energy in
branch \(j\) at output radius \(R_i\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta E_{j,i}&=E^q_{j,i}-E^q_{j,i-1},
\qquad
\langle Q\rangle_i&=
\frac{\sum_j \Delta E_{j,i}Q_{j,i}}
{\sum_j \Delta E_{j,i}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(Q_{j,i}\) can be any branch diagnostic, such as contact Lorentz factor or
injection Lorentz factor.  The weight is newly dissipated energy, not accumulated
mass.

\subsubsection{Combined secondary state}

The combined secondary state is the branch sum passed to the observer stage.
ASGARD first sums the physical reservoirs,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
M_s^b&=\sum_j M_j,\\
U_s&=\sum_j U_j,\\
V_s&=\sum_j V_j,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and then computes the effective magnetic field, pressure, and enthalpy density:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
B'_s&=\left(8\pi\epsilon_{B,r}\frac{U_s}{V_s}\right)^{1/2},\\
p'_s&=(\hat\gamma_{\rm h}-1)\frac{U_s}{V_s},\\
w'_s&=\frac{M_s^b c^2+U_s}{V_s}+p'_s .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(B'_s\) is used for secondary synchrotron diagnostics, and \(w'_s\) is the
combined hot-gas enthalpy density.

\subsubsection{Break-frequency diagnostics}

The break-frequency diagnostics translate the combined secondary state into
observable synchrotron scales.  The injection-break diagnostic uses the same
dissipation weights,
\begin{equation}
\bar{\gamma}_{m,s}=
\frac{\sum_j \Delta E_{j,i}\gamma_{m,j,i}}
{\sum_j \Delta E_{j,i}},
\end{equation}
where \(\bar{\gamma}_{m,s}\) is the energy-weighted injection Lorentz factor.
The synchrotron frequency coefficient is kept as the physical constant
\begin{equation}
C_\nu=\frac{3e}{4\pi m_ec}.
\end{equation}
The contact and region-2 Doppler factors are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\bar\beta_c&=(1-\langle g_c\rangle^{-2})^{1/2},
\qquad
\delta_c^{-1}&=\langle g_c\rangle(1-\bar\beta_c),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\delta_2^{-1}&=\Gamma_2(1-\beta_2),\\
\beta_2&=(1-\Gamma_2^{-2})^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The diagnostic break frequencies are
\begin{equation}
\nu_{m,s}=
\frac{C_\nu B'_s\bar{\gamma}_{m,s}^2\delta_c}{1+z},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\gamma_{c,s}=
\frac{6\pi m_ec(1+z)}
{\sigma_{\rm es} B_s^{\prime 2}\Gamma_2 t_{\rm obs}},
\qquad
\nu_{c,s}=
\frac{C_\nu B'_s\gamma_{c,s}^2\delta_2}{1+z}.
\end{equation}
Here \(\sigma_{\rm es}\) is the electron-scattering cross section, and
\(t_{\rm obs}\) is the observer time used for the diagnostic cooling break.

\subsubsection{Supported branch boundary}

The supported branch boundary is deliberately explicit.  The multi-density
reverse-shock path supports \(p_r>2\), the one-dimensional
\texttt{fullhide\_1d} or \texttt{dg\_1d} electron solver, and the direct 1D
observer path.  It does not support hadronic processes, forward SSC cooling,
reverse SSC, cross-zone IC, or coupled electron-photon feedback in this branch.
The event windows are diagnostic roots from linearly interpolated adjacent
output states.  The adaptive RK refinement controls the primary reverse-shock
variables \(\Gamma_2\), \(R\), and \(M_2\).  It does not provide a separate
conservative error norm for every secondary branch variable.  The secondary
reverse-shock output is therefore a density-jump diagnostic, not a general
reverse-shock model
\citep{DaiLu2002,NakarGranot2007,UhmZhang2014,Laskar2016GRB160509A,Alexander2017GRB160625B,Laskar2019GRB181201A,PangDai2024OffaxisRS}.

\begin{figure*}[!tp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig4_reverse_shock.pdf}
  \caption{Reverse-shock and density-jump secondary diagnostics.  (a) The magnetized reverse-shock state is recorded as a function of upstream \(\sigma\).  (b) The reverse-shock flux fraction at the optical peak gives the component contribution.  (c) Density-jump secondary reverse-shock event windows and the injected-to-dissipated energy ratio are computed from the returned branch state.}
  \label{fig:reverse-shock}
\end{figure*}

\section{Observer Projection and Component Assembly} \label{sec:projection}

\subsection{Equal-arrival-time projection}

\subsubsection{Local angular map}

The local angular map gives the photon arrival time and Doppler factor for one
emitting cell.  Observer projection uses this map only after the radius-grid
source terms and absorption factors have been assembled.  For an angular cell at
polar angle \(\theta\), azimuth \(\phi\), and viewing angle \(\theta_v\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mu&=\cos\theta_v\cos\theta+\sin\theta_v\sin\theta\cos\phi,\\
t_{\rm arr}(R,\theta,\phi)
&=t_0(R)+\frac{(1+z)R(1-\mu)}{c},\\
\delta^{-1}&=\Gamma(1-\beta\mu).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\mu\) is the angle cosine between the cell velocity and the line of sight,
\(t_0(R)\) is the on-axis shell-arrival time supplied by the local dynamical
solution, and \(\delta\) is the Doppler factor.

\subsubsection{Radius-to-observer interpolation}

The radius-to-observer interpolation places each local spectrum on the observer
time grid.  Along the equal-arrival-time surface, the code evaluates the comoving
frequency
\begin{equation}
\nu'=\frac{\nu_{\rm obs}(1+z)}{\delta},
\end{equation}
and applies the angular weight
\begin{equation}
dW=\frac{\delta^3\,d\Omega}{4\pi}.
\end{equation}
Density-jump and injection features are therefore broadened only by
equal-arrival-time geometry and by the stated interpolation rule.

\subsection{Structured-jet aggregation boundary}

\subsubsection{Backend selection}

The backend selection states which angular projection path is active.  For a
direct top-hat model with a supported medium, \asgard\ uses the direct
kernel-backed path.  Other active jet profiles are solved as angular patches by
either the Fortran structured backend or the Python patch backend.  The Fortran
structured backend supports uniform patch sampling and the one-dimensional
\texttt{fullhide\_1d} or \texttt{dg\_1d} electron solver.  It does not support
reverse-shock SSC, cross-zone IC, reverse-shock hadronic branches,
Bethe-Heitler, \(pp\), hadronic IC, or pair-cascade branches.  The
\texttt{dominant\_region\_ioka\_v1} and
\texttt{dominant\_region\_ioka\_time\_v1} samplers use only
\texttt{python\_patch}.

\subsubsection{Representative-patch register}

The representative-patch register reduces repeated one-dimensional solves.  The
compiled \texttt{structured\_jet\_1d} entry is the Fortran aggregate used by the
uniform structured backend.  The Python caller first samples the jet on midpoint
cells and passes
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
E_{ab}&=E_{\rm iso}(\phi_b,\theta_a),\\
\Gamma_{0,ab}&=\Gamma_0(\phi_b,\theta_a),\\
I^E_{ab}&=\mathbf{1}(E_{ab}>0),\\
I^\Gamma_{ab}&=\mathbf{1}(\Gamma_{0,ab}\ge\Gamma_{\min}),\\
A_{ab}&=I^E_{ab}I^\Gamma_{ab},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(a\) and \(b\) label polar and azimuthal cells.  The quantity
\(\Gamma_{\min}\) is the relativistic-backend activation boundary, not an extra
physical parameter.  Active cells are grouped into exact state classes,
\begin{equation}
(a,b)\sim(a',b')
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
E_{ab}=E_{a'b'}\ \mathrm{and}\ \Gamma_{0,ab}=\Gamma_{0,a'b'},
\end{equation}
so the aggregate solves one representative for each class and copies its radius
history and component SEDs to the remaining active cells in that class.

\subsubsection{Local one-dimensional solve}

The local one-dimensional solve is the same physical solve used by a direct
top-hat model.  For a representative patch \(q\), the aggregate replaces only the
local boundary entries
\begin{equation}
\Gamma_0\leftarrow\Gamma_{0,q},\qquad
E_{\rm iso}\leftarrow E_q
\end{equation}
and then runs the one-dimensional dynamics, electron, optional SSC, reverse
synchrotron, and selected hadronic component kernels described earlier.  The
structured aggregate changes angular bookkeeping and state reuse.  It does not
change the local shock, particle, or synchrotron equations.

\subsubsection{Shell prefactor}

The shell prefactor applies distance dilution and local photon survival before
angular projection.  For every active component \(c\in{\cal C}\),
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{P}_{\nu'}(R)=
\frac{1+z}{4\pi d_L^2}\,
\mathcal{A}_{\gamma\gamma}(\nu',R),
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\widetilde L_{\nu'}^{\,c}(R,q)=
\mathcal{P}_{\nu'}(R)L_{\nu'}^{\prime c}(R,q).
\end{equation}
Here \(d_L\) is the luminosity distance, and
\(\mathcal{A}_{\gamma\gamma}\) is the local shell survival factor.  The set
\({\cal C}\) contains the forward-synchrotron, forward-SSC, reverse-synchrotron,
and selected hadronic labels active in that run.

\subsubsection{Angular accumulation}

The angular accumulation sums the projected component fluxes over active
patches.  Axisymmetric profiles use
\texttt{SED\_interpolation\_structured}, while explicit two-dimensional angular
grids use \texttt{sed\_structured\_phi}.  The returned total is the algebraic
component sum
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
F_{\nu}(t_{\rm obs})&=F_{\nu}^{\rm fs}
+F_{\nu}^{\rm ic}
+F_{\nu}^{\rm h}
+F_{\nu}^{\rm rs}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The diagnostic radius track returned with a structured solve is copied from the
first representative patch that records the track.  It is a local
one-dimensional history, not a flux-weighted angular average.

\subsection{Projection symbol binding}

The projection symbol binding exposes compiled observer-frame projection
kernels.  The \texttt{src.Interpolation} initializer is an ABI binding layer, not
a physics model.  The public names
\texttt{sed\_interpolation}, \texttt{sed\_adaptive\_theta},
\texttt{sed\_interpolation\_chi}, and \texttt{sed\_chi\_electron} resolve to the
compiled \texttt{SED\_interpolation} module.  The same module also supplies
\texttt{sed\_chi\_ring}, \texttt{sed\_chiring\_batchlum}, and
\texttt{sed\_chiring\_batchlum\_ray}.
On first access, the initializer imports the compiled module and copies the
requested symbol into the package namespace.  Every other name is rejected.
This binding preserves the projection map
\begin{equation}
\{\widetilde L_{\nu'}(R),\tau_{\nu'}(R),R,\Gamma,t_{\rm obs},\nu_{\rm obs}\}
\longrightarrow F_{\nu_{\rm obs}}(t_{\rm obs}),
\end{equation}
but it does not define a radiation process, optical depth, Doppler factor,
arrival-time map, or interpolation weight.  Those operations belong to the
Fortran kernels selected by the exported symbol.

\subsection{Direct top-hat equal-arrival-time interpolation}

\subsubsection{Midpoint angular cells}

The midpoint angular cells divide a direct top-hat shell into observer-facing
surface elements.  The \texttt{SED\_interpolation} kernel uses these cells for
the direct observer-frame projection.  In the shell-level path, angular cell
\(a\) has solid angle
\begin{equation}
\Delta\Omega_a=
(\cos\theta_{a,-}-\cos\theta_{a,+})\,\Delta\phi .
\end{equation}
Here \(\theta_{a,-}\) and \(\theta_{a,+}\) are the lower and upper polar edges,
and \(\Delta\phi\) is the azimuthal width.  When the public top-hat grid stores a
collapsed azimuthal half-plane, the code uses the named collapsed-grid control
\(N_{\phi,c}\) to restore the full azimuthal weight without changing the local
arrival-time map.  For each angular cell,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\mu&=\cos\theta_v\cos\theta+\sin\theta_v\sin\theta\cos\phi ,\\
t_{\rm arr}(R)&=t_0(R)+\frac{(1+z)R(1-\mu)}{c}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\theta\) and \(\phi\) are the cell midpoint angles, \(\theta_v\) is the
viewing angle, and \(t_{\rm arr}\) is the observer arrival time for radius \(R\).

\subsubsection{Radial arrival-time bracket}

The radial arrival-time bracket finds the radius that contributes to a requested
observer time inside one angular cell.  For \(t_{\rm obs}\) between two adjacent
stored radii, the code solves
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\lambda_R&=\frac{t_{\rm obs}-t_{\rm arr}(R_i)}
{t_{\rm arr}(R_{i+1})-t_{\rm arr}(R_i)},\\
R_R&=(1-\lambda_R)R_i+\lambda_RR_{i+1}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\Gamma_R=\exp\!\left[(1-\lambda_R)\ln\Gamma_i+\lambda_R\ln\Gamma_{i+1}\right],
\end{equation}
and interpolates the local SED along the same radial segment.  Here
\(\lambda_R\) is the radial interpolation weight, \(R_R\) is the emitting radius
on the arrival-time surface, and \(\Gamma_R\) is the corresponding Lorentz
factor.

\subsubsection{Doppler shift and SED accumulation}

The Doppler shift converts the observer frequency to the comoving frequency of
the emitting cell.  At the interpolated radius,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\beta_R&=(1-\Gamma_R^{-2})^{1/2},\\
\delta_R&=\left[\Gamma_R(1-\beta_R\mu)\right]^{-1},\\
\nu'&=\frac{1+z}{\delta_R}\nu_{\rm obs}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The flux contribution from this angular cell is
\begin{equation}
\Delta F_{\nu_{\rm obs}}=
\frac{\Delta\Omega_a}{4\pi}\,
\delta_R^3\,
\widetilde L_{\nu'}(R_R).
\end{equation}
Here \(\widetilde L_{\nu'}\) is the local SED after shell-level survival and
distance prefactors have been applied.  Frequency interpolation is delegated to
the shared log-frequency SED accumulator.  When the two bracketing source bins
are positive, it uses log-log interpolation.  Otherwise it interpolates the
linear SED values, with empty brackets contributing zero.

\subsection{Adaptive angular quadrature}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{\(\theta\)}{theta}-cell refinement}

The adaptive angular quadrature changes only how polar angle is sampled.  It
keeps the same local arrival-time map, Doppler factor, and frequency
interpolation.  For a trial interval \([\theta_0,\theta_1]\), it compares a
midpoint estimate \(F_{\rm coarse}\) with the sum of two midpoint estimates
\(F_{\rm fine}\) on the two half intervals.  The interval is accepted when
\(F_{\rm fine}\) has zero norm or
\begin{equation}
\sum_{\nu,t}|F_{\rm fine}-F_{\rm coarse}|
\le \epsilon_{\rm ad}
\sum_{\nu,t}|F_{\rm fine}|,
\end{equation}
or when the requested recursion depth has been reached.  Here
\(\epsilon_{\rm ad}\) is the angular-quadrature tolerance.  This path changes
angular sampling density, not the arrival-time map, Doppler weight, or frequency
interpolation.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\(\chi\)}{chi}-resolved finite-cell projection}

\subsubsection{Finite-cell arrival map}

The finite-cell arrival map treats each downstream cell as a separate emitting
zone.  For the \(\chi\)-resolved path, the observer arrival time is
\begin{equation}
t_\chi(R)=t_0(R)+\frac{1+z}{c}
\left[R_f(R)-\mu R_\chi(R)\right],
\end{equation}
where \(R_f\) is the shock-front radius and \(R_\chi\) is the radius of the
emitting downstream cell.  The corresponding Doppler factor and comoving
frequency are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\delta_\chi&=\left[\Gamma_\chi(1-\beta_\chi\mu)\right]^{-1},\\
\nu'&=\frac{1+z}{\delta_\chi}\nu_{\rm obs}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\Gamma_\chi\) and \(\beta_\chi\) are the local Lorentz factor and velocity
of that downstream cell.

\subsubsection{Radial segment search}

The radial segment search finds which stored radii bracket an observer time.
The code first searches the monotonic sequence in \(R\).  If a \(\chi\) cell maps
nonmonotonically to observer time, each adjacent radial segment is searched
independently.  This changes only the search order.  It does not change the
arrival-time equation.

\subsubsection{Cell transfer factor}

The cell transfer factor accounts for absorption in front of and inside the
emitting cell.  Let \(\tau_{\rm front}=\sum_{b<\chi}\tau_b\) be the optical depth
of cells closer to the shock front, and let \(\tau_\chi\) be the optical depth
of the emitting cell.  Define
\begin{equation}
\psi(x)\equiv\frac{1-e^{-x}}{x},\qquad \psi(0)=1 .
\end{equation}
The finite-cell escape factor is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{E}_\chi(\nu)=
\exp[-\tau_{\rm front}(\nu)]\,\psi[\tau_\chi(\nu)] ,
\end{equation}
and the projected \(\chi\)-cell contribution is
\begin{equation}
\Delta F_{\nu,\chi}=
\frac{\Delta\Omega}{4\pi}\,
\delta_\chi^3\,
W_\chi\,\widetilde L_{\nu',\chi}\,
\mathcal{E}_\chi(\nu').
\end{equation}
Here \(W_\chi\) is the finite-cell weight carried by the \(\chi\)-resolved state.
The direct-electron diagnostic first constructs \(\widetilde L_{\nu',\chi}\) and
\(\tau_{\nu',\chi}\) from the \(\chi\)-local electron spectrum.  It then calls
the same \(\chi\)-projection kernel.

\subsection{Structured rings and ray-list assembly}

\subsubsection{Ring summation}

The ring summation reuses the \(\chi\)-resolved projection on precomputed
\(\theta\) rings.  The batch-luminosity variant first applies the observer
prefactor
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{P}_d=\frac{1+z}{4\pi d_L^2}.
\end{equation}
It then sums the projected ring luminosities.

\subsubsection{Ray-event construction}

The ray-event construction is used when foreground SSA must be traced along the
line of sight.  At each observer time, the ray-traced SSA variant builds a list
of emitting events \(e=(a,\chi,i,b)\), where \(a\) is the ring, \(b\) is the
azimuthal leaf, and \(i\) is the radial bracket.  Each event stores its sky
position, angular weight, cell faces, and Doppler factor,
\begin{equation}
\delta_e^{-1}=\Gamma_e(1-\beta_e\mu_e).
\end{equation}
The observer direction is then aberrated into the comoving frame:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
n'_r&=\frac{\mu_e-\beta_e}{1-\beta_e\mu_e},\\
n'_\theta&=\frac{n_\theta}{\Gamma_e(1-\beta_e\mu_e)},\\
n'_\phi&=\frac{n_\phi}{\Gamma_e(1-\beta_e\mu_e)}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Foreground SSA path}

The foreground SSA path converts stored radial optical depths into line-of-sight
optical depths.  The stored radial optical depth is
\(\tau_r=\alpha'_{\nu'}\Delta r'\).  For event \(e\), the local ray factor
\begin{equation}
\ell_e=\frac{\Delta s'_e}{\Delta r'_e}
\end{equation}
is the chord through the comoving slab divided by the cell comoving radial
width.  A foreground event \(h\) is accepted only when its projected sky patch
contains the emitting point and \(h\) lies in front of \(e\) along the line of
sight.  The foreground optical depth is then
\begin{equation}
\tau_{\rm front}(\nu')=\sum_h \tau_{r,h}(\nu')\,\ell_h.
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Ray flux accumulation}

The ray flux accumulation multiplies the local emission by foreground absorption
and by the emitting-cell escape factor.  The event contribution is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta F_{\nu_{\rm obs}}=
\mathcal{P}_d\,\frac{\Delta\Omega_e}{4\pi}\,\delta_e^3\,
\widetilde L_{\nu'}\,
\exp[-\tau_{\rm front}]\,
\psi(\tau_{r,e}\ell_e).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This ray path is still a projection operator over already computed synchrotron
luminosity and SSA grids; it does not recompute the electron transport or the
synchrotron kernel inside the observer loop.

\subsection{Legacy structured-jet projection}

\subsubsection{Structured angular quadrature}

The structured angular quadrature assigns weights to thin-shell jet patches.  The
companion \texttt{SED\_interpolation\_structured} source is used internally by
\texttt{structured\_jet\_1d}.  It is not exported by the package-level
\texttt{src.Interpolation} lazy binding.  The axisymmetric entry receives one
radius history and one comoving SED per \(\theta\) patch.  For patch \(a\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\theta_{a,-}&=(a-1)\Delta\theta,\\
\theta_{a,+}&=a\Delta\theta,\\
\bar\theta_a&=\frac{\theta_{a,-}+\theta_{a,+}}{2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\Delta\theta&=\frac{\theta_j}{N_\theta},\\
\Delta\Omega_a&=(\cos\theta_{a,-}-\cos\theta_{a,+})\Delta\phi .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(\bar\theta_a\) is the midpoint angle and \(\Delta\Omega_a\) is the patch
solid angle.  Cells whose midpoint lies inside the supplied excluded narrow cone
are skipped.  For the retained half-plane quadrature,
\begin{equation}
\Delta\phi=\pi/N_\phi ,
\end{equation}
the final sum is multiplied by the mirror factor \(S_\phi\).  The collapsed
\(N_\phi=1\) diagnostic uses an internal azimuthal subdivision
\(N_{\phi,\mathrm{col}}\),
\begin{equation}
\Delta\phi=\frac{\pi}{N_{\phi,\mathrm{col}}},\qquad
S_\phi=2N_{\phi,\mathrm{col}},
\end{equation}
so that \(S_\phi\Delta\phi=2\pi\), the full azimuthal measure.  The
non-axisymmetric entry instead receives patch-dependent histories
\((R,t_0,\Gamma,\widetilde L_{\nu'})_{ab}\), uses
\(\Delta\phi=2\pi/N_\phi\), and applies no mirror factor.

\subsubsection{Patch arrival-time accumulation}

Patch arrival-time accumulation maps each structured patch to the observer grid.
Both structured entries use the same local arrival-time and Doppler map as the
top-hat kernel, but with patch-dependent radial histories:
\begin{equation}
\mu_{ab}=
\cos\theta_v\cos\bar\theta_a
+\sin\theta_v\sin\bar\theta_a\cos\phi_b,
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
t_{\mathrm{arr},ab}(R)&=t_{0,ab}(R)
+\frac{(1+z)R_{ab}(R)(1-\mu_{ab})}{c}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(a\) labels the polar patch and \(b\) labels the azimuthal patch.  For an
observer time bracketed by two stored radii,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\lambda_R&=\frac{t_{\rm obs}-t_{\mathrm{arr},ab}(R_i)}
{t_{\mathrm{arr},ab}(R_{i+1})-t_{\mathrm{arr},ab}(R_i)},\\
\Gamma_R&=\exp\!\left[(1-\lambda_R)\ln\Gamma_i
+\lambda_R\ln\Gamma_{i+1}\right],
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where \(\lambda_R\) is the radial interpolation weight.  The comoving luminosity
is interpolated as a non-negative physical flux,
\begin{equation}
\widetilde L_{\nu',R}=(1-\lambda_R)\widetilde L_{\nu',i}
+\lambda_R\widetilde L_{\nu',i+1}.
\end{equation}
Positive bins are passed to the shared log-frequency accumulator as
\(\ln \widetilde L_{\nu',R}\), while empty bins remain empty.  The Doppler
quantities are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\beta_R&=(1-\Gamma_R^{-2})^{1/2},\\
\delta_R&=[\Gamma_R(1-\beta_R\mu_{ab})]^{-1},\\
\nu'_R&=\frac{(1+z)\nu_{\rm obs}}{\delta_R}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Each structured patch contributes
\begin{equation}
\Delta F_{\nu_{\rm obs}}=
S_\phi\frac{\Delta\Omega_a}{4\pi}\,
\delta_R^3\,
\widetilde L_{\nu'_R,R},
\end{equation}
where \(S_\phi=1\) for the explicit two-dimensional patch grid.  Thus the
structured file changes the angular bookkeeping and patch histories, not the
radiative transformation itself.

\subsection{Shared frequency-grid accumulator}

\subsubsection{Log-SED accumulator}

The log-SED accumulator places a shifted source spectrum on the observer
frequency grid.  The shared \texttt{interpolation\_common} module supplies this
accumulator to the projection kernels.  Let \(x_i=\ln\nu_i\) be the
source-frequency coordinate after any Doppler/redshift shift, and let
\(X_j=\ln\nu_{{\rm obs},j}\) be an observer-grid coordinate.  For
\(x_i\le X_j\le x_{i+1}\),
\begin{equation}
q=\frac{X_j-x_i}{x_{i+1}-x_i}.
\end{equation}
If both neighboring source amplitudes are positive, the accumulator uses
log-linear interpolation:
\begin{equation}
F(X_j)\leftarrow F(X_j)+
\exp\!\left[(1-q)\ln S_i+q\ln S_{i+1}\right],
\end{equation}
which is exact for a local power-law SED segment.  If either endpoint is an
empty bin, the same source interval is added in linear amplitude:
\begin{equation}
F(X_j)\leftarrow F(X_j)+(1-q)S_i+qS_{i+1},
\end{equation}
so that physical zeros remain zeros and no artificial logarithmic floor is
introduced.

\subsubsection{Shifted-source accumulator}

The shifted-source accumulator applies a global frequency shift and flux weight.
It uses the equivalent target coordinate
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
X'_j&=X_j+\Delta\ln\nu,\\
W_s&=\exp(\Delta\ln W).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
It then adds the shifted source amplitude as
\begin{equation}
F(X_j)\leftarrow F(X_j)+W_sS(X'_j),
\end{equation}
where \(\Delta\ln\nu\) is the global Doppler/redshift frequency shift and
\(\Delta\ln W\) is the multiplicative projection weight.  Both helpers evaluate
the closed source interval \([x_1,x_N]\), so an observer frequency exactly on a
stored source endpoint is treated as the corresponding physical grid value.

\subsection{Public projection boundaries}

\subsubsection{Supported projection kernels}

The public light-curve path returns total and component-resolved flux matrices
from these projected component arrays.  The geometry kernel options are \texttt{sed\_legacy},
\texttt{sed\_adaptive\_theta}, and \texttt{chi\_eats\_2d}.
The \(\chi\)-resolved projection requires a two-dimensional electron solver and
the stored \(\chi\)-resolved luminosity, optical depth, radius, bulk Lorentz
factor, and volume weights.  Its finite-cell self-absorption transfer averages
through the emitting cell instead of applying a single front-face optical depth,
and the interpolation handles nonmonotonic \(\chi\)-arrival ordering by
searching each adjacent radial segment independently.
This path replaces only the forward-shock synchrotron plus SSA light-curve
projection; SSC, hadronic, and pair-cascade components remain shell-level
components.  An off-axis top-hat projection also requires a non-collapsed
azimuthal EATS grid; the collapsed single-azimuth grid is valid only for the
on-axis case.

\subsection{Ray-traced synchrotron self-absorption boundary}

\subsubsection{Ray-path optical depth}

The \(\chi\)-resolved synchrotron projection treats synchrotron self-absorption
as local comoving transfer followed by a global ray projection.  The EATS map,
Doppler factor, and optically thin event weight are those defined in the
structured-ring projection above.  The extra operation is the nonlocal
foreground optical-depth sum.

The absorption coefficient and path length in this ray step are comoving, so the
optical depth along a ray segment is
\begin{equation}
d\tau_{\nu'}=\alpha'_{\nu'}\,ds' .
\end{equation}
If the local radial optical-depth grid stores
\begin{equation}
\tau_r=\alpha'_{\nu'}\Delta r',
\qquad
\Delta r'=\Gamma\left(r_{\rm out}-r_{\rm in}\right),
\end{equation}
then a ray segment of comoving length \(\Delta s'\) contributes
\begin{equation}
\tau_{\rm ray}=\tau_r\ell,\qquad
\ell\equiv\frac{\Delta s'}{\Delta r'} .
\end{equation}
For a foreground cell \(h\), this length is defined by the intersection of the
source ray with the cell volume.  The lab-frame ray is
\begin{equation}
\boldsymbol x(s)=\boldsymbol x_{\rm src}+s\hat{\boldsymbol n},
\qquad s>0 .
\end{equation}
For local radial direction \(\hat{\boldsymbol e}_{r,h}\),
\begin{equation}
\mu_h=\hat{\boldsymbol n}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol e}_{r,h},
\qquad
\Delta s'_h=
\Gamma_h\left(1-\beta_h\mu_h\right)\Delta s_h .
\end{equation}
Here \(\Delta s_h=s_{\rm out}-s_{\rm in}\) is the positive line-cell
intersection length.  In a local cell basis \((r,\theta,\phi)\), the
intersection is computed by the slab equations
\begin{equation}
x_i(s)=x_{0,i}+s n_i,
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
s_{i,-}&=\min\left[
\frac{x_{i,\rm lo}-x_{0,i}}{n_i},
\frac{x_{i,\rm hi}-x_{0,i}}{n_i}\right],\\
s_{i,+}&=\max\left[
\frac{x_{i,\rm lo}-x_{0,i}}{n_i},
\frac{x_{i,\rm hi}-x_{0,i}}{n_i}\right],
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
s_{\rm in}=\max_i s_{i,-},
\qquad
s_{\rm out}=\min_i s_{i,+}.
\end{equation}
A foreground cell contributes only when \(s_{\rm out}>s_{\rm in}\).  Its
optical-depth contribution is therefore
\begin{equation}
\tau_h=
\tau_{r,h}\,
\frac{(1-\beta_h\mu_h)\Delta s_h}
{r_{h,+}-r_{h,-}} .
\end{equation}
The foreground attenuation of a source cell is
\begin{equation}
\tau_{{\rm f},e}=\sum_h\tau_h .
\end{equation}

The emitting cell is treated as a uniform source and absorber over its own ray
segment.  With \(\ell_e=\Delta s'_e/\Delta r'_e\), its transfer factor is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{T}(\tau_e\ell_e)=
\frac{1-\exp(-\tau_e\ell_e)}{\tau_e\ell_e},
\qquad
\mathcal{T}(0)=1 .
\end{equation}
The final ray-traced synchrotron contribution is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
F_{\nu_{\rm obs}}(t_{\rm obs})&=
\sum_e{\cal W}_e L'_{\nu'_e,e}W_{\chi,e}\\
&\quad \times
\exp(-\tau_{{\rm f},e})
\mathcal{T}(\tau_e\ell_e).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here
\begin{equation}
{\cal W}_e=
\frac{1+z}{4\pi d_L^2}
\frac{d\Omega_e}{4\pi}
\delta_e^3 .
\end{equation}
This expression is nonlocal in angle because a foreground cell can attenuate
emission from a different angular cell.  Consequently, local flux-pair criteria
such as \(F(A\cup B)=F(A)+F(B)\) are not valid convergence tests for
ray-traced SSA.  The supported convergence checks are global resolution tests
in \(\theta\), \(\phi\), \(\chi\), and radius, while adaptive initial grids may
only choose the starting angular resolution before the global ray projection is
evaluated.

\section{Fitting, Runtime, and Reproducibility} \label{sec:fitting}

\subsection{Active-variable map}

The fitting layer is an observer-side wrapper around the same radius-ordered
calculation, not a separate physical branch.  For each active coordinate \(u_j\)
it first applies a declared map to the model parameter \(\vartheta_j\),
\begin{equation}
\vartheta_j=
\begin{cases}
10^{u_j}, & j\in{\cal P}_{\log},\\
u_j, & j\in{\cal P}_{\rm lin},\\
\vartheta_{j,0}, & j\in{\cal P}_{\rm fix},
\end{cases}
\qquad
a_j\le u_j\le b_j ,
\end{equation}
where \(a_j\) and \(b_j\) are the declared sampling bounds.  The trial parameter
set is then passed to the ordinary public model path, which rebuilds the
runtime configuration, solves the radius sequence, and projects it to the
requested observer grid.

\subsection{Observation-block residuals}

The compiled observation plan groups flux-density points, spectra, and band
fluxes into blocks that share one observer grid.  After one model solve for a
block, the residual accumulator reads the requested entries from the total-flux
matrix.  For flux-density and spectral data the Gaussian comparison is
\begin{equation}
\ln\mathcal{L}
=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_i
\left[
\frac{F_i^{\rm obs}-F_i^{\rm mod}}{\sigma_i}
\right]^2 ,
\end{equation}
with the fixed normalization omitted because the implemented use is parameter
comparison at fixed uncertainties.  This likelihood stage therefore changes
only how observer products are scored; it does not alter the hydrodynamics,
electron transport, or projection kernels.

\subsection{Band-flux quadrature}

Band-flux observations are treated as an integration problem on the model
spectrum at one observer time.  The public band product is
\begin{equation}
F_b^{\rm mod}
=\int_{\nu_1}^{\nu_2}F_\nu(t_b,\nu;\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\,d\nu
\simeq
\sum_q w_q^{\rm trap}
F_\nu(t_b,\nu_q;\boldsymbol{\vartheta}) ,
\end{equation}
where the support \(\nu_q\) is the user-declared logarithmic band grid and
\(w_q^{\rm trap}\) are the corresponding trapezoid weights.  The same residual
formula is then applied to \(F_b^{\rm mod}\), so the integration is a product
definition rather than a new radiation calculation.

\subsection{Exposure quadrature}

Exposure queries average a point-source flux density over the reported time
window before the result is compared with data.  For an exposure centered at
\(t_e\),
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\bar F_{\nu,e}
&=\frac{1}{\Delta t_e}
\int_{t_e-\Delta t_e/2}^{t_e+\Delta t_e/2}
F_\nu(t,\nu_e;\boldsymbol{\vartheta})\,dt\\
&\simeq
\sum_q w_q^{\rm G}
F_\nu(t_{e,q},\nu_e;\boldsymbol{\vartheta}) .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here \(t_{e,q}\) are the mapped Gauss--Legendre nodes and \(w_q^{\rm G}\) are
normalized by \(\sum_q w_q^{\rm G}=1\).  Repeated \((t_{e,q},\nu_e)\) pairs are
deduplicated before projection, so the pair cache changes runtime cost only.

\subsection{Runtime query cache and public products}

The public model object also keeps state-level caches keyed by observer grids,
projection kind, observer geometry, and the runtime configuration.  These caches
store already solved flux matrices and diagnostic tracks; they do not evolve,
renormalize, or smooth any physical state.  The same runtime state supports
direct flux-density, spectrum, band-flux, exposure-averaged flux, and component
queries.

\section{Verification and Figure Evidence} \label{sec:verification}

\subsection{Figure-backed state checks}

The figures are organized as checks of algorithm contracts, not as a catalog of
available switches.  Figure~\ref{fig:radius-state} verifies the first contract:
the local state and the observer-time map are exposed before projection.
Figure~\ref{fig:forward-api} then follows the dynamics-to-observer path by
showing how \(n(R)\), \(\Gamma(R)\), and \(B'(R)\) enter multi-band light
curves, spectra, and component outputs.  Figure~\ref{fig:electron-transport}
checks the particle and photon state used by the transport and projection
sections.  Figure~\ref{fig:reverse-shock} isolates the reverse-shock and
density-jump diagnostics, where the relevant state is written before it is added
to the observer components.  Appendix Figure~\ref{fig:hadronic-feedback}
records the threshold and photon-survival semantics used when the bounded
hadronic and pair branches are enabled.

\subsection{Bounded validation metrics}

Figure~\ref{fig:validation} is the reproducibility dashboard.  Panel (a) tests
whether event-split dynamics in tabulated and density-jump media reproduces the
reference \(\Gamma(R)\) and \(M_{\rm sw}(R)\) tracks.  Panel (b) measures the
direct angular-projection error against a declared reference grid.  Panel (c)
compares the reverse-shock adaptive observer grid with the direct grid at the
same frequencies.  Panel (d) records the before-after flux change introduced by
the transport remap in one- and two-dimensional characteristic diagnostics.
These are bounded checks of documented calculation paths; they are not evidence
that every public switch combination is supported.

\begin{figure*}[!tp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig5_validation_benchmark.pdf}
  \caption{Validation and reproducibility dashboard.  (a) Event-split dynamics errors for tabulated and multi-jump media.  (b) Direct angular projection compared with a declared \(N_\theta^{\rm ref}\times N_\phi^{\rm ref}\) grid.  (c) Reverse-shock adaptive-grid differences relative to the direct grid.  (d) Transport-remap changes for one- and two-dimensional characteristic diagnostics.  The panels test documented paths rather than all switch combinations.}
  \label{fig:validation}
\end{figure*}

The validation evidence therefore supports a narrow method claim: the documented
calculation paths expose their local state, component accounting, reverse-shock
diagnostics, and projection benchmarks.  It does not turn unsupported switch
combinations into model predictions.

\section{Model Boundaries} \label{sec:limits}

\subsection{Runtime configuration boundaries}

The current release rejects or excludes runtime configurations that do not map
onto an implemented radius-ordered state sequence.  Let \(N_{\rho,\max}\) denote
the tabulated-medium capacity and \(N_{J,\max}\) the density-jump register
length.
\begin{itemize}
\item Medium dispatch is limited to uniform media, \(k=2\) stellar winds, and tabulated media; user-defined medium kernels do not enter the Fortran backend.
\item Tabulated media require \(2\le N_\rho\le N_{\rho,\max}\) positive, strictly increasing radius-density points.
\item Density jumps require \(N_J\le N_{J,\max}\) Gaussian jumps.
\item Jet spreading is not implemented in the current backend.
\item Adaptive observer-time grids are limited to direct top-hat kernel-backed models.
\item The structured Fortran backend requires uniform patch sampling and either \texttt{fullhide\_1d} or \texttt{dg\_1d}.
\item Non-uniform dominant-region samplers remain limited to the patch backend; sky-image and synchrotron-Stokes products are direct top-hat observer products, with toroidal-field polarization restricted to axisymmetric jets.
\item Thermal electrons are limited to the one-dimensional implicit electron solvers.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Coupled-physics boundaries}

The following limits define which coupled physics branches have a complete local
state and observer-projection contract.
\begin{itemize}
\item Formal hadronic transport requires a one-dimensional forward electron solver.
\item The legacy hadronic transport branch does not support \(p\gamma\) or neutrino channels.
\item Joint electron-photon coupling requires the one-dimensional implicit electron solver, the AM3-based one-dimensional hadronic solver, Hummer--2010 photohadronic response, enabled Bethe-Heitler physics, numeric IC/KN cooling, no reverse shock, no \(\chi\)-resolved transport, and fixed electron substeps.
\item Multi-density reverse-shock v1 requires \(p_r>2\) and excludes hadronic processes, forward SSC, reverse SSC, cross-zone IC, and joint electron-photon coupling.
\item The current formal hadronic path is not chi-resolved.
\item Pair-cascade iterations cover shell-sequence \(\gamma\gamma\) pair/synch feedback, not IC-mediated electromagnetic cascades.
\item \texttt{chi\_eats\_2d} replaces only forward-shock synchrotron plus SSA projection; it does not make SSC, hadronic emission, or pair cascades \(\chi\)-local.
\end{itemize}
These limits are part of the published method.  A future extension must define
the missing local state, projection operator, and validation evidence before the
manuscript claims support for a new coupled case.

\section{Summary} \label{sec:summary}

\asgard\ is a radius-ordered GRB afterglow toolkit in which observer products
are derived from an inspectable local state rather than from a final light curve
alone.  The state \(\mathcal{S}(R)\) carries the dynamics, electron
distribution, photon fields, optional hadronic species, reverse-shock variables,
absorption factors, and observer components through a fixed calculation order.
This ordering keeps the physical source terms local until the equal-arrival-time
projection is applied.

The evidence in this paper supports a bounded method claim.  The figures
validate documented calculation paths, while Section~\ref{sec:limits} defines
where the claim stops.  Unsupported couplings are rejected or explicitly excluded
rather than hidden in defaults.

\appendix
\setcounter{figure}{0}
\renewcommand{\thefigure}{A\arabic{figure}}
\renewcommand{\theHfigure}{A\arabic{figure}}

\section{Hadronic and Pair-Feedback Figure} \label{app:hadronic-figure}

The main text treats hadronic transport as a bounded one-dimensional branch.
Figure~\ref{fig:hadronic-feedback} records the photon-field, threshold, and
shell-transfer semantics used when that branch is active.  It is a bookkeeping
figure for the optional branch, not a claim that hadronic feedback controls the
main observer products.

\begin{figure*}[!tp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/figA1_hadronic_feedback.pdf}
  \caption{Hadronic and pair-feedback branch semantics.  (a) Representative target photon field after conversion from radiation energy density to photon number density.  (b) \(p\gamma\), Bethe-Heitler, and \(\gamma\gamma\) threshold scales written from particle rest-mass energies.  (c) Pure attenuation \(e^{-\tau}\) and finite-shell transfer \((1-e^{-\tau})/\tau\).  These curves define optional-branch bookkeeping and do not imply that every hadronic channel is active in every calculation.}
  \label{fig:hadronic-feedback}
\end{figure*}

\section{Software and Data Availability} \label{app:data}

The \asgard\ source code and the manuscript code baseline are identified by the
software record cited in this paper \citep{ASGARDSoftware}.  The figure evidence
used here is restricted to CSV source tables in \texttt{paper/source\_data/},
the formal figure-generation entry point
\texttt{tests/generate\_paper\_figures.py}, and the PDF, SVG, and TIFF products
under \texttt{paper/figures/}.  Exploratory output outside this tracked
manuscript path is not evidence for the claims made above.  The implementation
uses NumPy, SciPy, Matplotlib, and Astropy
\citep{Harris2020,Virtanen2020,Hunter2007,Astropy2022}.
